## Getting Started {#getting-started}

### Course Introduction {#course-introduction}

```
Hi everyone.
Alice Zhao here and welcome to.
Natural language processing in Python.
The fifth and final course of our Data Science in Python series.
If you're an aspiring data scientist that's new to NLP or a seasoned data scientist wanting to learn
the latest NLP techniques, you're in the right place.
This is a practical, hands on course designed to give you a comprehensive overview of the NLP space
and teach you the essential skills for working with text data in Python.
We'll start by reviewing the history and evolution of NLP over the past 70 years, up to the most popular
LLM architecture at the moment, Transformers.
After that, this course is split into two parts.
The first half covers traditional machine learning techniques, and the second half covers modern deep
learning and LLM approaches.
In the first half, we'll start by walking through the initial text pre-processing steps required for
modeling.
You'll learn how to clean and normalize data with Pandas and Spacy, and then vectorize that data into
a document term matrix using both word counts and TF-IDF scores.
Next, we'll dive into traditional NLP applications, starting with sentiment analysis to determine
the positivity or negativity of text using the Vader library.
Then we'll cover text classification on label data with Naive Bayes, as well as topic modeling on unlabeled
data using non-negative matrix factorization.
All using the scikit learn library.
Once you have a solid understanding of the foundational NLP concepts, we'll move on to the second half
of the course on modern NLP techniques, which covers the major advancements in NLP over the past decade.
We'll start with the basic building blocks of modern NLP, which are neural networks.
You'll learn how neural networks are trained and become familiar with key terms like layers and nodes,
and weights and activation functions.
We'll also introduce you to popular deep learning architectures like CNNs, RNNs, and LSTMs and their
practical applications.
After that, we'll talk about Transformers, which are the architecture behind popular llms like ChatGPT,
Gemini, Claude, and more.
We'll talk about the three main layers, which include embeddings, attention and feedforward neural
networks, and what they do and how they work.
We'll also review the differences between encoders and decoders, along with popular llms in each category.
Last but not least, after going through all that deep learning theory, we're going to apply what we've
learned within Python.
We'll be using Hugging Face Transformers library and their model hub to demo six practical NLP applications,
including sentiment analysis.
Named entity recognition, zero shot classification, text summarization, text generation, and document
similarity.
Throughout the course, you'll play the role of a data science consultant working on multiple projects
using the skills you've picked up throughout the course.
You'll use Python to rank movies by sentiment, identify movie themes based on their summaries, recommend
similar books based on their descriptions, and much more.
If you're an aspiring or seasoned data scientist looking for a practical overview of both traditional
and modern NLP techniques in Python, this is the course for you.
So what do you say?
Ready to dive in?
Sign up today and get immediate access to high quality video content, downloadable resources, course
projects and assignments, and one on one expert support, all backed by a 30 day, 100% guarantee.
We've got a lot to cover, so let's dive in.
```

### About This Series {#about-this-series}

```
Welcome to the course.
This is part five of our five part Data Science in Python series, which starts with data prep and exploratory
data analysis, and then moves on to more advanced machine learning techniques, including regression,
classification, and unsupervised learning.
And now we're at the final piece, which is our natural language processing.
So we're moving from working with numeric data to now working with text data.
If you've been following along throughout this whole series, congrats!
You've finally made it to the end here.
And personally, I think this is the most exciting part of our five part series.
Many of the recent advancements in data science, machine learning, and AI have been within the natural
language processing space.
And we'll be covering some of the most advanced NLP techniques that are currently being used today.
```


## Installation & Setup {#installation-setup}

### Course Structure & Outline {#course-structure-outline}

```
To give you an idea of how this course is structured.
If you've taken a maven analytics course before, you'll know that our courses are practical and hands
on, and in this course, we'll be using that same approach to introduce you to NLP concepts, as well
as actually applying the techniques within Python.
In addition to the video lessons, you're also going to get a downloadable PDF that you can reference
at any time after this course.
We'll also have quizzes and assignments throughout to help you reinforce the concepts that you're learning
throughout the videos, along with step by step solutions, where I'll be coding up the assignment solutions
from scratch within Python.
And finally, there will be interactive demos throughout the course to help keep you engaged.
So instead of just the slides here, you'll also see me working with the command line, debugging Python
code, and just generally talking through my thought process as we walk through the lessons throughout
this course.
Now this course is broken up into eight different sections.
First we're going to start with installation setup.
So we're going to install Anaconda and start writing Python code within Jupyter notebooks.
If you've been following along with the rest of this data Science and Python series, you'll already
have done this.
The extra setup step for this course though, is creating and activating conda environments.
We're going to be using a lot of NLP libraries throughout this course, so we're adding this piece on
environments to help keep us organized.
Now after the first step, once we're all done with the technical setup, we're going to move on to
NLP 101.
Now in this section, we're going to give you a crash course on the history of NLP and talk about just
how much it's evolved over the past 50 to 70 years.
We'll also talk about the differences between traditional versus modern NLP techniques, with traditional
techniques focusing more on machine learning.
We'll talk about common applications and Python libraries for that.
And then we'll move on to modern large language model approaches.
And then again some of the most common applications in Python libraries for that.
Once we're all on the same page and you have those NLP foundations, we're going to move on to text
pre-processing.
In this section we'll be starting with raw text data.
And we'll be moving it through NLP pipeline where we're going to clean it, normalize it, and vectorize
it so that the data is ready for further analysis down the line.
Once we pre-process or clean our data, then we're ready to do analysis on our data.
And this next section is all about using machine learning to do so.
We'll be talking about how you can use traditional machine learning techniques on text data.
And we'll also introduce a few new machine learning algorithms that work especially well with text data,
including Naive Bayes for classification and non-negative matrix factorization, or NMF for topic modeling.
And after that, once you have a solid understanding of NLP fundamentals and traditional techniques,
we're going to be moving on to modern NLP techniques now with modern NLP.
The first thing you have to understand are neural networks and deep learning.
So we're going to spend this section talking about the theory behind these two concepts and make sure
you really understand how they work and the key terms and concepts before we move on to more complex
deep learning architectures, which is what the next section is all about.
For this section on Transformers and Llms, we're going to be diving into the main parts of a transformer
for you to understand how each piece works and truly appreciate just how amazing these transformers
are.
We'll talk about what embeddings are, how attention works, as well as what feedforward neural networks
do, and then we'll talk about popular LMS at the moment, including Bert, GPT and so on.
We'll talk about the different categories of LMS, as well as the different variations out there, so
that when you come across new LMS, you'll have a general idea of how they work and what they do.
Now, once we get through all that theory, we're going to actually apply this within Python, and we're
going to be using the hugging Face Transformers library for this.
We'll be using pre-trained LMS for a variety of applications, including sentiment analysis.
Named entity recognition, and much more.
Now for this section.
We're not only going to demo these on short text strings, but we'll be applying these to multiple text
documents.
And we'll also walk through practical applications for each of these scenarios that you see here, as
well as tips and tricks for how to speed up your code when you're using LMS in Python.
And finally, we're going to wrap it all up with an NLP review and talk about next steps.
So we'll talk about all the techniques that we've covered in this course, as well as a flowchart for
when you should use which technique.
And then we'll also give you resources on next steps you can take to dive deeper and stay up to date
with the latest NLP happenings and announcements.
```

## Transformers & LLMs {#transformers-llms}

### Anaconda Overview {#anaconda-overview}

```
Let's start by talking about Anaconda.
Anaconda is the most popular package and environment manager for data scientists.
So what does that mean exactly?
The main thing to note here is that Anaconda is used by data scientists.
So if you're getting started in data science, this is the first thing you should install.
And here are some of the things that it comes with.
First coding languages and tools.
The two coding languages that you see here are Python and R, which are on the left, and the corresponding
tools are listed to the right.
Jupyter notebook and RStudio are tools you would use to code in those languages.
For this course, we're specifically going to be using those top two.
We'll be using Jupyter Notebook to write Python code.
In addition, Anaconda comes with a lot of popular packages.
And what these packages allow us to do is extend Python's capabilities beyond just basic Python code.
So with NumPy, we're able to do additional math calculations.
With pandas, we're able to create data frames and so on.
And finally when you install Anaconda it'll come with a package and environment manager.
What that means is that as you're installing these packages, Anaconda manages all these package installations
to make sure that nothing breaks when you install two packages.
They may have different dependencies and introduce some conflicts, but Anaconda takes care of all that,
so you don't have to focus on all the installations and you can spend your time focusing on your analysis.
In addition to the package manager, there's an environment manager, and what the environment manager
allows us to do is have different versions of Python and these packages at different places on our computer,
so we can work with a different set of packages or versions of packages for every project that we work
on.
Again, this is a more advanced concept, and we'll be talking about it in a lot more detail within
the conda environments.
Lessons within the section.
Now for this course I'll be doing all the demos using Anaconda, but you're absolutely welcome to take
this course without Anaconda as well.
We highly recommend that you use it as your package and environment manager.
If you want to follow along with the demos and see exactly on your screen what you see presented here.
But if you're already used to installing packages, using Pip installs, or creating new environments
using the VM module within Python, you can absolutely use those as well instead of using Anaconda.
As long as you can write Python code, you'll be able to complete this course.
```


## Transformers with Hugging Face {#transformers-with-hugging-face}

### Installing Anaconda {#installing-anaconda}

```
In this video, I'll talk about how you can install Anaconda on a mac, as well as the PC to install
Anaconda on a mac.
First, you want to go to the Anaconda download website, and while it prompts you to provide an email
address, you don't have to do so.
And you can just click Skip registration.
And that'll take you to another page where you'll see the button download for Mac.
Now to the right of that text, you'll see an arrow, and if you click on it, you'll see an option
for Apple Silicon or Intel.
If you have a newer Mac with an M3 or M4 chip, you're going to want to click Apple Silicon.
And if you have an older Mac, you're going to want to choose Intel.
To figure out what type of Mac you have on your Mac, you can go to the top left and click on the Apple
icon about this Mac.
And then you'll see here I have a newer Mac because it says I have an Apple M3 chip.
If you see Apple M something, that means you have a newer Mac.
If you have an older Mac, this will say Intel something.
Now let's go back to our slide to step two to figure out which installer to download.
If in your about this Mac you see Apple m something chip, you're going to choose Apple Silicon because
you have a newer computer.
Otherwise you'll see Intel in there, which means you have an older computer.
And you should select Intel as your option here.
In either case, once you do your download, you'll see this dot jpg file within your downloads folder,
and then you can double click on that to launch the installer.
At this point, you're just going to follow the installation steps and say okay to all the default options.
So you'll see this installer.
You're going to click continue next next next.
Keep on going all the way until you get to the end.
And then you can click install.
And with that you'll have installed Anaconda on your Mac to install Anaconda on your PC.
The steps are very similar.
First you're going to go to Anaconda.com download.
You'll see a place to provide your email, but you don't have to.
So you can just click Skip registration.
And that should take you to this new page with the download button.
You can go ahead and click that download button, and once it downloads, you should see an executable
file within your downloads folder.
And from there you can double click on it to launch the installer.
And you can just go ahead and follow the installation steps.
So just click okay to all the default settings.
You'll see a window that pops up that looks something like this.
You can click next next all the way to this step.
And then you're just going to click install.
And once it installs you can click next.
And that'll lead you to launching Anaconda.
Now once you do all these steps, you'll have installed Anaconda on your computer and you're ready to
move on to the next lesson.
```

### Conda Environments {#conda-environments}

```
One of the great things about Anaconda is that we're able to create conda environments.
A conda environment is a place on your computer where you can install Python and other packages for
a specific project, without affecting other projects on your computer.
You'll typically have multiple environments, and for each project that you work on, you'll use a different
environment.
So let's say this is my computer here.
I can create one environment on my computer that has Python 3.13 installed.
And then I can create a separate environment with Python 3.10 installed.
And then also in this environment I'm going to install numpy.
So this is typically a foreign concept.
If you're a beginner Python user, you're used to having just one version of Python installed on your
computer.
But in practice you can have multiple versions of Python installed.
Now why would you want to do this?
Let's say I'm working on a beginner Python 101 project, and I'm learning all my Python fundamentals.
In this case, I'm probably going to download the latest version of Python, which currently is 3.13.
And then I'm going to activate this environment so that when I'm coding I'm using Python 3.13.
.13.
Now let's say that at the same time I'm also taking a numpy course and the instructor is using Python
3.10, and he's also using numpy 2.2.
And I want to make sure that my code matches his perfectly.
And I see the exact same outputs that he sees in this case, when I'm taking his course, I would want
to activate environment two so that I'm using Python 3.10 as well as numpy 2.2.
So on my computer, both these environments can exist at the same time.
And then depending on whether I'm working on my Python 101 project or taking this numpy course, I can
switch between environments.
Now let's also take a look at how coding in the two environments might be slightly different.
So let's say I've activated environment one or I'm using environment one which has Python 3.13 installed.
From here I can just go ahead and write my Python code as usual.
But then when I try to import a library like numpy because I don't have numpy installed in this environment,
I'm going to get an error.
So to summarize what's happening here within environment one, we can use all the base or the built
in Python 3.13 functions.
But because numpy isn't installed, we can't import it in this environment.
However, if we're in an environment two with Python and NumPy installed, then again we can write our
basic Python code.
But in this case, when we import numpy, you'll see that it does so successfully.
And then we can go on to use numpy functions.
So in this case we can use all of the base Python functions as well as the numpy library because they're
both installed in this environment.
Now if you're a Python beginner up until this point you've likely just been using the default environment.
But as you get to be more advanced, you can create new environments for each new project that you work
on.
And again, the reason that you would create a new environment is that with each new environment, you
get a blank slate to freshly install Python and all the packages.
You need to make sure that all the versions and dependencies are correct for your project.
So so far as a Python beginner, you've likely just been using the default environment, and this is
absolutely fine if you're just using a few basic libraries like NumPy and pandas.
However, as you go on to work on more niche projects, which is what we'll be doing throughout this
course, you're going to want to create new environments.
So for example, we're going to create a new environment for our sentiment analysis project.
And you can see here it has some of the libraries as our default environment.
But we've also included the Vader sentiment library which allows us to do sentiment analysis.
And then later on we're going to be working on an LM project.
And in that case, we have a whole new set of libraries that we're going to be using.
The main one is the Transformers library.
And in addition to that you'll see PyTorch and all these other ones here.
So what's going to happen is on our computer, we're going to have all these environments available.
And then based on the specific project that we're working on, we're going to activate or use just one
of these environments for each project.
If you've been working with Python for a while and haven't used environments yet, you've probably run
into install issues where certain library versions aren't compatible with one another.
And now that you understand how environments work by creating different environments for different projects,
this should fix a lot of those issues.
```

### Conda Workflow {#conda-workflow}

```
When you're working with conda environments and packages, this is the general workflow you should be
following.
In our example here we're going to create a new environment to use hugging face transformers library.
Now the first step here is to create a new environment.
Let's say we haven't created a new environment yet and all we have is our base or our default environment.
Currently we have Python installed as well as NumPy and pandas.
This base environment is our active environment by default, and we've represented that with this teal
box that you see here.
And if we want to create a new environment we can do conda create.
And that will give us this new LMDh project environment.
You can see that this new environment that we've created is empty.
There are no packages installed here just yet.
But at this point with our first step, our goal was to create a new environment and we've done that.
So now the next step is to activate the new environment.
And we can do that using Conda activate.
And now you can see that the new environment we created is highlighted in teal.
Another way of thinking of the term activate is saying this is the environment that I want to use.
Once you've activated your environment, the next step is to install the packages that you need.
So again this is our new environment.
And by saying conda install transformers because we want to use the Transformers library, the Transformers
library will be installed along with all of its dependencies.
So even if we're not explicitly saying install numpy, install pandas because of conda's package manager.
It automatically installs those when you install transformers.
So with just one line of code, we've been able to install everything that you see here.
And just to be clear, when one package depends on another package, that means that that first package
uses code from the other package.
Now at this point, we have all the packages we need to do our analysis so we can move on to the next
step which is launching Jupyter within this environment.
So from here we're going to type Jupyter Notebook.
And that will launch a Jupyter notebook for us.
And because we launched it from this environment, that Jupyter notebook will have access to all the
packages available here.
And now we can just start writing Python code as usual.
So within our Jupyter notebook, we can use our Transformers library and start doing sentiment analysis
within Python.
And I'm able to use the Transformers library because it's in this active environment.
Now once I'm done with all my analysis, the last step is to deactivate the environment using conda
deactivate.
And that's going to switch us from our LM project environment back to our base or our default environment.
Now one thing I want to note here is that even though we've deactivated our new environment, all those
packages still live there.
And whenever we want to use them, we can just switch back to that environment by activating it and
launching a new Jupyter notebook.
Now that you know the workflow, let's practice writing and executing conda commands.
```

### Conda Commands {#conda-commands}

```
This is a table of conda commands for your reference.
It includes some of the commands we just covered within the last lesson on the conda workflow.
You can see that there are three categories of commands.
The first are environment commands.
Those will allow us to view, create and activate new environments.
There are also package commands that will help us install new packages within our environments.
And finally we have YAML commands.
These will allow us to create new environments really quickly from YAML files or YAML files.
We'll be going through the environment and package commands all together as a group.
And then the YAML commands are for more experienced Python users if you want an alternative way to create
new environments.
Now to get started, all these conda commands should be written and executed within the terminal application.
If you're on a mac or within the Anaconda Prompt application if you're on a PC.
This is the same place where we launched our Jupyter Notebook a few lessons ago.
And the application should look something like this.
Here I have this white background.
You might see a black background on your computer, but the main things to note here are.
First, this base term on the left here.
This tells us that we're in our base environment or what we've been calling our default environment.
Whenever you open up your terminal or your Anaconda Prompt application.
You should by default be in this base environment.
Now the command that we've typed in here is conda env list.
And what that does is list out all the available conda environments on our computer.
If you haven't used environments before, you should only see the base environment in your output.
But by the end of this course you'll have created these three new environments here.
Now, one last thing I want to point out is the star here.
This tells us which environment we're currently using.
So by default we're using the base environment, which is why you see the star here.
And we can change which environment we're using by using conda commands.
One last thing I want to note before we move into our demo is that you're only going to see this base
prefix and be able to use these commands if you have Anaconda installed.
If you don't, when you open up your terminal, you won't be able to see this base prefix.
And you also won't be able to run conda commands.
But like I mentioned before, even if you're not using Anaconda, you can still do your installs using
pip installs and create new environments using Vmv.
But Anaconda and Conda commands are the recommended approach for this course.
Now let's walk step by step through how to create a new conda environment within a demo.
```

### Intro to NLP {#intro-to-nlp}

```
Starting with the basics.
This is the definition of natural language processing.
Now there is a lot of text here.
It has something to do with machine learning, with understanding human language and so on.
But in short, it's really just using computers to work with text data.
So what does that look like?
Here we have a table and you can see this review column has text data in it.
And this review text is really easy for humans to interpret and understand.
But this is where computers struggle.
Computers are really good at working with numeric data.
But with text data it gets more complex.
And that's where NLP comes in.
To give you an idea of where NLP fits in within the broader landscape of AI and machine learning, you
can think of NLP as falling under the general umbrella of artificial intelligence or AI.
An AI is the field that tries to replicate what humans can do but using computers.
So starting at the very top here we have artificial intelligence or AI.
And then there are different fields of AI.
Three of them are the ones that you see here.
So starting with computer vision, remember that AI is trying to imitate what humans can do.
So for computer vision, it's trying to see like a human and some computer vision.
Tasks include things like image recognition or object detection.
With machine learning, computers are trying to learn like a human, and machine learning algorithms
fall under supervised learning and unsupervised learning.
We're going to be talking about both of those more in the NLP for Machine Learning section.
And finally here we have natural language processing which is interpreting language like a human.
There are many NLP applications out there, including things like sentiment analysis and text summarization.
Now these are just three of the fields that fall under AI.
There are many others out there as well.
And again, it's all about having computers do things that humans can do.
Now, at this point, you might be wondering where does data science fit in here?
And the answer is that the general job of a data scientist is to use data to extract insights.
And they do that using a variety of techniques, including all the things that you see here.
So they'll use computer vision models, machine learning models, and NLP models to extract insights
and make data driven decisions.
```

### History of NLP {#history-of-nlp}

```
The history of NLP is really interesting.
It started in the 1950s and it's evolved significantly over the past 70 years.
So let's start with our timeline here.
In the 1950s, there was this georgetown-ibm experiment that did machine translation for the first time.
It translated Russian into English with a small set of terms and a few rules.
A decade later, Eliza, the first chatbot, was created at MIT.
This was a situation where you could ask a computer questions, and then it would respond to you and
have a conversation with you.
Again, this was based on a set of rules and pattern matching techniques.
Now, this phase of NLP, I like to call the early phases of NLP.
At this point, researchers had their hands on computers and they were really excited to start experimenting
with language.
But very quickly they realized that the results they were getting didn't really meet expectations,
and that interpreting language would be a really hard task.
And that takes us to the next phase of NLP, starting in the 1980s, where things move on beyond rules
based techniques with the introduction of probabilistic models, instead of just rules based models.
You'll see here that statistical machine translation is done instead of just rules based translation.
In addition, in the 90s, the Penn Treebank is created, and this is an amazing body of text where
linguists and grad students have manually labeled millions of words for NLP researchers to do analysis
on.
And this is really useful in the 90s because computers are getting stronger and they're actually able
to analyze large amounts of text data along with all the annotations.
Now, this time period, here is what I would call traditional NLP.
This is where statistical and machine learning models are getting introduced into the NLP space.
There's also this huge increase in computing power.
And with the combination of those two things, the results are looking a lot better than the simple
rules based techniques that were introduced in the early NLP phase.
So with that, there's this increasing amount of excitement around NLP.
So now from here we're going to fast forward to the 20 tens.
And this is where deep learning gets paired with NLP.
And the results are really impressive.
Fast forward a few years and Transformers are introduced and they perform even better than the deep
learning models from just a few years ago.
We'll be talking a lot more about deep learning and Transformers later on in this course.
But this exciting time now is what I would call modern NLP.
In the last decade, there's been an explosion of research because of how well transformers are performing.
This is still an active area of research, with new papers and models coming out all the time.
Now let's summarize some of the techniques that were being used during each of these phases in the early
NLP phase.
Rules based techniques were primarily being used, so things such as looking at grammar rules or pattern
matching, such as checking if a certain word was present.
Then we move on to traditional NLP techniques, including statistical approaches that use stats and
probability, as well as machine learning approaches using supervised and unsupervised learning.
Then we move on to modern NLP techniques, starting with recurrent based techniques.
Popular deep learning architectures include things like RNNs, LSTMs, and more.
In addition, we have Transformers based techniques.
Those include large language models like GPT, Bert, and much more.
Now all the NLP techniques that you see on the slide have been used at some point, but they're not
all currently being used today.
For example, traditional NLP has largely replaced early NLP.
You'll rarely see those simplistic early NLP approaches in practice today.
In addition, Transformers based techniques have largely replaced recurrent based techniques.
Recurrent based techniques such as RNNs and LSTMs were all the rage for a few years.
But then once Transformers came into the picture, they performed so much better that the focus became
on these models here instead of recurrent based ones.
So what you see highlighted here are the techniques that will be going through throughout this course.
The first half of this course focuses on those traditional NLP techniques, and the second half of this
course will focus on modern NLP techniques and specifically Transformers based techniques, because
that's what's being used in practice today.
Now, one more thing I wanted to mention, in addition to this shift in techniques, is a mindset shift
in NLP over the years as well.
Moving from traditional NLP to modern NLP with traditional NLP, there's a huge focus on understanding
everything that's happening.
If you've worked with machine learning models at all, you know that there's a big emphasis on making
sure that you understand how each model works, as well as how to interpret the results of each model.
With modern NLP, that's not really the case anymore.
These deep learning architectures are so complex that there's no way to fully understand everything
that's happening inside this black box of a large language model.
And what matters more in this modern NLP time is performance.
There's less of a focus on understanding exactly how a model works, and more on just seeing if the
results make sense and seem human like.
I really think that this mindset shift is one of the biggest ways that NLP has evolved over the years,
and you'll see that within this course as well.
Within the traditional NLP section, we're going to make sure that you understand how all of these models
truly work before applying them on data sets.
With the modern NLP section, we're going to give you a high level overview of how Transformers generally
work.
And then we're going to move right on to application, where we're going to take pre-trained models
and apply them to our data sets.
And we're going to check the outputs to confirm if they make sense.
And that's your brief history of early traditional and modern NLP just within the last 70 or so years.
```

### NLP Applications & Techniques {#nlp-applications-techniques}

```
There are many NLP applications and techniques out there, and in this video we'll walk through the
ones that we'll be covering throughout this course.
Here's the full list of techniques that we're going to be going over.
These first few fall under the traditional NLP category.
Rules based techniques are actually a bit more simplistic, but we'll just lump them in with the traditional
techniques.
And then the ones listed at the bottom here are all modern techniques.
You can see that these modern techniques here all use llms or large language models.
Now using each of these techniques, we're going to apply them to different problems starting with rules
based techniques.
We're going to use that for sentiment analysis.
And then with Naive Bayes we're going to use that algorithm for text classification.
And then the following NMF model for topic modeling.
These three are very common traditional or machine learning applications for text data.
Now moving on to modern techniques we're again going to start with sentiment analysis.
Sentiment analysis is something that can be done using traditional or modern techniques.
And what we're going to do is compare the results side by side so you can see the differences yourself.
We're also going to be using large language models for named entity recognition, zero shot classification,
text summarization and text generation.
We'll talk about all of these in a lot more detail in the Hugging Face Transformers section of this
course.
But just know for the time being, that these are common things you can do with large language models.
And finally, we're going to go through an example of document similarity.
This is a really cool example that combines embeddings, which is a piece of an LLM, along with a statistical
technique to calculate similarity scores between documents and make text recommendations.
By the end of this course, you'll understand how all these techniques work, and you'll also have seen
all these applications within demos and practice them on your own within the assignments.
```

### NLP Libraries in Python {#nlp-libraries-in-python}

```
For this course, we're going to be doing all of our analysis within Python, and there are a number
of NLP libraries available for us to use throughout this data Science and Python series.
So far we've been mainly using pandas and scikit learn, but for this course, we're going to be installing
a few more NLP specific Python libraries.
In this table here you can see the main libraries we'll be using within each course section.
In the next section on text pre-processing we're going to be using pandas, Spacy and scikit learn.
Spacy is the new library here, and it's an NLP library that's commonly used for cleaning, normalization
and linguistic analysis.
After that, there's a section on NLP with machine learning.
And the new library we're introducing here is called Vader, which is commonly used for sentiment analysis.
We're also going to be using scikit learn as usual for machine learning algorithms.
After that we'll talk about neural networks and deep learning.
And again we're going to be using scikit learn.
And then finally there's going to be a section on hugging face Transformers.
We're specifically going to be using the Transformers library.
And that has many applications.
All the modern applications that we mentioned in the last lesson can be done using the Transformers
library.
And that includes things like text summarization generation and so on.
Now, if you've dabbled with NLP and Python before.
You may notice that there are a few libraries that are missing.
These are some popular NLP libraries that we won't be covering within this course.
One of them is NLTK, and the reason that we're not covering that is because Spacy is very similar to
NLTK, but it's even simpler to use and faster.
Also, we won't be covering Gensim.
We're going to be using scikit learn for topic modeling, which can also be done using Gensim.
But because at this point we're more familiar with scikit learn, that's where we're going to be doing
topic modeling.
And also there's TensorFlow and PyTorch.
These are two libraries that are often used for deep learning models.
And we're going to be using the Transformers library instead of those.
The reason is because the Transformers library is much easier to use, and it directly interacts with
TensorFlow or PyTorch to work with deep learning models.
So in summary, all the libraries that we've selected for this course are popular libraries that are
known for their simplicity and ease of use.
And they're really great for seeing how the concepts and techniques we're going to be covering actually
work in practice.
At this point, if you don't have all these libraries installed, that's absolutely fine.
Throughout each section of this course, we're going to have a dedicated demo on how to install each
of these libraries.
```

### NLP Pipeline {#nlp-pipeline}

```
Let's start by reviewing the data science workflow.
When you're working on an NLP project, you're going to be following the same data science workflow
that we've been talking about throughout this whole Data Science in Python series.
The only difference is that there's an extra text pre-processing step involved.
So let's go over the general data science workflow one more time with any data science project.
You're first going to start by scoping the project.
So really defining the goal for your project and what question you want to answer.
Once you do that, you move on to gathering the data you need for your project.
Then you move on to cleaning that data.
And as you're cleaning the data, you might realize you have to go back and gather more data.
Then you're going to move on to exploring the data.
And again, as you explore the data, you might have to go back and do more cleaning or gather more
data.
And then after that you move on to modeling.
This is the fun part of data science.
This is where you get to use all the fancy machine learning algorithms.
And again, this whole thing is an iterative process.
So you might have to go back to some of the previous steps.
And then once you're done with all that and you've locked in your final analysis, the last step is
to share your insights.
So if you're doing one time analysis this might be a presentation.
It could be a dashboard.
You could be deploying your model so that other people can interact with it and so on.
Now this was a really quick summary of the data science workflow.
If you want more details about this, you can find them in the Dataprep and EDA course which is the
first course of this Data Science in Python series.
Okay, so now where does NLP fit into all this?
Like I said before, NLP projects will follow this exact same workflow, except there's one extra step
which is the text pre-processing step.
And that falls between cleaning and exploration.
The idea here is that after you've done some general cleaning of your data, there are some more text
cleaning steps that you have to take to make sure that your data is ready to be explored and modeled.
And in terms of modeling techniques, you can use any general ML algo that we've learned throughout
this data Science in Python series.
And we're also going to go through a few NLP specific ones later on within this course.
Now this process here is typically called the data science workflow within the NLP space.
Sometimes you'll hear these middle steps here called the NLP pipeline.
And the way to think about the NLP pipeline is that it's all the steps of the workflow that actually
deal with the data.
So if you exclude the scoping of project and sharing insight steps, these middle five steps actually
touch the data.
So with the NLP pipeline what you do is you start with raw text data.
You gather that you clean it, you do text pre-processing, explore it and model it.
And that NLP pipeline takes your raw text data and step by step, turns it into data and analysis results
that we can share out with others.
```

### Text Preprocessing Overview {#text-preprocessing-overview}

```
Now to specifically dive into the text pre-processing step, because that is the focus of this section.
Text pre-processing is all about turning that raw text data into numeric data, so that you can use
that process data for analysis and modeling.
Again, here is our data science workflow.
And now we've added in this text pre-processing step.
And if we were to break down this step what comes in at this point is generally clean text data.
Because at this step of the workflow, you've already gone through the general cleaning data steps,
which include things like removing duplicate data, handling outliers, and so on.
And at this step you have this generally clean text data.
And now to kick off the text pre-processing phase, the first thing you're going to do is clean and
normalize that text.
So that includes things like removing unnecessary text and then also making all the text consistent.
And these two things here can be done using the pandas library or the Spacy library within Python.
After your data is cleaned and normalized, you would move on to vectorization.
So what that means is turning your text data into numeric data.
And more specifically, it becomes a matrix of numeric data.
What ends up happening is that each document of your data, or each bit of text that we're working with
gets turned into a vector.
And that vector will include a bunch of numeric values.
Those values could be word counts, or they could also be something called TF-IDF values.
And this is something we'll be walking through how to do within the scikit learn library.
And at the end of all this, you'll have cleaned, normalized, and vectorized text data that's ready
for exploratory data analysis and modeling.
At this point, you can jump right back into the data science workflow and continue using the exploration
and modeling techniques we've covered throughout this whole Data Science in Python series.
In terms of the specific techniques we'll be going through within this course, here is a list of them
all.
You can see the first category is cleaning and normalization.
We'll be going through these six concepts here.
I won't talk through the descriptions at this point, because we're going to be going through this in
a lot more detail in the next few lessons, but please use this as a reference slide.
One thing to know is with these concepts listed here, these steps don't necessarily have to happen
in this order.
They really can be mixed and matched for every NLP project that you work on.
After we go through those cleaning and normalization concepts, we're going to move on to vectorization.
And for vectorization we're going to talk about two different concepts.
First the document term matrix which is a general idea.
And then we're going to get more specific with something called a TF-IDF score.
At this point we've talked about the high level theory for this section.
Now let's get into it with the code.
```

### Text Preprocessing with Pandas {#text-preprocessing-with-pandas}

```
We're going to start by using pandas for text pre-processing.
The pandas library is well known for data analysis, and it can also be used for simple text cleaning
and normalization.
The two string methods we're going to introduce here are lower to make all your text lowercase, and
also dot replace, which will allow us to replace special characters in our text.
So removing punctuation numbers and so on.
Let's start with dot lower in this code here our goal is to lowercase our text.
We're going to create a new column called Sentence Clean and set that equal to our current column which
is sentence which has our raw text data in it.
And then we're going to call a string method by doing str and specifically the lower method.
And here you can see that our original sentence column is now lowercase.
And this new sentence clean column.
Now let's also look at some examples of replace.
In this case here our goal is going to be to remove text between brackets including the brackets themselves.
So if you take a look at our sentence column, you'll see in the last row that all recipes is in square
brackets there.
And let's say we want to remove that.
We can do that by using the replace method.
So in our code here we're updating our sentence clean column by setting it equal to the latest sentence
clean column which had lowercase text.
And now we're calling another string method replace.
Now within the replace method we have a lot of punctuation here.
And all these symbols are actually a regular expression.
If you aren't familiar with regular expressions, they're sometimes called regex and they allow you
to find patterns.
So imagine you have a word document.
Perhaps one thing you've done before is a control F, where you're able to find certain words within
your text.
That's a typical find, but regular expressions take that to another level.
With regular expressions, you're not only able to find specific text, you can find patterns as well.
So, for example, show me all sentences that start with a capital letter and end with a period.
Or in this case find all text that's within square brackets.
Then once you find that text using a regex, it's up to you what you want to do with it, such as removing
it or replacing it with something else.
Regular expressions are really powerful and they're language agnostic, so you can use them in Python,
you can use them in SQL or Excel and so on.
However, the main downside is that the syntax is really hard to remember.
The good news is that these days we have tools like ChatGPT.
So once you understand the basic idea of a regular expression, you can just use something like ChatGPT
to generate the actual syntax for you.
And this is something that I do all the time.
I'll walk through the process for doing this in a lot more detail in a demo right after this lesson,
but for the time being, just know that if we're looking at that replace code, all that red syntax
there, that is a regular expression that finds all texts within square brackets.
And what we're doing in this case is replacing that with a blank value.
So you see within that dot replace we have all those symbols there comma.
And then we have some blank text.
And that's what we're replacing the bracketed text with.
And finally in this case I've set reg X equal to true meaning that interpret what we're searching for
as a regular expression.
Again, I'll be walking through this all in a lot more detail within the demo.
Now, one more thing I wanted to show you before we dive into a Jupyter notebook is the second example
of replace in this code here.
Our goal is to remove punctuation from our text.
So what I'm doing in this code here is again updating the sentence clean column.
And here I'm again calling a string method str.
I'm again using that dot replace method.
And the difference with this code versus the previous code is the text right after the replace.
This is a different set of symbols.
And what this regex is doing is removing all the symbols or the punctuation from our text.
So you can see in our sentence clean column here there are no more special characters.
Everything here is either a letter, a number, or a space.
Okay.
With that high level overview of text pre-processing with pandas, let's now walk through all this in
more detail within a Jupyter notebook.
```

### PRO TIP: Create a Function {#pro-tip-create-a-function}

```
Now, at this point, I want to show you a pro tip, which is to take all these steps that we covered
here and put them within a function so that we can apply these same steps to different bodies of text
in the future.
So what I'm going to do is copy these three steps that we just went through.
Here's one.
Two and three.
And let me just add some cells below this.
Okay I'm going to use all this for reference.
What I'm going to do is create a function.
And I'm going to do that using the def keyword in Python which stands for define.
And what a function does in Python is it allows you to repeat steps again and again.
So at any point you can call a function that you made, and those steps can get applied to a different
data set.
I'm going to call this function my lower and replace function.
So any text that I input into this function will get lowercased.
And then all those special characters will get removed.
Now the first thing I have to do is specify what I want the input to look like.
And just like we did before, in this case, I'm inputting a column of data or a series of data.
So I'm going to say input a series of text, and then you can see it's automatically included this indentation
for me.
And now I can specify what I want to do with that text.
So for the series of text the first thing I want to do is make everything lowercase.
And I'm going to save this as my output.
And then once my series is lowercased that's saved as the output.
Now I want to remove everything in square brackets.
So I'm going to say output.
And then with that lowercase output remove everything in square brackets.
And again I'm going to save that as output.
And then finally with this output here I'm going to apply this regex to remove all the punctuation.
And again I'm going to save that as an updated output.
And at the very end of this I want the function to return this output.
So let's just talk through each step of this function once again.
We're naming this function lower replace, we're going to require the user to input a series of data
or a column of a data frame.
And then what's going to happen is we're going to take that series, make everything lowercase and save
it to an output.
Then with that output, we're going to remove everything in square brackets and save that to an updated
output.
We're going to take that output.
We're going to remove all the punctuation and then save that into an even more updated output.
And finally we're going to return that output.
So if I run this cell it looks like nothing happened.
But what's actually happening behind the scenes is that lower replace has been saved.
And what we can do with this is apply it on any data set.
So if I go all the way back to the very beginning, if you remember, we created this test data set,
which is a series and it's called test series.
I'm going to copy that.
And then also for this whole section we've been using the DF data set.
So let's keep both those in mind.
And down here let's test this function on both of those data sets.

### Text Preprocessing with spaCy {#text-preprocessing-with-spacy}

```
Now that we've done some basic text pre-processing using pandas, let's do some more using a true NLP
library.
In this case, we're going to be using Spacy, which is a library that can handle a variety of NLP tasks,
including things like tokenization, lemmatization, stopwords, and much more.
We're going to be going through all these terms here later in this lesson.
Now when you use Spacy, the first step is to turn your text string into Spacy document object.
And the code for that looks like this.
So here the first thing we're doing is importing Spacy.
And with Spacy we also have to specify which language we want to use.
In this case we want to use English which is why I'm doing a dot load.
And then you can see n there for English.
And that's going to download a bunch of information about the English language.
So things like annotated text, linguistic rules and so on.
Now once we do that we can create Spacy object.
Here we're going to look at the phrase I'm selling lemons for $5 today.
Now I recognize that just a few lessons ago we removed punctuation.
But in this case I'm including this punctuation so you can see how Spacy deals with it.
Now in this last code block here, we're creating a Spacy object.
So when I do NLP of my phrase that output is a spacy object.
And now doc holds my spacy object with that sentence in it.
And now I can go on to use all of the available spacy methods for natural language processing.
Now at this point, instead of diving right into a demo, I'm going to showcase four things that you
can do with Spacy.
And then afterwards we'll have a few demos which dives into the code in more detail.
```

### Tokenization {#tokenization}

```
The first thing I want to cover is tokenization.
The idea of tokenization is that it lets you break up your text into smaller units.
Typically words.
And the way that tokens are typically created is by taking a text string and splitting it on whitespace
to then generate tokens.
So let's tokenize the Spacy document that we just created.
In this code here, first we can see our doc text once again.
And then in the second code block I'm breaking up the text into tokens.
Now this syntax here represents a special concept within Python called a list comprehension.
And the way I like to read a list comprehension like this is actually from right to left.
So this is saying in doc which means look at the document object and then it's saying for token.
So what that means is look at the document object, find all the tokens in the document object.
And then the first part of the list comprehension is stating what we want returned.
So in this case within this document Find all the tokens and then return the text of that token.
So in this output here you can see my original text string has now been tokenized.
It's been broken up into these smaller pieces that are generally words, but sometimes they're smaller
than words.
Now, originally I said that tokenization typically means splitting by whitespace.
But if you look at the tokens here, it actually goes beyond that.
And the reason is because Spacy's tokenizer is smarter than that.
What it does is it mainly splits on whitespace, but then there's some additional logic.
For example, it'll break up common contractions such as I'm in this case.
And then punctuation also gets separated unless it's a URL, email address and so on.
So you can see in this case dollar five got separated.
There are many other cases of this as well.
But you can see two of them in this example alone.
While there's a lot of complex logic there, the simple takeaway for this lesson is that tokenization
breaks a larger body of text into smaller bits of text.
```

### Lemmatization {#lemmatization}

```
In addition to tokenization, there's also lemmatization.
And the idea of lemmatization is that it reduces words to its base form.
This is a common way to normalize text or make your text consistent.
And the way that Spacy does this is using a combination of rules as well as statistical models.
So let's take a look at the code for this.
If you were to compare this code with the last slide, everything here is identical except for what
we're returning.
So again we're starting with doc to view our document.
And then we have this list comprehension here.
But instead of returning token dot text or returning token dot lemma.
And with that you'll notice a few differences.
For example that I token was originally lowercase and now it's been updated to a capital I, because
within the English language, whenever you see an I on its own, it's typically capitalized.
You can also see that spelling has been shortened to just sell, and then lemons has been shortened
to just lemon.
Now why would you want to do something like this?
If you think about it, if you had many text strings and one had lemon in it and the other one had lemons
in it, if you didn't lemmatize, those two text documents would be talking about different things.
But at the end of the day, they're all talking about lemons.
So by Lemmatizing you're able to convey that those two documents are talking about the same thing,
which is lemons.
Now, when people talk about lemmatization, they often talk about stemming as well.
So I wanted to clarify the difference between the two.
First, how are they the same?
What they have in common is that they both reduce words to their base form.
However, out of the two lemmatization is the smarter approach and it typically works better.
So if you were to choose one, I would say go with Lemmatization.
And just to show you a few examples of that.
Here's an example of stemming.
You can see in this case, if you had the words Am is an R and you were to use stemming, they would
change to Am is and are without the e.
And then in the case of happy and happiness, those would both change into happy with an I.
And the reason for this is because with stemming, the idea is typically to cut off the end of a word,
which is why you saw the E cut off with R and then ness cut off with happiness.
Which is why happy with a y turns into happy with an I.
But it's really not as sophisticated as lemmatization.
If you were to lemmatize these words, this is what you'd get as a result.
You can see in the first case all those words are mapped to B, and in the second case, both those
words are mapped to happy with a y.
So you can see just in these few examples here that lemmatization is the better choice.
```

### Stop Words {#stop-words}

```
Next, let's move on to Stopwords.
A stopword is a word that doesn't have any significant meaning.
For example, words like the a are in and so on.
There are a bunch of stopwords in every language.
If you want to view the full stopword list, you can do so within Spacy with this code here.
That's something I'll show in the demo.
But if you want to see Stopwords in action, you can just look at this code here.
Again, we're starting with our document.
And then in the second code block, just like before we have our list comprehension.
The difference here is that there's an if statement at the end.
So the first portion is actually identical to the Lemmatization code.
But what's been added here is the if not token is stop.
And what that's doing is it's saying only return tokens that are not stopwords.
And with that you can see at the bottom here that I and four have been removed.
Those are two stopwords that don't have any significant meaning.
And don't add too much to the overall meaning of the sentence.
You'll notice that some other things stayed here that may not make sense, such as that lowercase m.
One thing to keep in mind with natural language processing is that cleaning is never perfect.
That's something that can be addressed at a later point.
```

### Parts of Speech Tagging {#parts-of-speech-tagging}

```
The final preprocessing topic I want to go through before we dive into our demos is parts of speech
tagging.
This allows you to label words as nouns, verbs, and so on.
This is an optional step, whereas the previous three concepts I covered are more commonly used.
But I find that parts of speech tagging is really useful as a filtering technique.
I'll often filter by just nouns or proper nouns, for example, before I apply other machine learning
algorithms.
So if you look at the code for this, you can see here in that second block, again I'm using a list
comprehension.
And the difference here is that I'm now returning two pieces of information.
First, I'm returning the text from that token.
So you can see I'm selling lemons for $5 today.
And then I'm also returning the part of speech.
So you can see I is a pronoun.
Lemons is a noun.
And so on.
Now again, parts of speech tagging is a lesser used technique compared to all the other techniques
that we've covered throughout these lessons.
However, it's a good way to showcase one of the many linguistic analysis capabilities that are available
within Spacy.
Some other types of analysis include things like named entity recognition, which allows you to extract
common terms in your text.
There's also dependency parsing, which allows you to see relationships between words in your text,
and so on.
One thing that's interesting about linguistic analysis techniques, such as parts of speech tagging,
is that it actually works better with raw text.
And the reason for that is because a lot of these analysis techniques use a combination of linguistic
rules as well as statistical models.
So in addition to logic, like if you see a capital I on its own, that's a pronoun.
It also takes neighboring words into account.
And that's where the modeling comes in.
So in one situation, if we had the word play, depending on where it is in a sentence, it might be
a noun.
And if it's somewhere else, it might be a verb.
Okay, now that we've introduced four different pre-processing techniques within Spacy, let's actually
play around with them some more.
Within a few demos within Jupyter Notebook.
```

### PRO TIP: Use the Apply Method {#pro-tip-use-the-apply-method}

```
Now, just like we did with the last demo.
As a pro tip or a best practice, I'm going to put all these things here that we just talked about into
a function so that we can apply it to multiple data sets.
Let's start by defining a new function.
So in this case our function is going to do three things.
It's going to tokenize our text.
It's going to lemmatize our text.

### Vectorization {#vectorization}

```
Now that we've talked about cleaning and normalization, let's move on to vectorization.
Vectorization is the idea of converting text data into numeric data.
And the reason you would do something like this is because most of the data analysis techniques that
are out there work best on numeric data.
So once you get your text data into a numeric format, then you can use it for data analysis and input
it into machine learning models.
I do want to mention that while vectorization is needed for most techniques, it's not needed for all
techniques.
So to state that a bit more clearly, for most machine learning models, the inputs need to be in a
numeric format, so it requires that your raw text is cleaned and then normalized and put into a numeric
format before you can apply machine learning.
However, there are some other techniques out there, such as sentiment analysis that require text to
be in its raw text form, so not numeric.
And the reason for this is because with something like sentiment analysis, word order matters.
So happy might be positive, but then not happy should be negative.
This is something we briefly touched on in this section as well for linguistic analysis techniques such
as parts of speech tagging.
often that raw text form is better than data that's clean, normalized, and vectorized.
That said, if you want to apply machine learning techniques to your text data, then vectorization
is required.
And in this course we're going to be covering three different vectorization techniques.
The simplest one is word counts.
So you can see here every row represents a different sentence.
And then we're just simply capturing the number of times each word appears in a specific sentence.
We're also going to be talking about something called TF-IDF.
And if you compare that with the word counts, you can see that where the zeros are there are also zeros
in this TF-IDF output.
But then where you see those one values there's decimal values here instead.
And those all have meaning.
And then finally later on in the modern NLP section of this course we're going to talk about embeddings.
Embeddings are a much more sophisticated technique that utilizes deep learning.
So we'll be talking about that later.
And then for this section we're going to focus on the first two techniques.
So let's start by becoming familiar with this idea of a document term matrix.
Typically what happens in the NLP pipeline is you clean and normalize your text.
And then that output is turned into a document term matrix or a DTM.
And this DTM is a vectorized version of your text or a numeric version of your text.
And those numbers are all represented inside a matrix, where each row represents a different document,
or a phrase or a sentence.
And then each column represents a different term, hence the name document term matrix.
Now, in terms of the values inside that DTM, they can be a variety of things such as word counts,
TF-IDF scores and so on.
Let's start with a very basic example of just word counts.
Here we have our text Clean series.
These are eight documents that have been cleaned and normalized.
And now let's vectorize it.
So let's make this text numeric by turning it into a document term matrix.
That would look something like this.
You can see in this case every row is a document.
Every column is a term.
And all of the values are word counts.
So to look at a specific document you can see in this case Lemon Maven market all those word counts
are captured on the right here.
So to summarize every value here is the count of a specific term within a document.
And then if you go down a few rows to row three, you can see that six value there because there are
six lemons within that document.
So again this document term matrix is capturing word counts.
Now when we talk about dtms sometimes you'll hear the term bag of words.
Because this idea here is a bag of words representation of the text.
So every document is just represented by a few words.
And order doesn't matter.
So it's like you're holding a bag of words for every document.
And the interesting thing about this bag of words idea that doesn't take order into account, is that
it actually works decently well.
While modern techniques do take order into account, and the results are really good with traditional
techniques, sometimes this simple model is good enough.
So if you look at that zeroth row up there, it's about life, lemons and lemonade.
And that's a pretty good representation of the text.
You can also look at the last row there iced tea favorite.
And again that gives you a decent sense of what the text is all about.
Okay.
Now that you understand this basic idea of a document term matrix, let's actually create one within
Python.
```

### Count Vectorizer in Python {#count-vectorizer-in-python}

```
Within Python, you would create a Countvectorizer object to make a document term matrix.
Here's what the code for that would look like.
You can see where first importing count vectorizer from scikit learn, and in typical scikit learn fashion,
the first thing we have to do is create a Countvectorizer object.
Now, within this Countvectorizer object, we're specifying three parameters here stopwords, ngram,
range, and min df.
These are all optional parameters, but they are the three that I use most frequently.
So first stopwords.
This is a concept you're already familiar with.
These are words that don't have much meaning, and within your Countvectorizer object you can specify
a language here.
So you can say remove all English Stopwords.
By default no stopwords are removed.
There's also this n gram range and this is where you set your term length.
Do you want to see unigrams or one words bigrams, two words, trigrams, three words, and so on.
So by default the n Ngram range is 1 to 1, so that would only return single words.
Sometimes I set it to 1 to 2.
That way it returns both single words as well as bigrams or two term words.
And then you can see in the last example three three would return only trigrams or three word terms.
And finally there's this min df here.
This stands for minimum document frequency.
And in this case when I set it equal to two, that means only return words that are in a minimum of
two documents.
You can also set a percentage here.
And this is something I'll walk through in a lot more detail within the demo.
The default here is one.
Meaning just return all the terms in our corpus.
A corpus is a collection of documents, or you can think of it as a corpus is just all of our text data.
Now, one more thing you may have noticed here that's interesting, or even a bit confusing, is that
we're removing Stopwords again.
We recently removed these in the last section on Spacy, and we're doing it here again.
So you may be wondering, why are we doing it twice?
And the reason is because these are two different Python libraries.
We had Spacy before and now we're in scikit learn.
And they're designed by two different developers.
And both of them thought that stopwords might be useful within their libraries.
So because of that you have the option to choose which library you want to remove Stopwords in.
So down here I mentioned that both tokenization and removing stopwords are things that you can do within
both spacy and scikit learn.
So at the end of the day, it's really up to you which library you choose to do so.
If you're doing more linguistic analysis within Spacy, I would recommend do your stopword removal there.
But if you're working with data frames a lot and working on machine learning models with scikit learn,
then you can remove your stopwords here within sklearn.
Okay, let's see an example of this.
Here we have our text clean data once again.
This data has been cleaned and normalized using Pandas and Spacy.
And now we're going to turn it into a document term matrix using Countvectorizer.
So here's the code to do that.
Let's walk through this step by step at a high level.
And then we'll dive into more details when we actually code it up in a demo.
So first you can see I'm importing Countvectorizer.
Then I create a Countvectorizer object.
And in this case, because this is our first exposure to Countvectorizer, I'm including no parameters
in there.
I'm just creating or instantiating our first Countvectorizer object here.
And then the next step we say take that Countvectorizer and do a fit transform of text clean.
If you've used scikit learn in the past, you'll know that this is a very common thing you do with scikit
learn objects.
In this case, with Countvectorizer, what the fit does is it looks at all your text, it tokenizes
it, and then it finds all the unique terms in that text and makes those your columns of data.
And then it does the transform.
And what the transform does is it looks at every row in your data frame or every document in your corpus,
and then it fills in those word counts.
So it actually sees how often each term appears in each document.
Now after that we have a really long line of code.
We'll talk about this a lot more within the demo.
But the idea of this line of code is just to turn the output from the previous line into a data frame.
It looks complex, but it's doing something simple, which is turning a messy DTM object into this nice
clean data frame here.
And this here is your document term matrix.
By using just the default parameters, because we did a countvectorizer with nothing in the parentheses,
we ended up with all these word counts here.
And these are the word counts for the 15 terms that Countvectorizer found in our text clean series.
And you can see the specific word counts for all eight documents.
Now, if you're looking closely at that text clean series on the left, you may notice that in row four
there's an S and that doesn't appear in our final document term matrix.
And the reason for that is because of the way Countvectorizer does its tokenization.
It only returns terms that have two or more letters in it.
This is something we'll address in the demo as well, and it's something that you typically don't have
to deal with.
But sometimes it's nice to really understand the specifics of how all the calculations are being made
behind the scenes.
Okay, let's look at one more Countvectorizer object before we move on to our demo.
So in the last case, we just used all the default parameters, and we ended up with those 15 columns
or those 15 terms in this case here I'm creating the second Countvectorizer object or CV two.
And you can see within the Countvectorizer parentheses I'm including three different parameters.
So I'm specifying I want to remove stopwords that are English Stopwords I want to include both unigrams
and bigrams or one and two word terms.
And then finally I want to only return terms that appear in two or more documents.
So you can see in the code after we do that specification.
Everything else is exactly the same as before, except I've added a two to all the variable names,
and then this output here is a little bit different.
You can see what's happened here is we originally had 15 columns and that's been reduced to nine columns.
This reduction in the number of columns is a really common thing to do.
Because when you create countvectorizer objects, you typically end up with thousands of columns, because
if you're looking at a large amount of text, there's typically thousands of unique words in that text.
However, if you're inputting that data into machine learning models, you typically don't want too
many columns.
We talk about this more in our earlier courses in this Data Science in Python series.
To create a simple model, you don't want to include too many columns.
So often with Countvectorizer, you'll introduce these parameter tweaks to reduce the number of columns.
Now, in this case by indicating English stopwords that would reduce the text.
And then in the next parameter ngram range, we're actually increasing the number of columns by including
bigrams as well, such as iced tea, lemon, lemon and maven market.
We're increasing the number of columns.
But then finally with that minimum document frequency parameter, we're reducing the number of columns.
So if you look on the left there in row two you see the words dozen and gallon.
But because those appear just once in the entire corpus, we're not going to include them in the document
term matrix, because that information might not be as useful in this document term matrix.
If we're trying to compare documents with one another, and only that document has the terms dozen and
gallon, we can't really compare that with another document.
Now, that's just one example of some analysis you can do.
But the general idea here is with something like Mindf, you can reduce the number of columns.
And you can see after all those parameter tweaks, our columns have been reduced from 15 columns to
nine columns.
Now let's dive into a Jupyter notebook to demo all the concepts that we just learned.
```
