### TF-IDF {#tf-idf}

```
Next, let's talk about tf IDF.
This stands for term frequency inverse document frequency, which is quite a mouthful, but the main
thing you need to know is that it's an alternative to the word count calculation within a document term
matrix.
So how is it different than just simple word counts?
The main idea is that it emphasizes important words by reducing the impact of common words.
So how does it emphasize important words?
It does so using this calculation here.
Now I know this looks like a lot.
So let's go through this formula step by step.
Let's start with that tf IDF on the left.
This score here is calculated for every cell within a document term matrix.
So if you think about our original DTM, remember each row is a different document.
Each column is a different term.
And then each value inside that DTM was a word count.
In this case we're going to replace each value with a tf IDF score.
So this whole equation here is calculated for every document term combination.
And this calculation comes in two parts.
First there's the term frequency side.
And then there's the inverse document frequency side.
So let's start with the term frequency side.
If you look at this formula here it's calculating the term count in a document divided by the total
terms in that document.
So the way you can think about this is let's say we had a document with the word cat in it three times
and also dog in it three times.
If we were looking at word counts, the value for cat would be three and the value for dog would be
three.
But in this case, when we're looking at term frequency, if we were to make this calculation on that
document, it would say the term cat appears three times.
That would be in the numerator.
And then in the denominator we have six terms total three cats and three dogs.
So this section here would end up being three over 6 or 1 half or 50%.
And this would tell me Cat shows up as 50% of the terms in this document.
So we've essentially taken a word count and turned it into a percentage.
Now why would you want to calculate term frequency?
The main problem that it solves is getting rid of high counts.
If you have one document that's much longer than another document, those high counts can dominate.
So maybe one document is really short and it just shows cat one time and dog one time.
But then another document is really long and it shows cat 100 times and dog 100 times.
And if you were to compare those two, the word counts are actually not similar at all.
But if you change it to term frequency, in both cases the documents are 50% cat related.
And so those two documents end up being quite similar after all.
So another way to think about it is by calculating this term frequency, you're normalizing the counts
so that they're all on the same scale.
Now when I say normalizing here I'm talking about normalizing numeric data.
Just a few lessons ago we talked about normalizing text data, which is all about making your text consistent.
And you'll see that is similar to this numeric normalization.
Here we're putting all of our numbers on a more consistent or similar scale.
Okay.
Now let's talk about inverse document frequency.
I know this side of the equation looks a bit complex, but I promise you the takeaway makes a lot of
sense.
So let's just leave out the log and the plus one pieces.
To start with.
The main calculation here is total documents divided by documents with the term.
Now, the goal of this side of the formula is trying to figure out which terms are important versus
not important.
So let's say we had ten documents, and in just one of those documents we mentioned the word cat.
And then in five of the documents we mentioned the term the.
If we were to make this calculation here for both those terms, we would get the importance of a particular
word.
So in the case of Cat, we would have ten total documents divided by one document with that term.
So for Cat the IDF score would be ten.
Now for the term.
The five out of the ten documents contain that word.
So to calculate the IDF score ten divided by five would be two.
So the would have an IDF or an important score of two, whereas Cat would have an IDF or important score
of ten.
And using this calculation, we were able to say cat is much more important than the because its score
is ten versus two.
So that's all that IDF is calculating the importance.
So to summarize the problem that it solves when we're using word counts, each word is treated equally.
If cat shows up one time, it's labeled as a one.
If the shows up one time, it's labeled as a one.
But cat being a rare word is much more important than the.
So with IDF, we assign more weight or we emphasize those rare words.
So cat would be given a weight of ten and the would be given a weight of two.
Now what about that log in the plus one starting with the plus one.
The reason there's a plus one there is because in the denominator, if that value was zero, if no documents
contain a certain term, then you couldn't do this division.
You can't divide by zero.
So because of that we add a plus one to the denominator to make sure that the math works.
We're also doing a plus one on the top to keep things consistent.
And then after that we're also doing a log of all this.
And what happens when you log something is whatever calculation you make it dampens those higher calculations.
So when our cat and the example, our final weight calculations were ten and 210 might feel like too
extreme of a weight.
So if you were to take the log of ten and two, you end up getting 2.3 and 0.7 respectively, which
are a bit more even.
So that's what those extra calculations there mean.
Now, we've covered a lot here, so I just want to summarize the main takeaways.
Remember that a TF-IDF score is a better version of a word count, and it consists of two parts.
The side with the term frequency is normalizing.
All of your word counts so that they're all on the same scale.
So you can think of this as a percentage of word counts instead of raw word counts.
And then the right side is all about importance.
So more rare words are given more weight and then more common words are given less weight.
And together they form a different representation of the terms in the documents.
Personally, this is another surprising calculation for me within the data science space.
It seems like a relatively simplistic calculation or representation of the text, but it's actually
a pretty nice way of looking at the text.
And when you look at the results, they make a lot of sense.
So now at this point, let's take a look at how you would calculate these TF-IDF scores within Python
and then also compare them with our original word counts.
```

### TF-IDF Vectorizer in Python {#tf-idf-vectorizer-in-python}

```
To calculate TF-IDF scores within Python instead of Countvectorizer, you would create a Tfidfvectorizer
instead.
Now the format of this is almost identical to a Countvectorizer.
You'll see very similar code and many of the same parameters.
So here's what the code would look like.
You can see the main difference is that now instead of importing Countvectorizer we're importing Tfidfvectorizer.
And then below that the first thing we have to do is instantiate or create a new Vectorizer.
And in this case we're creating a Tfidfvectorizer.
And then after that we go through the same steps.
We do a fit transform.
We turn it into a data frame and then we view the output.
And with the fit transform the steps are the same.
When it's doing a fit, it's finding the unique terms within the documents or within the corpus.
And then when it's doing the transform, it's actually calculating those values.
Before it was calculating word counts.
But in this case it's calculating the TF-IDF score for each cell.
Now one thing that you notice immediately is that instead of integers, everything here is now a decimal.
But in terms of the shape of this data frame or this matrix, it's exactly the same as our first Countvectorizer
example.
We have eight documents here that are represented by the rows.
And we have 15 columns here.
And those are all the terms in our corpus.
And the only difference are the values here that are now TF-IDF scores.
Now from here let's update our code to include the same parameters as our Countvectorizer example.
So in this case we're excluding English stopwords, including bigrams as well as the unigrams and setting
a min document frequency of two, so that only terms that show up in two or more documents show up in
this final output.
And you'll see here, just like before, we end up with nine terms instead of 15.
And the reason for that is because with that dot fit step, we're doing the exact same thing here with
Tfidfvectorizer as we did with Countvectorizer.
It's just listing the documents here and then listing those unique terms.
And then the difference is in that transform step where all these values get filled with tfidf score
values instead of word counts.
So the main takeaway here is that for both Countvectorizer and Tfidfvectorizer, whatever parameter
tweaks you decide on that will create the general structure of your document term matrix.
And the only difference is now instead of counts, we have TF-IDF scores as the values.
Now, before we dive into a demo, let's compare the counts with the Tfidf scores side by side so you
can see the differences.
I'm going to point out a few different situations here.
First, if you look at document one you'll see that Lemon Market and Maven all show up once in that
document.
And so within the word counts those are all represented by one.
And all those terms are equal.
However if you look at the Tfidf scores you'll notice that Maven and Market have higher values than
lemon.
And the reason for that is because it's saying that Maven and market are more important, or they're
more rare within this corpus or this body of text we're working with.
And that makes sense because lemon is a really common word in all of our sentences.
But maven and market are not, and that's accounted for in the Tfidf score, Maven and market.
If you were to compare that to other documents, it's a better differentiator of the differences between
documents.
Now let's look at another example.
In this case here you see the number six because lemon showed up six times within this document.
But then over here you can see it's been reduced to one because it's showing a percentage instead of
an actual count.
So on the left side that six really skews the data.
It really sets that document apart from all the other documents.
But on the right side here, it's just saying 100% of the words in this document are lemon.
Depending on what type of analysis you want to do, maybe the counts on the left are better, and in
other cases the normalized values on the right are better.
It really depends on your analysis, and this is something that you can easily swap out and test to
see which one works the best for your data set and for your goal.
Now, one more example I want to show you is this example of lemonade versus tea.
So if you look at the lemonade column, you'll see that lemonade shows up three times and tea shows
up just two times.
So out of the two, tea is the less frequent word, or it's the more important word.
Now that isn't captured with the word counts on the left here, but on the right you'll see it is captured.
You can see in the top two cases of lemonade there's a 0.82.
But then in the bottom you have a 0.52.
So within those documents at the top, lemonade is really important.
But then when you're looking at document six at the bottom, it contains both lemonade and tea.
But it knows that tea is the more important word or it's the more rare word.
So tea is given a weight of 0.6, versus lemonade is given a weight of 0.52 here, because with the
TF-IDF score, it's able to capture that importance and give that extra weight to tea in this case.
Okay, now that you have a general idea of the differences between count and TF-IDF, let's walk through
the code for this within a demo.
```

We talk about this more within the supervised and unsupervised learning courses, within this data Science
and Python series for supervised learning models.
When you're trying to predict something, if you have too many columns, that could lead to overfitting.
And then with unsupervised learning models, if you have too many columns, that can lead to something
called the curse of dimensionality, where every row becomes very different from one another, and then
it's hard to compare rows.

### What is Machine Learning? {#what-is-machine-learning}

```
Let's start by answering the question what is machine learning?
We mentioned this a little bit earlier in the NLP 101 section, where we talked about machine learning
being a subset of artificial intelligence.
And the whole idea here is that we're trying to teach computers to be like humans.
And with machine learning specifically, we're trying to teach computers to learn like humans.
So to recap that all in one sentence, data scientists use machine learning.
And then those machine learning algorithms will enable computers to learn and also make decisions from
data.
Of course, data scientists are there the whole way to make sure that those decisions are the right
ones.
Now, when we talk about machine learning algorithms, they generally fall into two main categories
supervised learning and unsupervised learning.
Supervised learning algorithms are ones that use historical data to predict the future.
The key word here is predict.
Many times when people think of data scientists or machine learning, this is what they think of.
Being able to make predictions.
On the other hand, you have unsupervised learning.
And this is all about finding patterns or relationships in your data.
There are no predictions here, but this subset of machine learning is also really powerful.
It's all about trying to understand the structure of your data, not using intuition, but rather using
algorithms.
So let me show you a few examples of both supervised and unsupervised learning.
A common supervised learning question is what will house prices look like for the next 12 months.
In this case we're going to use historical data.
So house prices in the past.
And then we're going to use that to predict house prices in the future.
Another example of supervised learning is how can I flag suspicious emails as spam.
So in this case we would look at past emails that are either spam or not spam and understand what a
spam email looks like versus a not spam email.
So that in the future when we see a new email, we can predict whether it's spam or not spam.
Now, as for unsupervised learning examples, a popular one is how can I segment my customers?
And the idea here is you have many customers.
You don't know how they're different from one another.
And so you can use an unsupervised technique to find those differences and separate out your customers.
Another example of this is what hidden themes are in these product reviews.
Maybe you're looking at thousands of product reviews, and you want to know the most common topics that
people are talking about, and that's something that can be done with an unsupervised learning technique.
So in short, supervised learning is all about making predictions.
And then unsupervised learning is all about finding patterns.
Now within these four examples here there's actually a hidden nugget as well.
The top two situations both deal with primarily numeric data.
And then the bottom two situations deal with text data.
So the bottom two are both NLP specific examples of supervised and unsupervised learning.
And within this section we'll talk about both these examples and a lot more detail.
```

### Common ML Algorithms for NLP {#common-ml-algorithms-for-nlp}

```
There are so many machine learning algorithms that are out there.
And the main takeaway I want you to get from this lesson is that you can use any of these common machine
learning algorithms for NLP.
Once you've pre-processed your text data or gotten it from that text form to that numeric form, you
can just input that numeric data or that document term matrix into a machine learning model, just like
we've been doing throughout this entire Data Science in Python series.
Now let's walk through some of those machine learning algorithms at a high level.
At the very top, we have our overall umbrella of machine learning.
And like we talked about in the last lesson, there are two main categories of machine learning.
Supervised learning where you're making a prediction, and unsupervised learning where you're finding
patterns within supervised learning.
We can divide that into two more categories.
There's regression techniques and classification techniques.
Regression techniques help you predict a continuous variable such as house prices or temperature.
And then classification techniques help you predict a categorical variable.
So things like spam or not, spam, high priority versus low priority, and so on.
Here are some examples of regression techniques.
The main ones are linear regression and time series analysis.
These are some common classification techniques.
The main ones here are k-NN and logistic regression.
And then the bottom three are tree based algorithms.
And these classification techniques can also sometimes be used for regression, but for the most part
they're there used to classify categorical data.
And finally we have all these unsupervised learning techniques here.
The top few are clustering techniques.
The middle one is an anomaly detection technique and the bottom few are dimensionality reduction techniques.
Now, if a lot of these terms look unfamiliar to you and you want to learn more, then I highly recommend
that you check out the other courses in our Data Science in Python series.
It's a five part series, and parts two, three, and four correspond with regression, classification,
and unsupervised learning.
And then finally, for this fifth course on NLP, we're going to be covering two additional algorithms
Naive Bayes and Non-negative matrix factorization.
These two algorithms are general machine learning algorithms, but they work really well on NLP tasks,
which is why we're introducing them in this course.
Naive Bayes is a supervised learning technique that works really well for text classification, and
then NMF is an unsupervised learning technique, more specifically, a dimensionality reduction technique
that works really well for topic modeling.
Within this section, we're going to be introducing these two new algorithms, and we're going to apply
them to our vectorized text data.
But like I mentioned at the beginning of this lesson, while these two techniques are more often used
for NLP tasks, you can use any of the algorithms that you see here on your text data.
As long as you've vectorized it or converted it into a numeric format so that you can input it into
a machine learning model.
```

### Traditional NLP Overview {#traditional-nlp-overview}

```
In the NLP 101 section, we talked about the three different phases of NLP over time, starting with
early NLP, then traditional NLP and most recently modern NLP.
In this section, we're going to quickly touch on an early NLP approach and then spend most of our time
focused on traditional NLP.
We'll be doing this by covering three common NLP tasks that are often solved using traditional NLP methods,
starting with simple rules based techniques that air on the NLP side.
But we're going to lump it all in here within the traditional NLP section.
And then we're also going to talk about a few more complex NLP tasks, which involve machine learning.
We'll be covering sentiment analysis, text classification and topic modeling.
And these encapsulate three different types of traditional NLP approaches.
First, for sentiment analysis, this is all about identifying the positivity or negativity of text.
We're going to be using a rules based technique to do this.
And specifically the library we're going to be using is called Vader.
Another interesting thing about sentiment analysis is that your input into a sentiment analysis analyzer
should be raw text.
And the reason for that is because word order matters and you don't want to vectorize your text.
You actually want to keep that raw order so that you can get a better idea of the sentiment.
We'll also be talking about text classification, and this is all about classifying text as one label
or another.
For this we're going to be using a supervised learning technique.
Again the goal here is to make a prediction.
And the NLP specific technique we'll be talking about is Naive Bayes.
For this we're going to be using scikit learn.
And then our input format is going to be vectorized text.
So either a countvectorizer or a Tfidfvectorizer that we created in the text pre-processing section.
And finally for topic modeling this is all about finding themes within a corpus of text.
Remember a corpus of text just means many text documents.
So this is about finding themes within a larger body of text.
The approach we'll be using here is unsupervised learning.
Remember that's about finding patterns in our data.
And then the specific technique we're going to introduce is called NMF.
Again we'll be using the scikit learn library.
And the input format is vectorized text.
So a document term matrix where the rows are documents the columns are terms.
And then the values are either counts or TF-IDF scores.
Once you see these three different types of traditional NLP techniques, in practice you'll be able
to apply other rules based supervised learning and unsupervised learning techniques that are out there.
```

### Traditional vs Modern NLP {#traditional-vs-modern-nlp}

```
A question you might have at this point is when should I use traditional versus modern NLP techniques?
Remember, traditional NLP techniques typically involve machine learning, and then modern NLP techniques
involve deep learning.
We'll be talking about deep learning, Transformers and Llms later on in this course.
But in general, if you have the option of one or the other, the main thing that you should keep in
mind is start simple.
So here's a flowchart you can use to illustrate what I mean by starting simple.
When deciding between traditional versus modern, the first question you should ask yourself is what's
my NLP goal?
If your goal is one of these three tasks here, then you should be thinking to yourself, these are
all things that can be done using traditional NLP techniques.
And the second question you should ask yourself is how much data do I have?
If you have small to medium data so under roughly 100,000 rows, then in that case you should start
simple.
Try the traditional techniques first.
However, if you have a much larger data set so millions of rows of data, then you should consider
modern techniques.
But for the most part, if you're working with smaller data sets and you have one of these simpler goals
here, then you should go with a traditional technique.
On the other hand, if you have a more complex NLP goal such as generating text, translating text,
or answering questions, those cannot be done with traditional techniques.
So you have to use a modern technique instead.
So the main takeaway of this lesson is when you can start simple.
And that's what we're doing in this course.
We're starting with the traditional foundational skills before diving into more modern ones.
Now one more thing I want to mention is if you're still having a hard time differentiating these goals,
that's absolutely fine.
By the time you finish this course, you'll be more familiar with all of these concepts and have a much
clearer idea of the differences between them.
So with that, let's dive into our first traditional NLP task.
```

### Sentiment Analysis {#sentiment-analysis}

```
Let's kick things off with our first NLP application.
Sentiment analysis is a really common NLP task, and the idea is that you use it to determine the positivity
or negativity of text.
Typically, a sentiment score is given to each bit of text that's between negative one and positive
one to represent its negativity or positivity.
So let's say we had these three sample sentences.
If we were to apply sentiment analysis this first one would be labeled as neutral.
The second would be positive.
And then this third one would be negative.
Now if you look at these sentences, there are a few clues that tell us whether something is positive
or negative.
For example, this smiley face here, that's pretty positive.
And then this didn't here indicates negative sentiment.
Now one thing that I mentioned before, but I want to reiterate here, is you'll notice that sentiment
analysis is applied on raw text.
We just spent the entire last section talking about how you should vectorize your text and make your
text data numeric, and that's what you need to do for most data analysis and modeling techniques.
But sentiment analysis is an exception.
The reason that it's not cleaned is because punctuation like the smiley face matters.
And then it's also not vectorized because word order matters.
By looking at the raw text, we can see that it's negating the word like right after it.
From this lesson, you can see that the idea of sentiment analysis is pretty straightforward in theory.
And the good news is that it's also straightforward within Python.
```

### Sentiment Analysis in Python {#sentiment-analysis-in-python}

```
Now that you understand how sentiment analysis works, let's code it up in Python for sentiment analysis
specifically.
It can be done using a variety of approaches.
It can be done using rules based techniques, classification techniques, or modern NLP techniques.
In this demo, we're specifically going to be walking through the Vader library.
Vader stands for Valence Aware Dictionary and Sentiment Reasoner, and this library works especially
well on informal text, so things like social media text or online reviews.
This is one of the many sentiment analysis libraries that are out there.
So based on what type of text you're doing sentiment analysis on, you can choose an appropriate library
to work better on your type of text data.
Now, the general workflow for how to do sentiment analysis in all of these libraries is pretty similar.
So once you see how this is done in Vader, you're going to be able to do this on your own as well with
other libraries.
So here's what the code for Vader will look like.
You can see the first thing we're doing is importing the Sentiment intensity analyzer.
And then we have a sample string of text here.
And then to use Vader, the first thing we have to do is create a new sentiment intensity analyzer object.
Then we can take that object and do a dot polarity scores of our text.
And then very quickly we get all the sentiment information.
You can see here this is telling us that 0% of the text is negative, 75% of it is neutral and then
25% of it is positive.
And then the most important score is this compound score on the right here.
And this is the overall sentiment of the sentence.
And as a reminder, sentiment scores range from negative one to positive one.
So this 0.45 here means that this sentence here is positive.
Now you may be wondering how that compound score is calculated.
And the answer is that it's done using a series of rules.
First of all, Vader assigns predefined sentiment weights to thousands of words.
For example, amazing is given a score of 2.8, while horrible is given a score of -2.5.
And then to calculate this final compound score, it takes those sentiment weights.
And then it also incorporates modifiers.
So if the word na or vary is before a word, or if a word is in all caps, if there are certain punctuation
used, if there's an emoji present, and so on.
It uses basic math to compute a final sentiment score, which is this compound score you see here.
At every step, we're looking up the weights of the words, and then to incorporate modifiers, we're
seeing if certain words are present.
If they are, then make a particular calculation.
And that same if else logic gets applied to all this text, which makes it a rules based approach.
With that, let's dive into a demo to play around with all this more within a Jupyter notebook.
```

### Text Classification Basics {#text-classification-basics}

```
Next, let's move on to text classification.
This is a supervised learning NLP example.
The general idea of text classification is we have a bunch of text data, and we're trying to categorize
it into groups based on labeled data.
So what do I mean by labeled data.
Imagine we have these emails here.
Some of them are not spam and some of them are spam emails.
And this here is labeled data because even before starting our modeling, these emails have been pre-labeled
as either spam or not spam.
And now that we have this pre-labeled data, if we were to create a text classification model, then
going forward when we get a new email, our text classification model will tell us if it's spam or not.
So if we saw this new email that said send money ASAP, if we had a good text classification model,
it would tell us this is spam.
Another example of text classification would be for customer support tickets.
So let's say over the past year we've been collecting all these customer support tickets.
And every time one comes in, we label it as a billing issue, a tech support issue, or something else.
Again, this here is labeled data because before we bring it into a model, this data has already been
pre-labeled by someone on our team.
So at this point, again, we can put this data into a text classification model.
And then when we see a new support ticket that contains the text help me reset my password.
Our model will tell us what type of ticket it is, either a billing issue, a tech support issue, or
another issue.
So at a high level, I hope you have a better understanding of what text classification is.
As a reminder, you start with a bunch of text documents.
Those are pre-labeled as two or more categories.
And then you can create a classification model using that pre-labeled data so that when you see a new
text document, your model will automatically label it as one of your original labels.
```

### Text Classification Algorithms {#text-classification-algorithms}

```
Now, in terms of the algorithms that you can use for text classification, you can actually input your
vectorized text data into any classification algorithm.
So once you get your text data into a numeric form, it's just like any other numeric data set.
And you can use a wide variety of classification algorithms within part three of this data Science in
Python series, we cover classification.
And we talk about all these models here and a lot of detail.
And once you've vectorized your text you can simply input it into any of these models.
Now for this NLP course specifically, we're going to be introducing one more model called Naive Bayes.
And it's yet another classification algorithm that works on general numeric data.
But it works especially well on text data.
So at this point, you're probably wondering there are so many algorithms to choose from.
Which algorithm should I choose for my text data?
And there are no strict rules here.
But in general, if you have a small data set, a good idea is to start with Naive Bayes.
It's a really simplistic model and a solid starting point.
At this point, you might also want to try some other simplistic models, such as logistic regression
or k nearest neighbors.
If you're working with a medium data set.
A good idea is to start with logistic regression.
This is a solid machine learning algorithm that's used for a wide variety of tasks, and it performs
really well.
We'll be talking about this algorithm more in the neural networks section coming up next.
So if you need a quick refresher we'll get into that real soon.
But back to our medium data sets.
It's a good idea to start with logistic regression.
And then you can move on to slightly more complex algorithms, including all these tree based algorithms
that you see here.
And then finally, for large data sets, if you want to start with a traditional NLP approach, often
gradient boosted trees work really well.
And then if you need an even more powerful algorithm, then you can move on to modern NLP techniques.
Now for this course, our goal isn't to explain all these techniques in detail, but rather get you
familiar with the general workflow so that when you work on your own text classification problems,
you know where to start and you can swap out algorithms as needed.
```

### Naïve Bayes {#nave-bayes}

```
In this lesson, we'll be introducing the Naive Bayes algorithm.
This is a technique that's commonly used for text classification, and it's based on Bayes theorem.
I'll show you the formula for that in just a bit.
And one thing about Bayes theorem is it assumes conditionally independent features.
Now, I know that sounds really complex, but at a high level, what that means is if we're looking
at a bunch of emails and trying to figure out if there's spam or not, if we're looking at if it contains
the word ASAP and also if it contains a dollar sign, naive Bayes would assume that those two terms
are independent.
So whether an email contains the word ASAP doesn't affect if it contains a dollar sign or not.
In reality, though, those two things are related.
If an email has the word ASAP, it's more likely to contain a dollar sign.
But remember here we're dealing with Naive Bayes.
So again, it's based on this formula.
And it assumes this independence, which really isn't the case in reality.
This assumption is a naive one, which is why it's called Naive Bayes.
But in practice, the algorithm actually works surprisingly well on text data, which is why we're introducing
it here.
So to illustrate this, let's walk through an example.
Let's say we're trying to make this calculation.
If an email contains the word ASAP.
How likely is it going to be spam?
Now here's the formula for that.
And we'll walk through this step by step.
This first piece here is telling us the probability that an email will be spam, given that it contains
the word ASAP.
So this is just another way of restating our problem statement at the top.
But in mathematical terms.
And the way that we can find that probability is by making this calculation on the right.
First we're going to input the probability that the word ASAP appears in a spam email.
And how are we getting this information.
Remember we have pre-labeled data.
So within our Pre-labeled data set we can look at all the spam emails and find the probability that
the word ASAP appears in one of them.
And then we're going to multiply that by this probability here.
This is the probability that an email is spam.
So out of all the emails that we're feeding into our model, what percent of them are spam?
And then finally, we're going to divide all that by the probability that the word ASAP is in any email.
And with those three inputs, we can make a calculation and answer a question.
If an email contains the word ASAP, how likely is it going to be spam?
So let's actually plug in some numbers here.
So let's say we're starting with these thousand emails here.
If we look at the columns, the first column is telling us that there are 250 spam emails total in our
dataset.
And the zero column is telling us that there are 750 total non-spam emails.
And then the rows are telling us how often ASAP occurs.
So that first row is telling us that there are 60 emails that contain the term ASAP, and then the remaining
emails don't contain the word ASAP.
And now with that, we can plug these numbers into our formula here.
Remember, this was the formula on the right side of the equation on the previous slide.
And we're able to get all this information here from this data set summary on the left.
So first we need to figure out what's the probability that the term ASAP exists given that an email
is spam.
So to the left, if you look at that first column, those are all our spam emails.
And then 50 of them contain the word ASAP.
So the calculation here is 50 divided by all spam emails, which is 250.
And that's how we get this number here.
And then for the next calculation that's asking what's the probability that my email is spam.
Looking at all my emails.
So we have 1000 total emails.
That's what you see in the denominator.
And then the ones that are spam is what's in that left column there.
So there are 250 spam emails out of 1000.
And then at the bottom that's saying what's the probability that my email contains the word ASAP.
And for that we're summing up that top row of data.
So 60 total emails divided by all emails.
And now we run all those calculations.
At the very end our probability is roughly 0.83.
So what that's saying is there's an 83% chance an email is spam if it contains the word ASAP.
And if we think about that, that makes sense because ASAP feels urgent.
And that's what you typically see in spam emails.
Now this formula right here again, it's called Bayes theorem.
And when we're looking at just one word, which in this case was ASAP, this full calculation is just
Bayes theorem.
So now let's add some complexity to this.
Instead of just looking at if our email contains one word, let's run some numbers to see what happens
if it contains two words.
So let's update our example here.
Our new question is going to be if an email contains the word ASAP and the dollar sign symbol, how
likely is it going to be spam?
So for this example the formula is going to look a little bit different.
You'll see our goal here is roughly the same.
Before it was just looking at the probability of spam given that the term ASAP was in there.
But now we're looking at the probability that an email is spam, given that it contains both ASAP and
the dollar sign, Side.
And then as for the calculation, this piece is the exact same as before.
This is telling us the probability that the word ASAP appears in a spam email.
And now we've added this new piece here.
This is telling us the probability that a dollar sign appears in a spam email.
And then this piece is the same.
This is the probability that an email is spam in our input data set.
And then finally in the denominator this piece is tweaked a little bit.
This isn't just the probability that an email contains ASAP anymore, but rather that an email contains
both ASAP and the dollar sign.
Now, the piece I want to highlight in this formula here is this portion.
This here is what makes Naive Bayes naive.
The reason it's naive is because it treats all its input features as independent.
So it's assuming that the probability an email contains ASAP and the probability it contains a dollar
sign are independent, or they're not related to one another.
And in reality, that's actually not the case.
If the word ASAP appears in an email, there's a higher chance that the dollar sign will be present
as well.
So this here is a naive assumption, but the reason we're making this naive assumption is so that the
math is a lot easier.
By assuming these two parts are independent, we can simply multiply them together.
If we didn't make that assumption, this math here would be a lot more messy.
So for simplicity's sake, we're going to make this naive assumption and continue on with our calculation.
So again let's add some numbers into this.
You can see on the left we've added a new table.
And it gives us information about the dollar sign.
The way to read that 80 number is that out of all the spam emails, 80 of them contain dollar signs.
And then at the bottom, we also have information that 17 out of the thousand emails contain both the
words ASAP and a dollar sign.
And now let's plug in all our numbers first.
How often does ASAP appear in spam emails?
Then how often does the dollar sign appear in spam emails?
Then the probability that an email is spam overall.
And finally, the probability that the term ASAP and the dollar sign appear in an email together.
And now if we make all these calculations we get 0.94.
And this 0.94 tells us that there's a 94% chance an email is spam if it contains both the word ASAP
and a dollar sign.
And if we think about it, that makes a lot of sense.
Now, at this point, you might be thinking there are so many calculations that need to be made.
This seems like it would be really complex to implement, but it's actually the opposite of that.
When you're implementing this in a language like Python, all you have to do is specify that you want
to use Naive Bayes and then input in all your pre-labeled emails, and then all these probability calculations
will automatically be calculated for you by the model.
So now that you understand the theory, which is the most complex piece, let's create a Naive Bayes
model in Python, which is a lot simpler.
```

### Naïve Bayes in Python {#nave-bayes-in-python}

```
To work with Naive Bayes in Python.
We'll be using scikit learn once again.
And specifically we'll be using Multinomial Naive Bayes.
So to use this first you need to specify your input data, which in this case will be a Countvectorizer
or Tfidfvectorizer.
That's what we created at the end of the text pre-processing section.
And then the nice thing about Naive Bayes is that there are no parameters to tune.
So all you have to do is specify that you want to use Naive Bayes algorithm, and then you specify your
input and you should be all set.
Here's what the code for that will look like.
You can see first we're importing Multinomial Naive Bayes.
And then we're following the standard scikit learn process for a supervised learning model.
The first thing we're doing is instantiating a multinomial Naive Bayes object, which in simpler terms,
just means that we're creating a new multinomial Naive Bayes model.
And then the next step is to fit a Naive Bayes model.
And this is where we input in our data.
That X train will be either a Countvectorizer or a Tfidfvectorizer object, and that ytrain will be
the corresponding labels.
So whether each email is spam or not spam.
And then finally the last step is to make a prediction.
And in this case we're going to take our fitted model and use it to predict a new data point.
This might be a new email that doesn't have a label yet, and we want our model to tell us if it's spam
or not.
Spam.
Now for this demo.
I'm going to be walking through this process pretty quickly because the focus of this course is on NLP
and text data.
So I don't want to spend too much time diving into the supervised learning workflow.
If this is really interesting to you and you want to learn more, we talk about this scikit learn process
as well as things like splitting your data into train and test sets, cross-validation, and much more.
Within part three of this Data Science in Python series on classification, which is taught by my colleague
Chris Brule.
One more thing I want to mention before we dive into the demo is for the demo, we'll be using Multinomial
Naive Bayes, because the countvectorizer that we're going to be working with will have counts.
So all the values will be zero, one, two, and so on.
However, if your countvectorizer just had zero and one values, you would import Bernoulli Naive Bayes
instead.
So just to clarify this piece a bit more, let's say we're looking at spam emails and the term ASAP.
We could represent that ASAP as a count, or we could represent it as A01 value.
So in the case of a count, we would say in this email ASAP appears three times and that three would
get recorded.
And in that case we would use the Multinomial Naive Bayes model.
On the other hand, if we were just looking at zero one values, we would say does ASAP exist in this
email or not?
0 or 1.
And if it exists, we would put in a one if that's the way we're representing our input data.
Just as an indicator instead of a count.
Then we would use Bernoulli Naive Bayes instead.
In general, though, because we've introduced countvectorizer in this course, most of the time you'll
be working with counts.
And so because of that, typically you'll be importing Multinomial Naive Bayes.
Now with that, let's dive into our demo.
```

```

And now if I run that we get this sparse matrix.
So I'm going to turn this output here which I'm going to call x.
I'm going to turn that into a data frame.
And then I have to do a two array here.
And then also specify the column names.
And now if I run this, you can see we've created a document term matrix with every row as a document,
every column as a term, and all these values here as counts.
Now if any of this is unfamiliar to you, I would recommend going back to the countvectorizer lessons
and rewatching those.
Okay, I'm going to save this output as my X data frame so that we can use it going forward.

This row count should be familiar to us.
This is the number of reviews we have.
And then there are so many columns here because within the reviews there are a lot of unique words.
You can see people are talking about calories in many different ways 100 cal, 100 calories, 100 cals.
And so because of that, we have a huge vocabulary.
So let's add some countvectorizer parameters here to reduce the size of this document term matrix.
Because for modeling, you typically don't want to input 2000 columns into your model if you only have
roughly 500 rows.
In general, the rule of thumb is for every 20 rows, you should have one column and that will prevent
you from overfitting.
So now within here, the first thing I'm going to do is remove stopwords.
And if I run that you can see I have some fewer columns here.
I'm also going to set a min document frequency to 20%.
And that's saying only return terms that appear in at least 20% of the reviews.
So now if I run this, you can see I get just 16 columns now.
And this is a lot more manageable.
And it follows that roughly 20 to 1 ratio.
And now if we look at the terms here they make a lot of sense.
These 16 terms here do a pretty decent job of capturing the variety of opinions on pop chips.
Now one more thing I want to add in here is the Ngram range.
And that's going to increase our number of columns.
But I just want to showcase how n-grams can be useful.
So if I run this one more time.
You can see the one term that's been added is potato chip.
And that could be useful for our model.
Okay.
Now at this point we have vectorized our reviews and we've called this x.

So in this case these are the 17 columns that I want to input into my Naive Bayes model.
So I've called it x to specify that.

And then the only code we need is what's down here.

The first thing we're doing is a train test split where we specify our X data, which in our case is
this x.
I'm going to say that here.
and then our Y data which is this here.
And that's our input and output data.
And when you're working with machine learning models, you typically don't want to fit a model on your
entire data set.
Instead, you want to split it into a train and test set, and then train your model on the training
set and test how well the model performs on your test set.
All you need to know at this point is that we're fitting our model on 80% of our data, and we're going
to test it on 20% of our data.
And now down here we're specifying our multinomial naive Bayes model.

And now if you look at these F1 scores, these scores here are combinations of precision and recall.

But this score here is not very good.
And this is one of the most important metrics for this problem.

### PRO TIP: Compare ML Models {#pro-tip-compare-ml-models}

```
Now that we've gone through the entire text classification workflow, let's switch out a few of the
pieces.
If I scroll back up here, you can see for our first round of modeling, we used a Countvectorizer and
input that into a Naive Bayes model.
Now for the second round of modeling, let's do something a little bit different, but I'm going to
copy this code while we're up here.
So for this one I'm going to use the Tfidfvectorizer.
And I'm going to use a logistic regression instead.
So for the second round of modeling, instead of using this countvectorizer here that I just copied
down from the top, I'm going to change everything here to be tfidfvectorizer instead.
And now if I run this, you can see my output here.
We have our 564 rows or reviews and then 17 columns with all the terms here.
And now these values are TF-IDF scores instead of counts.
Okay.
So at this point for our model we've specified what we want our input to be, which is X in this case.
And we also have to specify what we want our output to be.
And in this case we're going to use the same output as before with all these low and high priority labels.
Okay.
So now that we have our input and output the next step is to fit a model.
So for this I'm going to go back up here and I'm going to copy this code block down here.
And we just have to update a few things now.
So for this train test split section we have to specify our input and our output our input instead of
Xccdf which was a countvectorizer we are going to specify Xccdf which is this Tfidfvectorizer.
So let me change that to Xtf.
We have our same output which is this y.
These are all the same.
And now in this case, instead of our model being Multinomial Naive Bayes, let's use logistic regression
instead.
And instead of saving that as model, which would override our earlier Naive Bayes model, let's call
this model LR.
And then down here as well.
In addition, I'm going to add TS to these variable names up here as well.
So we don't overwrite our X trains and X tests from earlier on.
Now I've updated all our variable names here.
And the main differences are that we're inputting a Tfidfvectorizer instead of a countvectorizer.
And also we're using logistic regression instead of Naive Bayes.
So now let's run this.
You can see that our output here is very similar to before with slight differences.
You can see our accuracy here is 0.84.
And if I scroll back up here for our other model it was also 0.84.
But you can see the differences within the precision and recall scores.
So here the things I want you to remember are that this is 0.6 and this is 0.98.
And if we scroll down here, you can see those are both one here.
These values range from 0 to 1 with higher values being better.
So these ones here are really good.
Now the way to interpret this one here is that all the reviews that this logistic regression model predicted
as high priority were truly high priority.
And then down here with this one, this is saying out of all the actual low priority reviews, this
model was able to flag all of them.
And those two things seem pretty good on the surface.
But like I mentioned earlier for our analysis, this score here is the one that matters most.
So if we go back up to our Naive Bayes example, you can see this was 16%, whereas down here it's only
5%.
And this is saying out of all the reviews that are actually high priority reviews, how many was this
model able to capture?
And it only found 5%, which is not very good because our whole goal here, if we scroll back to the
top one more time is to predict which reviews are high priority, and if our model is only catching
5% of those, then that's a problem and we need to make our model better to specifically optimize for
that metric.
And we'll be talking about ways to do that in the next lesson.
Now, at this point, we've been able to compare these metrics side by side.
Another way of comparing models is looking at their prediction scores.
And this is the last thing I'd like to walk through before we wrap up this demo.
So let's start by looking at our model LR right here.
And what I'm going to output here are probability scores.
So so far we've been using the dot predict to predict whether something's going to be high priority
or low priority.
But what these models are really giving us isn't just a high or low, it's actually giving us a continuous
predicted probability.
So let's extract those values.
I'm going to do instead of predict I'm going to do a predictproba which is a predict probability.
And I'm going to apply this to my input data before we did the train test split.
That way I have these predicted probabilities for all my reviews.

### Text Classification Next Steps {#text-classification-next-steps}

```
Now that you understand the general text classification workflow, let's talk about where you can go
from here.
The next step is fine tuning or improving your classification model.
And you can do that by changing up any part of the NLP pipeline.
Remember the first thing we did was text pre-processing, and at this point you can update any of the
cleaning or normalization steps.
Maybe you missed something with your first round of cleaning.
That's really common and you need to go back and do more cleaning.
And then within the vectorization step, there's a lot more fine tuning that you can do.
We talked about all these countvectorizer parameters, and that controls how many features you're inputting
into your model.
By tuning these parameters, you can decrease or increase that.
Typically you want to decrease.
And then also you can swap out your vectorizer.
So instead of using Countvectorizer you can also try using Tfidfvectorizer instead.
And now one thing we haven't talked about that much is feature engineering.
But this is a really important step of the data science workflow.
The idea here is that we specify additional inputs that we can feed into our model.
That makes our model smarter.
So, so far with Countvectorizer, the only thing that we're feeding into our model is word counts.
But in addition to that, we can include non-word values such as text length, sentiment, time of day,
and so on.
If you think about a spam email example, the length of the email probably matters in determining whether
an email is spam or not.
If it's a really long email, it's likely from someone you know versus if it's really short, it's more
likely spam.
And you can use that text length as another input into your text classification model.
So in addition to all your term columns, you could include another column to capture the length of
your email.
You could also include a column on sentiment scores.
Earlier, we calculated all the sentiment scores for our text.
That score could be a column of its own.
And then finally we could include non text values.
So things like time of day something was sent that might be a good predictor.
Maybe if it's not sent during business hours that has some meaning.
Feature engineering is a really big part of the classification workflow and something that you'll spend
a lot of time doing as a data scientist.
And finally, let's talk about the modeling step.
One thing you can do to fine tune a classification model is try a different probability cutoff.
With classification models, typically they output a probability, and then anything that's over 0.5
will be labeled as a one and everything else is zero.
But that's something you can tune.
You can try a lower or higher cutoff point and see if your results are better.
In addition, like we mentioned before and you saw in the demo, you can try a different classification
model.
We went over logistic regression, but gradient boosted trees often work quite well and you can just
easily swap in other models here.
Now this slide here might be short, but don't let that fool you.
This is a big part of being a data scientist.
And you can see all these fine tuning steps are very similar to what you would do for a general classification
model.
And then here it's slightly tweaked for an NLP model.
The main difference is that there are a lot of additional steps you can do here that are text specific
to further tune your model, and that is your general text classification.
Overview.
```

### Topic Modeling Basics {#topic-modeling-basics}

```
The last concept we'll introduce in this section is topic modeling.
This is an example of how you can use unsupervised learning for NLP.
Topic modeling is one of my favorite topics within machine learning, and I feel like it's lesser known,
but the results can be so useful.
The idea of topic modeling is that you use an algorithm to find themes in your unlabeled text documents.
So the main thing to note here is that our text is unlabeled when we covered text classification.
All of our inputs were labeled.
So before modeling we had a bunch of emails and we knew which emails were spam and which emails were
not spam.
With topic modeling, we don't have those labels.
So instead we would just be given a bunch of emails with no labels of whether they're spam or not.
Spam.
And with a topic modeling algorithm, we're able to see what the main themes or the main topics are
within all the emails.
Now, one interesting thing about topic modeling is that using an algorithm, it's able to extract the
topics from the data, but it's still up to you as a human to interpret those topics, see if they make
sense and name them.
So let's walk through a high level example of this.
Imagine we have all these phrases here.
As a human, you're probably looking at these phrases, and you already have a sense of the general
themes in these phrases.
Now, how does a computer do that?
What it would do is look at all these phrases, and let's say it's looking for two topics within the
phrases.
It would go through each phrase and then say this one is 100% topic one.
This other one is 100% topic two.
And then these ones here are a mix of both.
So again, what a topic modeling algorithm would do is tell us how much each phrase is topic one versus
topic two.
And you might be wondering what our topic one and topic two.
And that's the job of you as a human to figure out.
So the model would tell us that for topic one these are the main terms.
And then for topic two these are the main terms.
And we can clearly see as a human that all these things here have to do with food.
And then all these things here have to do with animals.
And that's the general idea and workflow for topic modeling.
Again, we start with unlabeled data.
The algorithm tells us these main topics.
And then it's up to us as a data scientist to interpret those topics.
```

### Topic Modeling Algorithms {#topic-modeling-algorithms}

```
Now let's dive a bit deeper into the technical piece.
When you start a topic modeling project, the first thing you need is vectorize text data.
Just like the case with text classification, you need to transform your text data into a numeric form
so that you can input it into a topic modeling algorithm.
And there are many algorithms to choose from, so you may be wondering which algorithm should I choose
for my text data?
If you're working with a smaller data set, I would recommend starting with non-negative matrix factorization
or NMF, which is what we'll be covering within this course.
This uses the scikit learn library, which is something you're already familiar with, and it also gives
you pretty interpretable topics for a small data set.
If you're working with a medium data set, you can try starting with another algorithm called Latent
Dirichlet Allocation.
This is a probabilistic model, so there's some randomness involved.
And that works better on medium datasets versus small datasets.
This uses the Gensim library, which is something we're not covering in this course.
But once you get the general idea of NMF, which we are covering in this course, you'll understand
the general topic modeling workflow and be able to swap out your algorithm if you want to try something
else out.
Now, if you're really interested in learning about LDA right away, you can check out my YouTube video
on it.
This video is from quite a few years ago, but the algorithm still stands, the math still works and
the general code structure is still the same.
Now, if you're working with a large data set, you may want to try out a modern NLP approach instead.
We'll be talking about embeddings later on, but there are several embedding based NLP approaches that
are good for topic modeling, such as Bert topic and Top Topic.
And again, once you're familiar with the high level topic modeling workflow, you'll be able to try
out these other techniques on your own.
```

### Non-Negative Matrix Factorization (NMF) {#non-negative-matrix-factorization-nmf}

```
Let's move on to the last algorithm for this section, called non-negative matrix factorization.
If you took the unsupervised learning course in this series just before this NLP course, you'll be
familiar with the idea of matrix factorization.
It falls under the general machine learning idea of dimensionality reduction.
So what does that mean?
Imagine you have a big table of data and you have your columns of data.
Those columns are sometimes called features or dimensions.
And the idea of dimensionality reduction is that you have a lot of columns and you're going to reduce
them into fewer columns, which is exactly what we're going to be doing in this lesson.
Keep that in mind.
That goal of taking many columns and reducing it into fewer columns, and we'll revisit that at the
end of this lesson.
Okay.
Let's start by defining non-negative matrix factorization.
It's often referred to simply as NMF, and it's one of the many topic modeling techniques that are out
there.
The idea is that it decomposes a matrix into two other matrices, and that starting matrix is going
to be our document term matrix.
We've also been calling it our countvectorizer or our Tfidfvectorizer.
And we're going to take that and split it into two matrices.
So what are those two matrices?
The first one is called a W matrix.
And it maps documents to topics.
So it shows for each document how much does each topic appear in that document.
And then the second matrix is the h matrix.
And that's the topic term matrix.
The idea here is we see for each topic what are the terms that are most relevant to that topic.
So to visualize this here is our V matrix or our starting matrix.
And because we're working with text data and doing NLP, this starting matrix will be our document term
matrix.
You can see every row is a document.
Every column is a term.
And then the values can be either counts or TF-IDF scores.
Now when we apply NMF this document term matrix gets split into these two matrices here.
And you can see this.
First one is our document topic matrix.
And this tells us for each document how much of it is topic one versus topic two.
If you remember earlier with our food versus animal example, one document might be more about food.
Another one is more about animals.
And then another one might be a mix of both.
And then on the right here we have our topic term matrix.
And this one helps us understand which terms are most relevant to each topic.
So for topic one, maybe the top terms are the ones related to foods.
And then for topic two it's the ones that are related to animals.
So to summarize, by looking at the matrix you can get a better idea of what the topics are all about.
And then looking at the W matrix what you get is for each document, what are the main topics that it's
composed of?
Now this here is one of many matrix factorization techniques and the unsupervised learning course.
We also talk about PCA principal component analysis as well as SVD singular value decomposition.
But out of these three matrix factorization techniques, NMF is the only one that returns all positive
results.
And this makes the most sense for text data.
When we were working with PCA before, the matrices on the right had positive and negative values.
But with NMF, all these values are non-negative.
Or in other words, they're positive.
And that makes sense here, because if we were looking at the H matrix, it wouldn't make sense for
a term to have a negative value within a topic.
So all these values here will be positive and the same with the W matrix.
It wouldn't make much sense for a topic to be negative in a document.
So that's why NMF is often used for NLP tasks versus these other matrix factorization techniques.
Now to wrap this all up, one thing I mentioned earlier on in this lesson is that NMF falls under the
general umbrella of dimensionality reduction.
And the whole idea there is you start with lots of columns of data and you reduce it into fewer columns.
And if you look at this visualization here, that's exactly what we're doing.
When we're going from V to W, we're starting with all those terms and we're reducing the columns so
that we get these topics.
This is one of the many advantages of dimensionality reduction.
Often when you have too many columns it's hard to interpret what's actually going on in those columns.
But when you reduce it into just a few columns like we did here, from terms to topics, those topics
often hold a lot more meaning, and we're able to interpret them and do a better job making sense of
our data.
```

### NMF in Python {#nmf-in-python}

```
To work with NMF within Python.
We're going to be using scikit learn once again.
Now the input into an NMF model is either a countvectorizer or a tfidfvectorizer.
And then from there we're going to use the NMF module.
Now with the NMF module it doesn't automatically tell you how many topics are within your corpus.
So what you'll typically do in practice is start looking at two topics and see if the results make sense.
And then you can increase that number by one and see if your topics make more sense.
And do that again and again until you figure out the best number of topics for your corpus.
So here's what the code for that would look like.
You can see the first thing we're doing is importing NMF.
And then after that we're going to follow the typical scikit learn process for an unsupervised learning
model.
So the first thing we're doing here is instantiating an object or creating a new NMF object.
And we're also setting some parameters here.
The main one is that n components parameter.
Again it's a best practice to start at two and then increase this by one at a time.
And there's also this random state parameter.
And the reason for this is because when you decompose a matrix into two other matrices, the way this
is done in Python incorporates Rates randomness.
So if you want the same set of results each time, you'll want to set a random state here.
And then the second step is to do a dot fit transform.
And the input in there that x value will be your Countvectorizer or your tfidfvectorizer.
This is a step where the decomposition actually happens that one matrix gets split into two.
And then finally, for an unsupervised learning model, the third thing you do within scikit learn is
take a look at the attributes.
So after a model has been fit, you want to see how it's been fit.
And the way you can do that for NMF is doing a dot components.
And that will show you for each topic.
What are the main terms that appear in that topic?
Now within this demo we're going to do a quick review of these steps.
But if this is really interesting to you and you want to spend more time exploring unsupervised learning
techniques such as dimensionality reduction and clustering, then I recommend you look into part four
of our Data Science in Python series on unsupervised learning.
We go through this scikit learn process many times, and we talk about all the other details as well,
including how to identify the best number of components, how to visualize the results, and much more.
Now with that, let's dive in to our NMF and Python demo.

### Topic Modeling Next Steps {#topic-modeling-next-steps}

```
Once you've done an initial round of topic modeling, you can continue to improve your model by tuning
any part of the NLP pipeline, starting with the text pre-processing steps.
Often when you're interpreting your topics, you may notice that certain characters or words show up
in your topics that you don't want, and you may need to do some additional cleaning or normalization.
You can also make tweaks to the vectorization step within both countvectorizer and Tfidfvectorizer.
There are a bunch of parameters you can tune.
Ngram is a nice one here for topic modeling, because it'll allow you to include two and three word
terms in your topics as well.
And in the demo we use Tfidfvectorizer, but you can also swap that out with Countvectorizer and see
if that works better instead.
And then moving on to the modeling phase.
The main parameter to tweak here is that n components parameter.
So again you want to start at two and then try increasing that.
And also you can try different topic modeling techniques.
At this point we covered NMF in this course.
But you can also try latent Dirichlet allocation within Gensim latent semantic analysis within scikit
learn.
And then more modern approaches like Bert topic or top to vec instead.
With all these techniques, the underlying algorithm is different, but the workflow is the same.
You start with your input text.
You see what the main topics are.
You interpret those topics, see if they make sense, and then you go on with your fine tuning.
And like I mentioned in the text classification next steps.
This fine tuning phase where you're improving your model is the main job of a data scientist.
So while we're going through all these steps very quickly on one slide, this is where you'll spend
a lot of your time.
Now to wrap up this section.
One more thing I want to show you is an example of how you can mix and match different algorithms for
your analysis.
So in our case, we're going to combine our topics along with sentiment scores and exploratory data
analysis to find out our customer sentiment about each topic.
```

### SOLUTION: Topic Modeling {#solution-topic-modeling}

```
For this topic.
