### Intro to Neural Networks {#intro-to-neural-networks}

```
Let's start with a high level overview of neural networks.
A neural network is a machine learning model, and the way that it works is that it processes information
in a way that's similar to how neurons work in the human brain.
So in our brains, neurons communicate by passing information to other neurons.
And in our neural network model, we're going to be doing the same.
We're going to be passing along data from neuron to neuron or in technical speak, node to node.
Now, the way that a neural network is structured isn't exactly the way that neurons work in our brain.
Keep that in mind.
It's just inspired by them.
But from a mathematical perspective, we've organized these neurons or these nodes into layers.
And these layers give us a nice, structured way of passing data from one set of nodes to the next.
So typically when you see a visualization of a neural network, it'll start with an input layer of nodes.
These are your features or what you would input into a model.
We'll talk about that a lot more in the next few lessons.
And then data from that input layer is passed to a hidden layer.
You can see this is another set of nodes.
And in this layer there are a lot of calculations happening.
We'll be talking about things like parameters and activation functions in just a bit.
And then again that information is passed along to another set of nodes.
And this is the output layer.
And this is where your final predictions are made.
So again data starts from the left here.
And then we pass data from all the input nodes to this first hidden node here.
And we do that same thing with all the other nodes.
And then from that hidden layer we do the same thing to the next layer.
We pass along information from all the nodes to that first output node as well as all the others.
Now this is a pretty simple neural network.
We just have one hidden layer here, but you can have much more.
And throughout this section we're going to work up to that.
So we're going to start with just a few nodes and then continue to add more nodes and layers once you
understand the foundations.
```

### Logistic Regression Refresher {#logistic-regression-refresher}

```
To understand how neural networks work.
I like to start by talking about a logistic regression model.
Now you may be wondering a logistic regression is a classification technique.
So why are we using it to describe a neural network.
And the reason is because a neural network can be used as a classification technique as well.
With both these techniques, at the end of the day your goal is to predict a true or false outcome.
Now, if you're not familiar with logistic regressions, that's fine.
At this point, all you need to know is that it's a really popular and solid classification algorithm,
and it can answer questions like this based on today's temperature.
Will my lemonade stand be profitable today?
Now we're going to answer this question using a logistic regression.
And we're going to break it down in a visual way while keeping the math in mind as well, because that
math is really important for understanding a neural network.
Okay.
Let's say we're trying to answer this question and we fit a logistic regression model.
The output would look something like this.
So you can see on the x axis we have today's temperature And then the y axis is our probability of profit.
So you can see the higher the temperature today, the more likely my lemonade stand will be profitable.
Now this s shape here is the classic shape of a logistic regression.
When you see a curve that ranges between 0 and 1 that looks like this, you know that this is a logistic
regression model.
Now from here, let's dive into some of the math.
The equation for this logistic regression model is this here.
If this looks foreign to you at the moment don't worry.
We're going to be breaking down each piece of this formula within this lesson.
So by the end you'll know what each piece means.
But at the moment just know that this is the formula for that's curve on the left.
The two pieces of the visualization that you can see in the formula here are the X piece that represents
today's temperature.
And then the piece which represents the probability of profit.
So the main idea I want you to take away here is whatever is on the x axis.
That's what we're inputting into our model which is today's temperature.
And then on the y axis That's what we get as an output which is represented by the P here.
So again the way to read this formula is we're making a calculation on the right.
And that's going to result in the output on the left.
So using this model we could input a temperature of 15 degrees.
Put that into the formula on the right.
And then that would give us a probability of 22%.
That would tell us if today's temperature is just 15 degrees, there's only a 22% chance we're going
to be profitable.
And that makes sense, because that's pretty cold to be buying lemonade.
Now, if it were a little warmer, let's say 25 degrees, then the probability would be 78%, which
means there's a good chance we're going to be making money.
And finally, if it's 35 degrees, it's almost certain that we're going to be profitable.
And again, all these numbers here are coming from that formula at the top right there.
And they also match the visualization on the left.
So just imagine on the y axis you're at that 25 tick.
If you move all the way up to the curve you're going to hit about 78%.
So at this point you should have a good idea of what an input and an output to a model looks like.
It's that x and that P there.
Now your next question is probably how do we get all these other values.
What are sigma m and b.
And that's a great question because that's what we're going to be talking about next.
So what we're going to do at this point is start with our X values.
Because those are our inputs.
And we're going to be working our way up to get to that final output or probability.
And for logistic regression this takes a two step approach.
So the first thing we're going to do here is start with our temperature on our x axis.
And now instead of jumping straight to that probability we want at the end we're actually going to take
an intermediate step.
We're going to map these temperatures to a y value.
And the way we're going to do that is by using a line.
Now the formula for the line is y equals mx plus b.
This should look familiar to you if you've done any high school math.
This is the generic formula for a line.
And once again on the right here's our input which is today's temperature.
And then on the left here is our output, which in this case is a variable we're calling y.
And then as you know from this formula we have our slope and our intercept.
So now let's add some numbers to this to represent the line on the left.
Now at this point you're probably thinking how do we get these numbers.
And the answer is that when we fit a logistic regression model within scikit learn, it gives us this
MX value.
We'll be talking about that at a later point.
But for the time being just assume that we know these numbers.
Okay.
Going back to this formula, our goal at this step is to map our X values or our temperature to this
y output.
So if we were to use this formula for this first data point, we would get a y of -1.25.
And you can map that on your own on the left as well.
If you're on the x axis at the 15 tick and you go down to that line, you'll see that y is -1.25.
And you can do this for the others as well.
For the second data, point Y is 1.25 and then it's 3.75.
Now, another question you might have right now is what does Y actually represent?
And in technical terms it's something called log odds.
But practically it's a hard concept to understand.
So instead of focusing on that which isn't too important at the moment, let's move on to the next step,
which will be a lot easier to interpret.
So now what we're going to do is we're going to use this y value as an input into our next step.
So on the left as usual our inputs are on the x axis.
So this time we're inputting in these y values.
And then what we're going to output is a probability of profit.
And this visual here would look like an S curve.
So the formula for this looks like this.
You can see in this case we're inputting this y over here.
And we're getting this probability as our output.
And this new piece here is this sigma function.
So what this sigma function does is it takes that line from the last slide where the range of values
was between negative infinity and positive infinity.
And it squishes it to become this S and now this S curve.
Instead of having values that range between negative infinity and positive infinity, all these probability
values you see here are mapped to be between 0 and 1.
So to put it simply, the whole point of the sigmoid function is to take a line and squash it into an
s and more technical terms.
It takes that line and it transforms it into an s.
Now, the reason you would want to do this is because it makes our outputs a lot more interpretable.
So those Y values earlier or the log odds are hard to interpret.
But once you put it on this S curve here, this probability output is much easier for a human to understand.
So let's make some calculations for this first data point.
We started with a temperature of 15.
That gave us a y of -1.25.
And by running it through this formula we get a probability of 22%.
So if you look at the x axis here, which we've called y when y is equal to -1.25, if you map that
up to the curve, you'll see that the probability output is roughly 22%.
Now let's do that for the others as well.
This middle data point the probability ends up being 78%.
And then finally for this last data point, the probability ends up being 98%, which is really close
to one.
And these outputs here are much more interpretable than the y values we originally input.
Now the next question you probably have is what is the sigmoid calculation?
And to answer that it looks like this.
I know it looks like a lot, but you know every piece of this formula.
Now this purple piece here is the sigmoid calculation.
And then this teal piece here is our y.
So let me just back up a bit to our prior calculation.
Remember here we set p equal to sigma of y.
And we can expand out both that sigma and that y value to this formula here.
Again this is our sigma calculation.
And in here that's our y calculation.
Now with this calculation let's create our visualization again.
If you look at the formula on the right.
The input here is x.
So we're back to x here as our input which is today's temperature.
And then all that is getting mapped to this probability on the left.
And if we were to make this calculation at the end we would get this S curve.
And this is the logistic regression visualization that we started with.
Now one more thing I want to do is just simplify the formula on the right there.
Typically the purple part here isn't written out.
It's just represented as a sigmoid.
But that teal portion is written out.
So if we update that we get back to this formula, which is the formula we introduced at the beginning
of this lesson.
Now let's summarize the steps that we took to get here.
The first thing we did was we started with our x input for today's temperature.
And then with that we applied a linear transformation.
So by adding this slope and intercept we map that from one set of x values to another set of y values
using a line.
And then after that we took that linear transformation.
Formation.
And with that output we applied a nonlinear transformation.
So we took that original line and then we squished it into an S.
That's nonlinear because this S here isn't a line.
And by taking these two steps we were able to start with our original X values, which was today's temperature.
And then with that linear transformation we map the x values to these y values.
And then with the nonlinear transformation we map those y values to these probabilities.
Now you're probably thinking why are you talking about these steps so much.
Why are they so important?
And the reason is because these steps are the main steps of a neural network.
For each node of a neural network, we're going to be doing a linear transformation followed by a nonlinear
transformation.
And these two steps are done again and again for an entire neural network.
So once you understand the general idea behind this two step process, you understand the basis for
how a neural network works.
And with that, let's use everything that we've learned here to talk about how a neural network works.
```

### Logistic Regression: Visually Explained {#logistic-regression-visually-explained}

```
In this lesson, we'll be going through a visual introduction to neural networks.
In the last lesson, we talked about how a logistic regression model works.
And in this lesson we're going to visually represent that logistic regression as a series of nodes.
Because the point that I want to make here is that a logistic regression is essentially a very simple
neural network.
So let's start with our example again based on today's temperature, will my lemonade stand be profitable
today.
And what we're going to do is create a logistic regression model to answer this question.
So for that logistic regression we're going to start with today's temperature which is what we're inputting
into our model.
And then we're going to make some calculations.
In the last lesson we talked about how that data first went through a linear transformation and then
a nonlinear one to get to this S curve.
And then after going through all those transformations at the end, we're going to get an output.
And that output is our probability of profit, which is the question we're trying to answer.
Will my lemonade stand be profitable today.
So again, what we've done is we've taken our logistic regression from the last lesson, and we've represented
it with circles and arrows here.
Now this may take a moment for you to wrap your head around, because this is an unusual way to represent
a logistic regression.
However, it should make a lot of sense.
Each circle here is capturing a different step of our modeling process.
But the main point here is that these circles represent the nodes of a neural network.
And each step here is a different layer of a neural network.
So here is our input layer.
That's what we're inputting into our model.
This is the hidden layer.
That's where the calculations are being made.
And finally we have our output layer.
And that's where we're making a prediction.
And all these steps here map to this formula that we covered in the last lesson.
So this input here sometimes we call this our features.
And this is represented in this formula with this x.
This is what we're inputting into our model which in this case is today's temperature.
And then this next step here consists of all our calculations.
There's a lot going on here which includes things like parameters and activation functions.
So let's define those here.
These two pieces here this w and this b.
In the last lesson we talked about how this is the formula for a line with W representing the slope
and B representing the intercept.
When we're talking about neural networks though these terms are a little bit different.
The W going forward we're going to call that the weight.
And then the b we're going to call the bias.
And together they're called the model parameters.
So once again we've started with our input or our x or multiplying it by a weight and adding a bias.
And with those parameters we're applying a linear transformation to our input.
Now the next transformation we're applying is the sigmoid function.
And like we talked about in the last lesson this is the type of nonlinear transformation.
This is what turns a line into that's curve but a neural network speak.
This is what we would call an activation function.
So once again to summarize what's happening in this hidden layer we're starting with our x input.
We're applying a linear transformation to that by using the w and b parameters.
And then we're applying a nonlinear transformation to that by using the sigmoid activation function.
And at the very end, once we make all those calculations, we end up with our final prediction, which
is this p here.
And this tells us based on today's temperature, will my lemonade stand be profitable today?
Now, if everything here feels like a really big jump for you, I recommend you go back and rewatch
the logistic regression lesson to get a good grasp of this linear and nonlinear transformation, because
we're going to be using these two steps again and again throughout this lesson.
But if this roughly makes sense to you at a high level, then I would recommend you keep on pushing
through and just know that all this takes a bit of time to digest.
Personally, I had to learn these concepts several times from multiple angles to truly understand how
they work.
So with all that in mind, let's move on to the next step.
So far we've just input one feature or one input which is today's temperature.
But today's temperature can't be the only thing that affects profitability.
So let's include some more inputs.
In this case we're going to add a weekend input.
So looking at today's temperature as well as if today's a weekend or a weekday, will my lemonade stand
be profitable today.
And once again this is my input layer.
These are the features I'm inputting into my model.
Here's our hidden layer where all the calculations happen.
And finally we're going to get a prediction in the output layer.
Now because we have two inputs into the hidden layer we're going to add some labels here.
These two variables stand for weight one and weight two.
Okay.
Now that we've updated our visualization let's update our formula as well.
So this formula here is giving me the output for h.
And h is the output of this hidden node.
Here you can see in this formula here we're or taking two inputs, x1 and x2.
And for both of those inputs, we're going to multiply them with a weight.
It's going to be weight one for x1 or today's temperature and weight two for x2, which is our weekend
feature.
Now, instead of just looking at the variable names, let's add some numbers to this.
So once again you might be thinking how do we get these numbers?
And the answer as usual is we get them from fitting a model.
We'll talk about that in later lessons in this section.
But for the time being let's just say we have these numbers and we're ready to make some calculations.
So for this first example we're inputting two features into our model.
This is saying if today's temperature is 15 degrees and today is not a weekend that's what the zero
represents.
Then the output of this formula is 22%.
If it's cold and it's a weekday, our lemonade stand doesn't have a good chance of being profitable.
Let's feed in another example.
Let's say it's cold today, but it is a weekend which is represented by that one there.
In that case, our calculation would tell us we have a better chance of being profitable, but it's
still pretty low.
Now let's look at the situations where today's temperature is a bit more moderate.
And then for that first case, it's not a weekend.
And the second case it is a weekend.
In these situations here you can see our outputs are a lot higher, meaning that there's a good chance
we're going to be profitable.
And then if it's really hot then whether it's not a weekend or it is a weekend, we're probably going
to be profitable.
Okay, so to summarize this example, in this case we fit a logistic regression model.
But instead of having one input feature which was temperature, we had two input features temperature
and weekend.
However coming up with these features is hard to do.
So instead of coming up with features on our own, let's have an algorithm do this for us.
And to do that, we are going to add more hidden nodes to this model, to change it from a logistic
regression to a true neural network.
```

### Neural Networks: Visually Explained {#neural-networks-visually-explained}

```
In this lesson, let's add more nodes to our example for you to get a better idea of how a true neural
network works.
In this example here, we're just going to be adding one node so you can get an idea of the formulas.
But in practice it's up to you as a data scientist to specify the number of nodes in the hidden layer,
as well as what activation function you want to be using for each node.
Now with that, let's add another node.
You can see this node here.
It's getting inputs from both temperature and weekend.
And then we're going to take that output and pass that along to our final probability prediction.
So once again we have our input layer our hidden layer and our output layer.
But in this case our hidden layer has two nodes.
So let's walk through the formulas for this.
One thing to keep in mind here is that even though there will be a lot of formulas, the structure of
everything is the same.
We've gone through the steps multiple times now, and we're going to go through those exact same steps,
but just more times in this case.
So here's our formula once again for that top node.
This is the calculation we did on the last slide.
Once again, if you look at the visualization on the left, let's label these two w1 and w2.
These are the two inputs into this top node here.
And then the output of this node we're going to call H1.
And now for the second hidden node we're going to make a very similar calculation.
The formula is going to look like this.
And if you look to the left there once again you'll see we've added weights here.
These are the two weights that are going into that second node.
And the output is what we're going to call H2.
Now when we do this both H1 and H2 become different combinations of the features or of the inputs.
So H1 is some combination of temperature and weekend.
And H2 is a different combination of temperature and weekend.
And what's really cool about this step is that H1 and H2 essentially become new features.
And these are features that the model discovered.
So for example, maybe that first feature represents the first level and then the second one represents
foot traffic.
So for the first level, maybe if it's a hot day and you're doing a lot of activities on the weekend,
you're going to be really thirsty.
And then for foot traffic, If it's a hot day and people aren't working on the weekend, there's going
to be more foot traffic now.
Thirst level and foot traffic both incorporate temperature and weekend, but they do so in different
ways.
And that's captured by these weights here.
Thirst level probably has more to do with temperature.
So weight one will likely be higher than weight two.
And then foot traffic probably has more to do with weekend.
So weight for would likely be higher than weight three.
But the main takeaway of all of this is that the algorithm was able to come up with these features for
us, and for this example, we were able to interpret them.
But in practice, they're less interpretable and not typically something you would do.
However, I do think it's worthwhile to mention this because it shows that each hidden node is capturing
that input information in a slightly different way.
Now we're almost at the end here.
Now that we have the calculations for H1 and H2, we can combine those calculations.
And we're going to do so using the exact same approach as these other hidden nodes.
We're going to take it through a linear transformation followed by a non-linear one.
So because that process is exactly the same The structure of the formula will be exactly the same as
well.
The only difference is that our parameters will be different.
And you could potentially switch up the activation function as well.
But in our case here this is the formula for the final probability.
You can see it's structured in the exact same way.
But what's different now is that the inputs are H1 and H2.
And then if you look at the left here we're incorporating these two new weights.
And as usual we add a bias to the end there.
And these weights and biases here are the parameters that are associated with this node.
And as usual we surround that with an activation function which for a classification problem like this
will be that sigmoid activation function.
And that will return to us our final probability.
So just to reiterate that one more time, we've taken the outputs from the hidden nodes.
And those are essentially our new inputs for this output layer.
And just like before we're going to take a linear combination of those parameters and then take that
final output through an activation function.
And at the end of all that we get a final prediction.
So just like we did at the beginning of this lesson with a logistic regression, we can map our input
data to predictions.
So if it's cold today and it's not a weekend, then with this new neural network model, it's telling
us that the probability that we're going to be profitable today is only 15%.
If it's cold today and it is a weekend, our probability is much higher.
And then if the temperature is moderate, if it's not a weekend, our probability is a little bit higher.
And if it is a weekend our probability is much higher.
And finally if it's a hot day then our probability is the highest.
And just to drive this point home, these probability calculations are coming from this neural network
here with two nodes in the hidden layer.
Versus at the beginning of this lesson we were making this calculation with a logistic regression.
And you can see for these two cases they both output slightly different probabilities.
And to determine which one is better you would use classification metrics just like you would do for
any other classification problem.
You would compare the predicted values with the actual values to see which model is better performing.
Okay, we've gone through a lot here, so let's summarize everything that we've learned with a few bullet
points.
```

### Neural Network Summary {#neural-network-summary}

```
Let's summarize everything we've learned so far about neural networks.
First, a neural network processes data through layers of nodes.
We start with an input layer.
Then you can have one or more hidden layers.
And finally we have our output layer.
In terms of how many layers and nodes you choose, that's something that's up to you as a data scientist
to decide on.
And then in terms of actually making the calculations, the same two step calculation is done at every
node.
The first step is to calculate a weighted sum.
So for every input you're going to multiply the input by a weight.
Add all those values together and add a bias.
And then all those weights and biases are called your parameters.
Once you've made that calculation, the next step is to apply a nonlinear transformation to that result.
And that transformation is called an activation function.
And for all of our examples in this section so far we've been using the sigmoid activation function.
The next thing to remember is that neural networks are just another approach to supervised learning.
Earlier on in this course we talked about text Classification and how.
For classification problem, you need to input in historical data that's labeled in some way.
And then as an output you get a prediction.
And we've done that same thing here with our neural network example.
We input in historical information about temperature and the day of the week.
We put that through our neural network.
And at the end it told us the probability that we were going to be profitable.
Coming up in just a few lessons, we'll also be going through a Python demo of how you can fit a neural
network using scikit learn, and you'll see it follows the exact same process as the classification
problem that we worked on earlier in this course.
So the main takeaway for this bullet here is that neural networks are another supervised learning technique.
So if you're trying to make a prediction you can use neural networks instead of logistic regression
or instead of naive Bayes, which are both algorithms that we talked about earlier.
Now, while you can do this, you probably don't want to due to the complexity.
And that's something we'll talk more about within the Python demo.
And finally, the last question you have at this point is probably how do you determine the parameters?
We've been talking about all these weights and biases.
And so far in all of these lessons I've just said, we'll talk about how to get those soon.
But for the time being just ignore those.
But now that we understand the whole structure of a neural network, we can actually get into this.
So how do we determine the parameters for a neural network model?
If we were doing this in Python, we would just do a dot fit using scikit learn.
And that would give us all those weights and biases.
This is something you'll see in the Python demo coming up real soon.
And then in theory there's a more involved training process here.
What happens is we start with random parameter values, and then they slowly get adjusted until we reach
our final ones.
And this is something we'll be talking about later on in this section when we go over how a neural network
is trained.
So at this point we've gone over a lot.
I hope you take a quick break to let all of that new information really sink into your brain.
And when you come back, we're going to go through an exercise to help you solidify these new concepts
that you just learned.
```

```
And then the final output here would be this h value.

And if you look over here we have one input layer two hidden layers and an output layer which means
we have four layers.
And that answers the next question as well which is how many hidden layers.
And those are these two gray layers here.
So this is two.

So remember all these circles are different node.
So we have three plus six plus four plus one which equals 14.
And then the next question is on weights.

And the answer to that is parameters are a combination of weights and biases.

function for each calculation.
And each calculation is happening at one of these hidden or output nodes.
So there's one activation function associated with each of these nodes here.
And that ends up being six plus four plus one.
So we end up with 11 total activation functions.
And once again the calculation that we're making at each node is this linear combination followed by
this nonlinear transformation.
And those are all the main neural network concepts or the main components of a neural network.
```

### Neural Networks in Python {#neural-networks-in-python}

```
Now that you understand the main components of a neural network, let's create a neural network within
Python to do so.
We can use either Mlpclassifier for classification problems or MLP regressor for regression problems.
Remember, classification is where you predict a categorical value and regression is when you predict
a continuous value.
As usual, we'll be doing this using scikit learn.
But first of all, I want to point out that MLP acronym that stands for Multi-layer Perceptron, which
is just another name for a neural network.
A perceptron was a simpler math technique that was invented in the 1950s, and it consisted of taking
a weighted sum followed by a non-linear transformation, which is what we've been doing again and again
within our neural network nodes.
The multi-layer piece builds on the original perceptron and allows for hidden layers.
So when you have multiple layers of weighted sums that are transformed, that's just a neural network.
So here's what the code would look like.
And you can see it's very similar to what we've been doing before.
You can see we're importing MLP classifier and then we're creating a new object.
And inside here there are two Python parameters.
First the hidden layer sizes.
And this defines the number of nodes in each hidden layer.
So by default the model will include one hidden layer with 100 nodes.
And that's represented by this 100 comma.
Nothing here.
If you want two layers you can specify two values with the exact number of nodes you want in those layers.
And from there you can specify more hidden layers as well.
In addition, you need to specify an activation function.
Remember that's the nonlinear transformation you're applying at each node.
And the default option is something called ReLU, which you'll see a visual of in just a moment.
But you can also see logistic there, which is the same thing as sigmoid transformation and also tangent
and identity.
Now to visualize some of these activation functions.
First of all, this is what ReLU looks like.
And it stands for rectified linear unit.
Basically if you pass a line through this, it will replace all negative values with a zero and then
keep all the positive values unchanged.
And these were the other options you could choose from.
Identity essentially means no change.
It just keeps that negative infinity to infinity value.
Logistic is the one we've been talking about so far.
It's also called the sigmoid function.
And it squeezes all your values between 0 and 1.
And then tanj is the other one here.
And it puts all your values between negative one and positive one.
Now in terms of which one is best, this is something that you will play around with.
It's just another Python parameter that you can tune to see which one gives you the best predictions.
Now, earlier I mentioned that within a neural network, you can specify the activation function for
each node or each layer, but that's not the case with Mlpclassifier.
This allows you to build a very simple neural network, and this ends up being your activation function
for all the hidden layers in your neural network.
If you want to fine tune this more, you would have to use a library like PyTorch or TensorFlow to do
so.
In addition, you may be wondering about that activation function in the final layer in the output layer.
And that's defined by whether you're using MLP classifier or MLP regressor.
In the classification situation, if you're predicting a one or a zero or a binary output, it would
automatically choose a sigmoid activation function for you.
If you have a multi-class problem where you're predicting more than two values, then in that case it
would default to something called a softmax transformation.
What that does is it makes all your output probabilities add up to one.
And finally, for a regression problem, there would be no transformation at the end because you just
want that continuous output from negative infinity to positive infinity.
Now, once you've decided on your hidden layers and activation functions, you can just use Mlpclassifier
like any other machine learning algorithm with scikit learn.
In this code block, we're following the typical scikit learn process for a supervised learning model
or for making a prediction.
So once again, we've imported Mlpclassifier.
We're instantiating an object or creating a new model.
We're setting our hidden layer sizes, our activation function, and then these other two inputs here
max iteration and Randomstate.
We'll be talking about this more in the next lessons when we cover neural network training.
Before the time being, just know that every time you create a new neural network model, it starts
with a random set of values.
That's why we're setting a random state here so we can see the same output every time.
And then this iteration piece is the number of updates those random values have to go through to get
to the final parameters.
But you can see at a high level we've instantiated a new object.
And then the next steps should look very familiar.
We're first doing a dot fit and we're inputting some data, our inputs as well as our labels.
And then after that we do a dot predict to make a prediction.
Now at this point you may be looking at this code and thinking, okay, great.
Neural networks are just another model in my toolbox that I can use when I want to make predictions.
But I would say don't fully do that just yet.
Because in practice, neural networks technically could be used for classification and regression problems,
but they're typically not.
And the reason for this is because it's often too much of a black box.
We don't really know what's happening at each of those hidden nodes.
And this is a problem because a lot of the times with machine learning, we're interested in more interpretable
models like logistic regression or linear regression, where we can more easily understand the relationship
between the inputs and output.
That said, even though neural networks aren't commonly used on their own, they are used all the time
as a building block for more complex techniques.
And we'll be talking about this in a lot more detail in the deep learning architectures lesson.
So this is something to keep in mind as we're going through the Python code and the demo.
Coming up next, we'll be focusing less on the syntax and more on being able to understand what the
outputs mean, because that's the main piece that matters when it comes to neural networks.
```

### PRO TIP: NN Notation & Matrices {#pro-tip-nn-notation-matrices}

```
This is a pro tip lesson.
A neural network notation and matrices.
What we'll be covering here is an essential to your neural network understanding.
But it's nice to know if you continue to learn about neural networks after this course.
Now in the previous lesson when we did the Python demo, we saw an example of how the weights and biases
of a neural network can be captured within matrices and vectors.
And this is an important piece to remember when we talk about transformers, because once we get to
those more complex architectures, we won't be viewing neural networks as visual nodes anymore.
But instead everything's going to be captured in matrices.
So if we go back to our original example where we had our input layer with two inputs, our hidden layer
with two hidden nodes and an output.
Sometimes when you're reading papers on neural networks, you'll see this arrow represented by this
W with all these numbers next to it.
So let's break down what all those numbers mean.
The way to read this notation is that this arrow here represents a weight.
And for the more complex architectures, you might have millions of weights that you're working with.
So all those numbers next to it help us better keep track of our weights.
The first number refers to the starting node.
The second number refers to the ending node, and the number at the top refers to the specific weight
matrix.
So in this case we're starting from x1, which is where that first one on the bottom left is coming
from.
We're going to H1 which is the ending node or the number on the bottom right.
And this weight is captured in our first weight matrix, which we'll see in just a moment.
But before we do that, in the visualization on the left, I'm going to add two more parameters.
You can see on the bottom there.
W21.
That information is coming from the second input node and going to the first hidden node.
And then those two arrows are combined with that B right there.
And that gives us this equation.
So while it looks like a lot at this point, you can just ignore the ones in the parentheses at the
top right.
And if you just look at the W's and B's you can see that first W represents that top arrow.
The second W represents the arrow going from the bottom left to the top middle.
And then finally you have your bias.
So now what we're going to do here is put in actual values.
So the 0.30.05 and the -6.5 represent that w, w and B on the left.
And now from here we can add more weights to our visualization.
So if you look at the left there we're going to add these weights here.
And this is all about that second hidden node.
You can see it's getting two inputs w1 to two and w2 to two.
And then we also have our second bias term B2.
And that creates this equation here.
And now at this point these two formulas represent all the arrows from the input layer to the hidden
layer.
And these parameters can be captured in a more concise format which is this matrix here.
So the way to read this is that every row here represents an input.
And then every column represents a hidden node.
So let's first just look at the information for that first column or for the first hidden node.
That point three and the .05 correspond to the two values in the H1 formula on the right 0.3 and 0.05.
And then that bias term is captured in another vector.
So you can see the -0.65 corresponds to that final bias.
And then if we want to look at what's happening at the second hidden node, we look at that second column,
the 0.082.5 and -1.8.
And those three numbers correspond to that second formula on the right.
Now at this point, you'll notice that all the parameters in the visualization on the left have that
one in parentheses in the top right.
Because once again that stands for the weight matrix.
And you can see in this case all these numbers are represented in our first weight matrix.
Or in other words our first set of connections.
And this is what we saw in the Python demo just before this as well.
Now, one side note I'd like to mention here is that you may have noticed that I said weight matrix
when I really meant weight matrix and bias vector.
It's very common for people in this space to just refer to the weights when they really mean both weights
and biases.
So again, within this space, the terms weights and parameters are often used interchangeably, even
though we know that they're not exactly the same.
Parameters include both weights and biases.
So that's just something to be aware of both inside and outside of this course.
Now we're not fully done here because all these weights and biases represent that first group of connections.
And then we're going to have a second set of connections with another weight matrix and another bias
vector to represent the next group of arrows.
So if you look at the visualization at the top left we're going to add these weights and biases.
And you can see at the top right of all these terms there is a two there.
Because we're now looking at the second set of connections or our second weight matrix.
Now, if we were to put all those parameters into a formula, it would look like this.
And then the corresponding weight matrix and bias vector would look like this.
Remember each column here represents a different layer.
And this third layer here represents the calculation at the output layer.
So we're multiplying those two inputs from the hidden nodes by 3.5 and three.
And then at the end we're subtracting 3.2 which is our bias.
So the main takeaway here is that up till now we've been mainly looking at this node visualization as
well as these formulas.
But in practice all this information is captured within these matrices.
And if ever these matrices are hard for you to interpret, you can always change them into the formulas
here to better understand how the calculations are being made.
Throughout this course, we're going to be using simpler notation, but this is the more technically
accurate notation that you'll see in other neural network literature.
```

### How a Neural Network is Trained {#how-a-neural-network-is-trained}

```
Now that you fit a neural network within Python, let's talk about how that fitting is actually happening.
This process of fitting a neural network or finding the parameters for a neural network is called training
a neural network.
So again, when we talk about training, we're talking about finding the weights and biases of a neural
network.
And this is done using a five step process.
The first step is a random start.
So after you've decided on the structure of your neural network, including how many hidden layers and
nodes you want, then you need to figure out what all the weights should be.
And surprisingly, all those weights start out random.
Now, after we've set our random weights and biases, the next step is called a forward pass.
So the idea here is that you input in some data.
You send it through your neural network from left to right, and you apply all the calculations.
So all those linear transformations with the weighted sums as well as the non-linear transformations
with the activation functions.
And once you do that, at the very end, you get a set of predicted values for your input data.
And from there you're going to calculate a loss.
So this is where you compare your predicted values that you just came up with from that forward pass
with the actual values in your data.
And then you find the difference between the two.
This is sometimes called error or loss.
And then the next step is the most mathematically intensive one.
And this is the step where we update the parameters.
So remember we started with those random weights and biases at this step.
What happens is those weights and biases get moved one step closer to the optimal weights and biases.
And this is done using two concepts.
One is called back propagation and another one is called gradient descent.
I bring up these terms here because they're commonly associated with neural networks.
That being the case, we're not going to go into the detailed math.
There's a lot of calculus involved.
And that's beyond the scope of this course.
But the main takeaway is you start with random parameters and then with backpropagation.
That allows you to calculate how much each parameter contributed to that final loss.
And the reason it's called backpropagation is because it's calculated from the output layer to the input
layer.
So from right to left.
And then once you make all those calculations, you end up with something called gradients.
And those gradients help you decide what direction to go in space to minimize your loss.
So this gradient descent term right here, what it does is it takes those random parameters and it moves
them one step closer in the right direction.
And by right direction I mean the optimal direction.
And by the optimal direction I mean one step closer to minimizing the loss.
Now I know that was a lot of information right there, but if you were to take one sentence away from
what I just said, you can just read that bottom part there that's highlighted in yellow.
Basically what's happening at this step is we're updating the parameters in the right direction.
We also mentioned here that it does so as efficiently as possible because using gradient descent, it's
going to guide us in that right direction instead of just testing out a bunch of random parameters each
time.
Okay.
So this fourth step was updating parameters.
And then the last step is just to repeat this process.
So after step four you would have a new set of parameters.
Then you go through the forward pass and you make all your calculations.
You calculate the loss or the error.
And then you go back and you update your parameters.
And you do that again and again until you minimize the loss.
So essentially you have the best fitting model.
Another stopping point would be if you've gone through this back and forth process X number of times,
and then you don't want to spend any more time training, then you can just stop your training there
as well.
So that is a high level overview of how you would train a neural network.
Now in typical Maven fashion, let's go through an example of this visually.
```
