### SOLUTION: Neural Network Training {#solution-neural-network-training}

```
The first thing we need to do is place these steps in order.
So when we train a neural network, the first step is to start with random parameters.
And then from there we're going to do a forward pass.
And remember the idea of a forward pass is you put in your input data and pass it through all the formulas
that include all those random parameters.
And at the very end, you get predictions for each row of data.
Now the next step is making a loss calculation.
And the idea here is you compare your predicted values with your actual values.
And you come up with a loss metric.
And that loss metric is what you want to optimize.
You want to minimize that loss to get to your final parameters.
Now one more thing I want to mention here is that there are many different types of loss calculations.
The one we went through in our example was called log loss.
And that's often used for a binary classification problem when you're trying to predict a one or a zero.
Sometimes you'll hear that called cross entropy.
And then other loss calculations include things like mean squared error, which is used for regression
problems.
The next step is to use this loss calculation to adjust the model's parameters.
And this is done by using a combination of back propagation and gradient descent.
Again, back propagation produces gradients and gradients tell us how much each parameter contributes
to the final error, as well as how to change those parameters to reduce the error.
And from there, once you calculate those gradients, you can use something called gradient descent
to update the parameters.
So instead of updating the parameters in any direction, you would update them in a smart direction.
In gradient descent just means taking a step in the right direction to lessen the loss.
Now, gradient descent is one of many optimization techniques out there.
It's usually the first one that you learn when you're learning about training.
But there's also variations of it.
Stochastic gradient descent is a popular one, and it's basically a faster gradient descent, but the
path to get to your optimal parameters is a bit more noisy.
Another popular optimizer is the Adam optimizer, and that's one you'll often see when working in Python.
In any case, if we move past those technical details, the main idea here is that we're updating the
parameters from a random set of values to something that's closer to the optimal values.
And now these steps two, three, and four are done again and again and again.
And then your parameters become more fine tuned.
And finally you end up with your optimal parameters.
And those are the weights and biases in your final neural network model that you can then use to make
predictions.
And that's the high level training process for a neural network.
Now let's answer a few questions over here as well.
First, what do you need to specify before training.
Now, if you think back to our neural network training lesson, there were two main things on the slides.
First, we had the neural network structure.
And this is something that you have to specify as a data scientist.
It's up to you to decide the number of layers and nodes, and also the specific activation functions
you want to use.
Remember you can have more than one hidden layer.
And then in our example we specified two nodes in the hidden layer.
But in Python we specified 100 nodes.
And then also for activation functions we've been using the sigmoid function.
But as we talked about in the Python lesson.
Some other popular ones are ReLU, tangent and so on.
Now in addition to the structure, when you are training a model you also have to provide data.
And because we're using these neural networks to make predictions this is a supervised learning problem.
So we need to provide both x inputs as well as y labels which is what we did in our example.
Our inputs were temperature and the day of the week, and then our Y or our label was historical data
on how profitable we were.
So if we were profitable, that would be given a one, and if not, that would be given a zero.
So at a high level, those are the things you need to specify before training.
We'll talk about a few more details when we get to the last question for this exercise.
The next question here is what do you get at the end of training a neural network?
And the answer to this is you get a set of final parameters, which are all the weights and biases in
your model.
And with those weights and biases, if you see a new data point, you can make predictions for that
data point by inputting data into your model with those finalized parameters going through all the calculations
at each node with that linear transformation followed by the activation function or that nonlinear transformation
again and again until you get to your final prediction.
And our last question here is how do you train a neural network model in practice?
And the answer is first that you use Python.
In this section we demoed how you would use scikit learn to fit a neural network model.
But scikit learn is only used for simple neural networks.
In practice, you would use a more powerful library like PyTorch or Keras.
And then within those libraries, if you're training a neural network, there are so many more parameters
to tune.
Now, when I say parameters here, I specifically mean Python parameters and not neural network parameters.
Once again, neural network parameters refer to the weights and biases, whereas for Python parameters,
that's where you specify all the details within Python of how many nodes you want, what activation
function you want to use, and so on.
So when you're training a neural network using PyTorch or Keras, again, the first thing you have to
specify is the structure of your neural network.
How many layers do you want?
How many nodes do you want?
What activation functions do you want to use?
And then there are a few more details you want to specify a loss function and what loss you choose will
depend on what type of problem you're trying to solve.
Remember we talked about log loss or cross-entropy for classification problems.
There's also mean squared error for regression problems.
You also need to specify an optimizer.
And we talked about gradient descent as well as stochastic gradient descent and Adam.
Those are all different optimization techniques that will take your initial random set of parameters
and adjust them until you get to your final set of parameters.
There are so many more details.
I've just listed a few more terms here, so I'll touch on them really quickly and you can dive into
this more outside of this course.
When you train a model, you want to specify your epochs, and that's the number of times you pass through
your entire data set.
There's also batch sizes, and that's how many rows you would look at to calculate gradients.
There's something called learning rate, and that's the size of the step you're taking every time you're
making parameter adjustments, and so much more.
If this is something you're interested in, you now have all the base knowledge to go out and explore
more on your own.
But for the purposes of this course, the main takeaway for this neural network training section is
that when you do a dot fit within Python, what's happening is you start your neural network with random
parameters and they slowly get adjusted to your final optimal parameters that get locked into that model.
And in the future, anytime you want to make a prediction, you don't have to go through this whole
training process again.
All you have to do is look at the model's final parameters and make all the calculations to make your
prediction.
And this is a really important point to keep in mind when we talk about pre-trained models later on
in this course.
And with that, we are done talking about neural networks, and we're ready to move on to deep learning.
```

### Intro to Deep Learning {#intro-to-deep-learning}

```
We're now moving from neural networks to deep learning.
But since you already have all the neural network foundations, this shouldn't be a big jump at all.
When people refer to deep learning, they're just referring to a neural network with three or more hidden
layers.
Now, the reason that deep learning has become so popular is because it's revolutionized the field of
AI.
So things like NLP, computer vision, and speech recognition in the last decade or so.
It's leveled up all these fields from producing okay results to great results.
But the math behind the basic deep learning concepts isn't anything new.
The things we've talked about in this section already, such as logistic regression and the perceptron,
which is a single layer neural network.
Those have been around since the 1950s.
It's really the computational power of the 20 tens that's allowed deep learning to become so powerful
and widespread.
So down here, let's compare neural networks and deep learning side by side.
This is the neural network that we're very familiar with at this point.
And you can see a deep learning model uses the same idea except it has more hidden layers.
But all the calculations are the same at every node.
We're taking a weighted sum And then we're doing a nonlinear transformation on that weighted sum and
passing that to the next node.
Now in terms of applications, neural networks are sometimes used for simple classification tasks,
such as predicting whether something is 0 or 1.
And as an input you may use a medium data set so thousands of rows of data.
If your data set is too small, you could run into overfitting where your model doesn't generalize well.
If your data set is too big, you could run into underfitting where your model doesn't capture all the
patterns in your data.
In general, like we talked about earlier, neural networks aren't a commonly used algorithm on their
own for machine learning, which is why it's listed as sometimes used here.
Deep learning, though, is much more commonly used.
It's used for much more complex tasks such as natural language processing, computer vision, automatic
speech recognition, and much more.
And for deep learning tasks, you would need to input huge data sets, meaning millions of rows of labeled
data.
And those large models are what's been able to produce some amazing results.
And we'll be diving into this in a lot more detail in the rest of this section.
```

### Deep Learning Architectures {#deep-learning-architectures}

```
At this point.
You understand that when we talk about deep learning, we're just talking about a neural network model
that has many hidden layers.
Now let's take that a step further.
Let's talk about deep learning architectures.
A deep learning architecture combines the basic concept of deep learning with additional calculations
and operations, and by doing so, we're able to make deep learning work for different types of data.
And those end up producing really great results.
So let's start with the most basic deep learning architecture, which is called a feedforward neural
network.
You've actually been exposed to this one already.
It's the one we've been talking about throughout this whole section.
And it looks something like this.
You can see here we have our inputs and they go through many different sets of hidden layers until we
get to our output.
And one thing you'll notice here is every node is connected to every other node.
And because of that you'll sometimes hear this called a fully connected neural network.
But going back to our general architecture again, this here is a feed forward neural network.
And the reason it's called that is because all the information flows from left to right.
We're just moving forward here.
And in addition, we don't have any additional calculations or operations that we're applying to our
input data.
So once again this idea is called an ffnn.
Now why am I stressing this idea so much?
It's because fans are often a piece of other more complex architectures.
We'll talk about that more in the next slide.
But this is also one of the three main components of a transformer.
So just keep this idea in the back of your mind as we go through the next lessons.
Okay, so the first deep learning architecture you've just learned is a feed forward neural network.
Now let's talk about a few more.
We'll be giving you a high level overview of some of the most popular deep learning architectures that
are currently used today.
You may have heard the term convolutional neural networks or CNNs.
They became popular in 2012 along with RNNs or recurrent neural networks.
They were popularized around the same time.
And then a few years later, that's when Transformers came into the picture.
Now, each of these here is a different architecture.
So each of these uses the general concept of deep learning.
But then it also combines that with other calculations and operations.
And each of these architectures work well for different types of data.
For CNNs this works well with image related data.
So if you're working on tasks like image classification or object detection, you'd want to work with
CNNs for RNNs.
These are used for sequential tasks.
You can think of NLP as a sequential task.
You have one word, you're trying to predict the next word, and so on.
And then other sequential tasks include time series analysis.
So anything that's time based, maybe you're looking at stock prices or the weather and so on.
And finally down here for Transformers these work really well on a variety of tasks.
The focus of this course is on natural language processing.
But again they also work really well for computer vision, automatic speech recognition and much more.
Now at this point, I want to give you a high level overview of what each of these architectures look
like.
We won't be diving into the details because that's beyond the scope of this course.
But once you understand the general structure, you can go on and learn more on your own.
So first let's start with a convolutional neural network.
Remember this is used for image data.
So you'll typically start with a raw image.
And then you pass it through what's called a convolutional layer.
And the idea here is you scan an image to extract image features.
So things like edges or textures in the image.
How is that done all using matrix math.
Because that's really the core of all these neural network and deep learning techniques.
But the main idea here is you're taking your raw image and you're extracting important things about
that image.
And then all that data gets passed to the pooling layer.
Because with the convolutions layer we've created a lot of extra data.
So the pooling layer aggregates that data and reduces the dimensions of the data.
And now at this point we've taken our raw image through all these transformations.
And we have an idea of the main features of that image.
And with that, we put that all into a feed forward neural network.
That was the basic architecture we just talked about on the last slide.
And what that fan does is it learns patterns from our data.
So it's taking in all that transformed image data.
And with that it makes a prediction.
Now for this deep learning architecture, all the learning is happening at that convolution layer and
the fan layer.
And then the other things around it such as that pooling layer, those are the additional calculations
that aren't necessarily related to deep learning.
However it's part of this overall deep learning architecture.
So how did someone come up with this?
They knew that fans did a great job learning patterns and making predictions.
And because this is for image tasks, convolutions and pooling were already concepts within the image
space that they combine with deep learning to create this overall deep learning architecture.
So this is what I mean when I say this combines deep learning along with additional calculations.
In this case, we're combining deep learning, which is the FNN piece, with additional calculations,
which are those two image specific earlier layers.
And that creates our overall architecture.
Next let's talk about RNNs.
Again this is for sequential tasks.
So the input could be either text or numeric data depending on what type of sequence you're processing.
So with this we would start with some sort of sequential data.
Let's say we're doing an NLP task where we're trying to predict the next word.
So this would be one word and we're trying to predict the next word.
So the input would just be that first word.
And then we take that data and pass it through hidden layers.
Now what's interesting about RNNs is that after you pass in some data and it goes through those hidden
layers and makes a prediction, it actually takes that output and loops it back inside the hidden layer.
So this feedback loop here, what it's doing is it's using predictions to feed into future predictions.
And that allows it to remember sequential data.
So it doesn't just look at the current state, but it remembers info from prior steps as well.
And it does that again and again until you get to a final prediction.
Again, this is called an RNN, and its main attribute is that it takes its predictions and puts it
back into the hidden layers.
Now, a spinoff of RNNs is something called long short term memory.
They're an extension of RNNs, and in addition to having logic to remember prior steps, it also includes
logic to forget prior steps.
This was revolutionary at the time and the best performing architecture for sequential tasks.
But that was short lived because very soon after Transformers came into the picture and very quickly
Transformers replaced RNNs and LSTMs for NLP tasks.
Here is the high level deep learning architecture for Transformers.
We'll be talking about this Transformers architecture in a lot more detail in the next section, but
for the time being, just know that what happens is we start with some raw text, and we take that through
an embeddings layer.
And what that embeddings layer does for us is it represents all of our words in space, and then we
pass that through the attention layer.
And what that does is it takes that location in space and it tweaks it based on the context of the text
around it.
And then finally we take that output and we put it into the FN step, and that FN learns those patterns.
And once it does all that, then we get a final prediction.
Now that was just a 32nd description of how transformers work.
Again, we'll be going through this in a lot more detail in the next section.
All right.
We've covered a lot.
So let's summarize everything in a table.
These are the four deep learning architectures that we covered in this section.
First we talked about feedforward neural networks.
And it's the most basic type of architecture.
You have your standard input and output layer along with a bunch of hidden layers.
And everything is fully connected.
So every node in one layer is connected to every node in the next layer.
And these ffnns typically aren't used on their own.
They're often building blocks for other deep learning architectures.
Next we talked about convolutional neural networks.
And these are mainly used for image tasks.
What happens is you input a raw image, you extract the image features using convolutions.
There is also a pooling layer to aggregate those features.
And finally you feed it into an ffnn to eventually make a prediction.
Now convolutional neural networks were introduced in 2012 and they work really well for image tasks.
So even today in the mid 2020s they are still being used.
Next we talked about RNNs and LSTMs and these were mainly used for sequential tasks.
The main feature here is you have your hidden layers like a normal deep learning model would, but there's
also this recurrence.
So at every step you use the output or the prediction as an input for the next prediction.
And currently this is still being used for sequential tasks like financial modeling or weather predictions.
But they're not so much used for NLP tasks anymore.
And the reason for that is because Transformers have come along and they perform so much better.
And we'll be talking about that in a lot more detail in the next section In terms of its applications.
We'll be talking about how it can be used for NLP, but in practice it's also used for many other applications.
And that's your introduction to deep learning architectures.
Remember, the main idea is that these architectures or these structures consist of the main deep learning
concepts with those hidden layers, but they also include additional calculations based on the type
of task you're trying to do.
So for CNNs, we're combining deep learning along with image calculations for sequential data.
Again, we have those hidden layers which are part of deep learning.
But we're incorporating this idea of a sequence.
And then for Transformers, the reason they work so well for NLP is because again, we're starting with
the basic idea of deep learning.
But we're also going to incorporate some other concepts, including word meaning, which is what embeddings
do for us, and context, which is what attention does for us.
It's pretty exciting stuff, and we'll get to that real soon.
But before then, let's talk about deep learning and practice.
```

### Deep Learning in Practice {#deep-learning-in-practice}

```
Now that you understand the basics of deep learning and deep learning architectures, you may be thinking,
how do we do this in practice?
Everything seems so complicated, but don't worry, it's actually not too bad to understand how to apply
deep learning models.
It really starts with this mindset shift from traditional NLP to modern NLP.
So let's talk about traditional NLP once again.
That's what we covered in the first half of this course.
And the main idea is once you've decided on a model, you train your own model.
So the steps are first you pick a model that's good for your problem.
For example, if our problem is we want to predict if a company will be profitable or not, we would
choose a classification model for this, let's say a logistic regression.
Okay.
So we've decided on our model.
And then the next step is to input your historical data.
So in the past looking at company descriptions were they profitable or not?
And then we take that data, feed it into our model to get final parameters.
And with that, our model has been trained.
And with that trained model we can make predictions.
Now again, this is the traditional NLP way of thinking.
But the downside here is if we were to use the same idea to fit a deep learning model, we would run
into two problems.
First, we would need a lot of data.
We would need millions of rows of labeled data.
And the reason for this is because when you have so many hidden layers, that means you have a lot of
parameters as well.
And with all those parameters, you need even more data for your model to generalize well, or in other
words, to avoid overfitting.
The second problem is that when you train a deep learning model, it requires significant computational
resources.
There are a lot of calculations that need to be made and a lot of iterations of those calculations.
So because of these downsides, that's why we need to move into a modern NLP way of thinking, which
is to use a pre-trained model.
And by pre-trained I mean that the parameters are locked in.
So if you think back to our neural network training lessons, we talked about starting with an initial
set of random parameters and then training our model to adjust those parameters until we have final
locked in parameters.
And that whole idea was the traditional NLP way of thinking, where we trained our own neural network
model with modern NLP.
When we're using a pre-trained model, someone else has already come up with those optimal parameters.
And then our job as a data scientist is just to use the model that they have already trained.
The first step is to pick a pre-trained model that's good for your problem.
So again, if our goal is to predict if a company will be profitable or not, we would pick a pre-trained
deep learning model that's good for classification.
And we'll talk about how to do this more in the hugging face section of this course.
From there you would just use that pre-trained model.
It already comes with all the parameters locked in, so you can dive right into making predictions using
those preset parameters.
Now, if your results are good enough, that's great and you can move on with your analysis.
But at this point you also have the option of improving your pre-trained model, which would then improve
your predictions.
This is done using something called transfer learning or fine tuning, which we'll talk about in the
next lesson.
Now you may be wondering who's creating these pre-trained models.
And the answer is research labs and large tech companies.
What typically happens is they'll train their own deep learning models from scratch that use a ton of
data and a ton of computational resources.
And then the majority of data scientists will use those pre-trained models that they've shared as a
starting point for their own analysis.
So let's talk about these pre-trained models just a little bit more before we dive into the details.
Again, most data scientists will be using pre-trained deep learning models, and these models have
already been trained on huge data sets.
So these research groups have chosen a deep learning architecture.
They've fed in millions and millions of rows of data.
They've gone through the process of starting with random parameters and updating them until they become
optimal parameters.
And once they're done with all that, all the parameters have been locked in.
So that includes things like weights and biases that we talked about in the neural network lessons,
as well as some architecture specific calculations.
And what that means for the rest of us is that all the hardest parts of deep learning have already been
completed, and all we need to do as a general data scientist or an analyst is use those models.
Now, another popular term you may have heard before is a large language model and a large language
model or an LLM is a subset of pre-trained deep learning models.
These are models that have been specifically trained on huge amounts of text data.
So that's where the term large language comes from.
A lot of text data.
Some popular llms are Bert, GPT and much more.
We'll be covering Llms and a lot more detail in the next section on Transformers and Llms.
Now, once again, this may all sound very complicated, but in practice it's not.
And that's the key takeaway I want you to get from this lesson as a data scientist to use an LLM, all
you have to do is pick a pre-trained model.
You input your data and then with just a few lines of code, all the calculations are made for you.
So all those weighted sums we talked about and the non-linear transformations, those are applied to
your input text to output a final prediction.
Now the final thing I want to mention here is again the mindset shift for the first four out of five
parts of this data science and Python series.
We've been talking about traditional machine learning, and you can see in that second paragraph there
we've been focused on model interpretability, and that should be the case when you're using traditional
machine learning models.
It's good to know what's happening behind the scenes and how your model works, to be able to interpret
the results correctly.
As a traditional data scientist, we typically avoid using black box techniques.
Basically, techniques where we don't know what's going on under the hood, because with those techniques,
we could potentially fall into the danger zone, which is applying a technique without understanding
the math and interpreting the results incorrectly.
However, with modern NLP, everything's a black box.
You can see in that third paragraph there currently pre-trained deep learning models work so well that
they're the gold standard, especially for NLP tasks.
So even though these pre-trained models are a black box these days, it's absolutely acceptable to use
them and I would highly encourage you to use them.
And now moving on to that last paragraph there.
The goal of this section here.
And the next one on Transformers is to help you break down these black box ideas.
You may be wondering, why did we go through so much struggle to understand how neural networks work?
If deep learning models are so easy to apply in practice, and personally, I really like understanding
how things work before I use them, because then when I see the outputs, first of all, I can appreciate
how amazing they are.
And then also I know how to better interact with the outputs.
So that's really the main goal of this section.
And the next is just to give you a better sense of what's happening with Llms before you apply them.
That's why this section and the next are very theory heavy.
But I promise we will get to the application when we talk about hugging face.
So just keep that in mind.
The hardest part of this course is over.
At this point.
It's all downhill in terms of complexity, but uphill in terms of how amazing the results are going
to be.
```

### Pretrained Deep Learning Models {#pretrained-deep-learning-models}

```
In this lesson, we'll be talking about the many ways that you can use pre-trained deep learning models.
First, let's start with the simplest situation, which is just to use a pre-trained model as is.
The way to do this is you download a pre-trained model that has all those parameters set, and then
you use those parameters to make a prediction.
So in this case, when you're just using a pre-trained model as is it's pretty straightforward.
All the parameters are fixed.
But even though it's simple it can be used for some pretty complex applications.
So things like sentiment analysis, text summarization and much more.
We'll be going through many examples of this using Python within the hugging face section of this course.
Another way to use a pre-trained deep learning model is by using its embeddings.
Now, we haven't talked about embeddings much yet.
There's a lesson on this coming up very soon, but at a high level you can think of an embedding as
a numeric representation of a word, and that numeric representation has meaning.
For example, in some part of space, maybe that's where all the fruit embeddings live.
And another part of space.
That's where all the animal embeddings live.
And so on.
Now, when you're working with llms or pre-trained models on large amounts of text data, one small
piece of that larger model will be the embeddings.
And again, those embeddings capture information about the words.
So to use pre-trained model embeddings what you would do is you would take your pre-trained model and
extract just the embedding piece.
And then you can input those embeddings into traditional machine learning models.
So in this case once again the parameters are fixed.
We're using a pre-trained model.
All the parameters are locked in.
We're not changing those at all.
But we're taking one piece of that pre-trained model which are called embeddings.
And we can use those numbers to make additional calculations.
You can use embeddings to calculate similarity, feed them into clustering or classification algorithms,
and much more.
Later on in this course we are going to be covering both these examples here.
Now, in addition to these two approaches, I also want to mention two other ways of using pre-trained
models that we won't be covering in this course, just in case you're interested in exploring them more
on your own after you take this course.
One of them is to use pre-trained models with transfer learning.
Now, the idea of transfer learning is that you train your model on one task, and then you use that
model for a different task.
So with this, what you would do is you start with a pre-trained model and then you apply that to your
data set.
But you don't stop there.
You also adjust the parameters by training that model on task or domain specific data.
Now the key difference here is that the parameters are no longer fixed.
And based on what type of transfer learning you're doing, you can either update the parameters in the
final layers of your model or in all the layers.
When we talk about updating only the final layers for your specific data set, that's typically referred
to as transfer learning.
And when you update all the layers, that's typically called fine tuning, But the idea is the same.
You start with a pre-trained model that's already been fit on a ton of data, and using those parameters
as a starting point, you adjust them for your specific dataset.
Now there are a lot of applications of this.
Common ones include text classification, industry specific analysis, and much more.
Now, one thing to note with this is because you are doing training at this point.
This requires two things.
Both a lot of labeled data as well as a lot of computational power in terms of data.
You want at least tens of thousands of labeled data points.
And then in terms of computation, you'll likely need more than a single computer.
And also you wouldn't just use CPUs on your machine but GPUs instead.
We'll be talking more about the difference between CPUs and GPUs later on in the hugging face section.
And also, you can use pre-trained models with rags and rags stands for Retrieval Augmented generation.
And the idea here is you combine the power of a pre-trained model with a database as well.
So instead of just generating a prediction from a pre-trained model, you also incorporate information
from a database and use that as one of your inputs to make your final prediction.
And the result of this is that your final prediction tends to be more up to date and also more context
aware, because the responses incorporate information from your database.
In these situations, you may or may not update your parameters, but as you may have guessed, there
are many useful applications for this.
This is often used for question answering, fact checking, and much more.
And one thing to note is that if you're using rags, you will again need a lot of data.
That external database that we talked about has to hold at least tens of thousands of documents for
you to incorporate into your model.
So both of these bottom applications here require a lot more data.
Again, these are outside of the scope for this course.
But now you have a high level idea of how they work.
And you can explore them more on your own and also be comfortable using these terms in conversation.
```

### SOLUTION: Deep Learning Concepts {#solution-deep-learning-concepts}

```
Let's start with the terms on the left here.
The first thing we're looking at is something that has 1 to 2 hidden layers, and that is a neural network.
And then a model that has three or more hidden layers is called deep learning.
So that's really the difference between neural networks and deep learning.
Next we're looking for a building block for other architectures.
And that's a feedforward neural network.
It's the most basic deep learning architecture but it's usually used inside of other architectures.
We're also looking for an architecture that's used for image related tasks.
And that is convolutional neural networks.
And then here we have an architecture that's used for NLP tasks.
But it's an outdated one.
And those are RNNs and LSTMs.
Those are for sequential data.
But they have been replaced by transformers.
And that's this next one here.
This architecture is used for NLP tasks.
And those are Transformers.
And for these final two down here we have a mindset where you train your own model versus a mindset
where you use a pre-trained model.
And that's the difference between traditional NLP, where you train your own model, versus modern NLP
where you use a pre-trained model, and then on the right here we have to order these from easiest to
hardest.
So training a model from scratch, that's pretty hard.
Fine tuning a pre-trained model that's also pretty hard.
Using a pre-trained model as is, that is the easiest one.
In this case, all the parameters are already preset, and we could just use that pre-trained model
to make a prediction.
And then the next most difficult one is this one using embeddings from a pre-trained model.
So in this case we still have fixed parameters.
But instead of just taking the model and making a prediction, we're going to extract a piece of that
model and then input it into other machine learning algorithms.
And that's not too difficult, but it takes just one extra step from using a pre-trained model as is.
And then out of these two, the next most difficult one is fine tuning a pre-trained model.
In this case, you start with a pre-trained model and all the parameters that come with it, and then
you fine tune that pre-trained model.
And by doing so, those parameters get slowly updated.
Again, we talked about transfer learning versus fine tuning.
With transfer learning, you usually freeze all the initial layers of your pre-trained model and then
just update the parameters for the last few layers.
And the other one we talked about is fine tuning where you update all the parameters.
And if you decide to do this, you have to have a lot of data, at least tens of thousands of rows.
And then finally, the hardest one is training a model from scratch.
And the reason this is so hard is because first you need a lot of data to be able to do this.
And then second, this takes a lot of computational power.
So training a model from scratch is typically only done by researchers and large tech companies.
And as a data scientist getting started with NLP, these two up here are what you'll typically do with
pre-trained models.
And we'll be going through many examples of that within the hugging Face Transformers section.
```
