### Transformers & LLMs Overview {#transformers-llms-overview}

```
As we talked about in the last section, Transformers are a type of deep learning architecture.
Again, what that means is that you're combining deep learning along with some other calculations,
and passing your data through a series of steps to extract meaningful patterns or make predictions.
In this section, we'll be talking about the three main layers of a transformer embeddings, attention,
and feedforward neural networks.
In addition to transformers, we have large language models, and large language models are models that
have been pre-trained on massive amounts of text data.
Now, the reason I'm bringing both of these up in the same lesson is because sometimes these two terms
are used interchangeably, but they're slightly different.
And I just want to clarify that here.
When we're talking about Transformers versus Llms, they are two distinct ideas, but they often both
refer to the same thing, which is transformer based.
Llms.
Currently, transformer based Llms are the most popular deep learning approach for NLP tasks, and that's
what we'll be covering in this section.
In addition, though, I want to clarify what's in these circles outside of that intersection.
If we look on the left, we have transformers.
And when we're talking about transformers and their relation to llms, we're talking about transformers
being used on text data.
That said, the Transformers architecture can also be used for other tasks.
Remember we talked about computer vision and automatic speech recognition, so that's something to keep
in mind.
But for the most part, transformers are mainly used for NLP applications or on text data.
On the other side, we're talking about large language models, and these are deep learning models that
are specifically trained on large amounts of text data.
Now, there are many deep learning architectures to choose from out there, and transformers are just
one of them.
So some other architectures we talked about are things like RNNs and LSTMs.
But one thing you'll remember about those from the deep learning section is that those are outdated
models.
And for the most part, llms these days are mainly based on transformers.
So once again, the key takeaway here is transformers refer to the architecture, whereas large language
models refer to the pre-trained models.
Large language models are specific to text data, whereas transformers are a general deep learning architecture.
But because this intersection here by far outweighs the other sections, these two terms are sometimes
used interchangeably.
For the rest of the section, we're going to be focused on transformer based llms, but I'll typically
just refer to them as Transformers or Llms.
```

### Transformer Architecture {#transformer-architecture}

```
Let's start with the transformer architecture.
When we say architecture, we're referring to the series of layers and computations that our input text
data passes through to get to a final result.
And along the way, that data gets gradually transformed step by step.
Which is why this is called a transformer architecture.
Now here's a visualization of the main layers of a transformer.
So we're going to start here with some raw text.
And then the first layer that text goes through is something called the embeddings layer.
Then it goes through an attention layer followed by a feedforward neural network.
And finally we get a prediction.
Now what's happening in each of these layers.
First for the embedding layer we're going to take our raw text and put them into vectors.
Now this idea may sound familiar because if you remember back all the way to the text pre-processing
section, we talked about a concept called vectorization.
And the idea is that you make your text data numeric That exact same thing is happening here, but these
embeddings are very smart vectors because each vector points to someplace in space, and that place
in space actually has meaning.
We'll be talking about this more in the next lesson, but for the time being, just know that this layer
will create a vector for each word that points somewhere in space.
And then from there, we pass that information to the attention layer.
And the way the attention layer works is that it makes adjustments to those vectors or those embeddings.
And the way it works is an embedding will be pointing somewhere in space.
And then in this layer, the position of that vector is slightly adjusted based on the context from
surrounding words.
So instead of each word being standalone in this layer, it's going to be influenced by the words around
it, which will make the definition of that word even more meaningful.
This piece here is the newest development in this whole Transformers architecture and the real game
changer.
We'll be spending quite a bit of time talking about how this works.
And then finally we pass that output into a feed forward neural network.
And this is something you're very familiar with already when we introduce neural networks and had that
lemonade stand example that was a feed forward neural network.
And if you remember from that lesson, what happened in the hidden nodes was that they learned new features
from our inputs.
So that's what's happening at this step as well with this fan at the very end, it's learning the patterns
from the prior layers and incorporating that into our model.
Remember, fans are machine learning technique and its whole goal is to learn.
So it basically summarizes what's been happening in the prior two layers.
It learns that locks that in.
And at the very end, once our raw text has gone through all these layers, we can make a final prediction.
Now let's dive into the details for each of these layers.
```

### Transformer Architecture | Embeddings {#transformer-architecture-embeddings}

```
We're going to start with embeddings, which is the first layer of a transformer.
And in the embeddings layer we're converting our raw text into meaningful numeric representations.
And what ends up happening is that each token or each word is put somewhere in space.
We're saying high dimensional space here because it's represented by many columns.
And then words that have similar meaning, they're going to be close to one another.
And words with different meanings are going to be farther from one another.
So what does this look like?
Let's say we have the sentence I love cold lemonade.
What would happen in the embedding layer is that that sentence gets represented as this matrix here.
You can see this data has been tokenized which is something we talked about in the text preprocessing
section.
Again the idea is we take a long string of text and we break it up into smaller pieces for simplicity's
sake.
We've tokenized our text into words here.
But in practice, these tokens can also represent Subwords.
And you can see even punctuation is included.
Okay.
So each row here is a token.
And then these columns here are our dimensions.
In other words they're the number of columns we want to use to represent each word for each model.
The number of dimensions vary, but 768 is a common length for popular llms these days.
Now you may be wondering how did they come up with 768?
It's something that researchers have found worked well.
It's large enough to capture the nuances in the text, but it's also small enough to be able to efficiently
do the numerous calculations in an LLM.
And then also for certain numbers that we'll be talking about throughout this section.
Sometimes it's chosen because the math works out well.
Okay.
Now back to this full table.
The way to read it is just like how we would read things in the vectorization section.
For each row of data, that's that vector that represents a specific bit of text.
So for the word I.
You can see there is a 768 dimension vector that represents where the word I is in space, and we have
that information for each of the tokens here.
Now the amazing thing about embeddings is that these vectors, they're not placed at a random location
in space.
Instead, the location has some semantic meaning.
What that means is where it is in space roughly tells us the definition of the word.
So to visualize that, imagine we're looking at only two dimensions.
Let's just say we're looking at these two here.
But in reality this would be hundreds of dimensions that we can't visualize as humans.
But the way you can think about it is let's say we're looking at the word cold, that word with its
raw text on its own.
We can't visualize it at all, but we can visualize its corresponding vector.
So if we were to put that vector in space over here, it might end up at this point.
And now what's interesting about embeddings is that this vector Representation or this embedding of
cold is in this spot.
And if we were to look at the vector representation or the embedding of the word hot, it would likely
be nearby.
And the reason for that is because hot and cold are both ways to describe temperature.
Now, somewhere else in space, we might see the words winter and summer.
And so in that top area of space, words over there seem to be about temperature.
And then in the bottom area, those seem to be about seasons.
And what's cool with word embeddings is that you can actually do math on these vectors or these embeddings
to find analogies.
So for example, in both cases, if we went this direction in space, that would tell us the type of
season that corresponds with the temperature.
So while the locations of cold and hot are represented by a vector and have some meaning, this idea
of what season is a certain temperature is represented by that dotted teal vector right there.
Pretty amazing right?
Now, I do want to mention at this point that this idea of embeddings was invented four years before
the Transformers architecture came out.
So this is an independent idea outside of Transformers, and just something that Transformers Architecture
decided to use.
Now, at this point, you're probably wondering how did you get these embeddings?
And the answer is using all the concepts that we've already covered throughout this course.
So let's start by just talking about embeddings on its own outside of the transformer space.
There were a few popular word embeddings that were created in the early 20 tens, called word two,
vec and glove.
And they were created using two different techniques.
First, for word two vec that was trained using a shallow neural network, a neural network with few
hidden layers.
And in the case with word two vec, it was trained using a neural network with just one hidden layer.
So all of the math that we went through in the neural network section, that was the math used to create
these word embeddings.
Now I want to pause there and say that is pretty mind blowing, right?
Within the neural network section, we talked about the calculations from one node to the next, and
in every step, all we did was a linear transformation followed by a nonlinear one and four embeddings.
That's all it took to generate these word vectors that actually have some meaning in space.
So I hope you agree with me that that's pretty amazing.
Another popular word embedding in the early 20 tens was something called glove.
And the way that this was created was using matrix factorization.
And if you remember from the topic modeling section, we talked about matrix factorization.
The idea is you start with some matrix and you decompose it into a few other matrices.
So how glove worked was that you started with something called a co-occurrence matrix.
So you would see how often word pairs or token pairs would be next to each other.
And then with that information that matrix was split into two other matrices.
And then one of those matrices became word embeddings.
So with all the knowledge that you've picked up throughout this course, you've been able to catch up
with the math for NLP tasks up to the early 20 tens.
Okay, so those two examples were word embeddings on their own outside of a transformer.
But in the case of a transformer based LLM, the way that embeddings work is exactly the same as a neural
network.
What happens is all these values or weights are randomly initialized, and slowly they're updated until
they reach their optimal values.
Remember, this is exactly what we talked about in the neural network section.
Except now the scope is a lot bigger.
Instead of just using a handful of nodes, we're talking about tens of thousands of nodes here.
And another thing we talked about in the neural network section is that all the parameters of a neural
network are represented as matrices in Python.
And this embeddings matrix that we just covered is exactly that.
Instead of everything being represented as nodes in that visual format, we can represent it in this
concise way as a matrix, and it holds all the parameter information about a neural network, which
in this case ends up being our embeddings.
So to summarize the answer to this question here, how are these embedding values generated?
The short answer is just one term neural networks.
Now one more thing I want to mention before we wrap up this embedding lecture is this idea of parameters.
When you hear about llms mentioned in the news, you often hear people talk about the millions and billions
of parameters in a model.
And you may have been wondering, how is it possible that a model has that many parameters?
And if you think about it, for this embedding step alone, if we had a vocabulary size of 50 K, which
is pretty standard, if you take all the words found from Wikipedia and you take that and pre-process
it and normalize it, you'll end up with a vocabulary of roughly 50 to 100 K.
So again, that's a standard vocabulary size.
And if we had that along with the 768 dimensions that we talked about in our matrix.
Then our embedding matrix alone would have 38 million parameters.
I'm just doing 50 k times 768 there.
And that's just the very first step of a transformer.
This is where those millions of parameters are coming from.
It's all those weights that are in these matrices inside a transformer based LM.
I hope you enjoyed this lesson.
This is the one lesson where everything comes together from this course.
We talked about neural networks.
We talked about matrix factorization representing weights as matrices.
Text preprocessing.
These are all things that we've introduced throughout this course.
So if you're able to understand this lesson fully congratulations.
That means you've truly grasped the concepts.
But if not, these are a lot of really hard concepts.
By learning them from different angles over time, they will make a lot more sense.
Okay, now that you understand embeddings, we're ready to move on to the next step, which is on attention.
```

### Transformer Architecture | Attention {#transformer-architecture-attention}

```
In this lesson, we'll be talking about attention.
And this is the most important component of the transformer architecture.
The introduction of attention completely changed the NLP space.
So let's get right to it.
What does attention do?
It's the second layer of a transformer.
And in this layer what happens is context is added to each token.
Or in other words, every token absorbs the meaning from its surrounding tokens.
So for example, let's take a look at the word lemonade in three different phrases.
In this first situation here, because we're just looking at one token, we know the exact location
of that embedding that's going to be located somewhere in space.
Now, in the second situation, lemonade is still the type of lemonade that you drink.
However, given the context here, we know that it's not just any lemonade, it's cold lemonade.
And also, somebody loves this lemonade.
And what the attention layer allows us to do is send those meanings from cold and love to lemonade to
adjust the location of the embedding.
So this version of lemonade in this sentence will be located at a slightly different place, because
it's not just any lemonade.
It's specifically cold lemonade that's loved.
And finally, in this situation over here, lemonade isn't something you drink at all.
Looking at the words around it, we know it's a music album.
So in space, the location of this lemonade should be very different from our original lemonade word
embedding.
So without the attention layer, the embedding for the word lemonade would have been at the same location
for all these sentences.
But with attention, we're able to capture the nuances in each of these contexts.
So now that you understand this at a high level, let's get a bit more technical.
So once again we're going to be looking at this phrase I love cold lemonade, and the token that we're
going to be focusing on here is the word lemonade.
Now, in this first situation, if we weren't using the attention layer, the meaning of lemonade would
only be captured by that word embedding, which is already pretty impressive on its own because it shows
us the meaning of the word lemonade in space.
However, within this sentence, we want to slightly adjust that meaning, because the lemonade that
we're talking about here isn't just standard lemonade, it's being affected by the other words in the
sentence.
So let's first take a look at the word cold.
The way you can think about it is with the attention layer.
The word cold adds context to lemonade or it enriches the definition of the word lemonade.
Now, in technical terms, when we're talking about attention, the correct way to describe this is
cold attends to lemonade.
But simply put, the way I like to think about it is this isn't just any lemonade?
It's specifically a cold one.
Now we can do the same thing with another word in the sentence.
Here we have the word love.
Again, with this attention layer, the word love adds context to lemonade.
Or in technical terms, love attends to lemonade.
So in simple terms, we could say this isn't just any lemonade.
It's specifically lemonade that's loved.
And what would happen in this case is lemonade is our current token, and it would absorb the meanings
of nearby words such as cold and love.
In this case, I isn't affecting lemonade that much, so it wouldn't absorb that as much.
Now, we've been talking about all these things here in a theoretical sense.
Now let's add some numbers to this.
So within the attention layer, everything that we've been talking about so far is captured in a few
matrices.
The ones we'll be talking about are the queries keys and attention score matrices.
In practice, there's also a matrix that actually transforms the embeddings, but the main ideas are
captured in these three here.
So that's going to be the focus for this lesson.
So let's first talk about the queries matrix.
It would look something like this.
And what it captures is questions about other tokens.
So for the love token here that query vector of 768 dimensions, that vector represents the question
who or what do I love?
Now let me pause here and just talk about embeddings once again.
Remember in the last lesson on embeddings, we talked about how we could represent a word meaning in
a vector of 768 numbers.
In this case, instead of representing a meaning of a word, we're representing a question.
So again this vector here represents the idea who or what do I love.
And in addition within this layer we have a keys matrix.
And this keys matrix holds the answers to those questions from the queries Matrix.
So if the question is who or what do I love in this case here.
Lemonade is saying I'm something that can be loved and this here would represent a query key match.
We have this question on the left that's answered by this answer on the right.
Now at this point, you might be wondering how did you come up with that specific question?
The who or what do I love?
And first of all, the answer is the model comes up with that for us.
But also this isn't the only question that's asked within the attention layer.
There are many queries and many keys.
So for example, other queries about love could be things like what is expressing the love in which
the key or the answer to that would be I?
Or what kind of love is being expressed?
And in this case, it's love for a beverage.
We'll talk more about these multiple queries and how there can be multiple attention components in the
transformer summary in just a few lessons.
But for the time being, just focus on these query and key vectors.
Now let's get back to this question of who or what do I love?
You can see on the right there we know as a human by reading the sentence that this is what this person
loves.
So lemonade, this is the key that's loved the most.
But in addition to lemonade, this person also loves cold things.
And while the focus is mainly on lemonade, love is attending to lemonade.
Love also attends a bit to cold.
So over here, these numbers would represent the idea of I am somewhat love to, and if we were to visualize
that, it would look something like this.
So the vector on the left, it could be represented by this dot here.
And that direction in space represents the idea who or what do I love.
And then you can think of the keys over here as vectors in the same space.
So lemonade would be here cold here.
And then the exclamation mark could be down here.
Now, the way you can think about this is that vectors that are closer to that query vector, or that
teal vector, means that it's a better match with that vector.
So when that top purple situation there, who or what do I love?
I'm love the most.
Those are really close to one another.
So that means that love attends to lemonade a lot.
In the second situation that I'm somewhat love situation, that's a little further.
So love will attend to cold a little bit.
And then down at the bottom there this could represent any other token.
And it's not a good match for the query.
Who or what do I love.
So it's really far away.
Now at this point we understand all these relationships.
So let's capture all this in another matrix.
What we're going to do is summarize all these relationships here and in attention scores matrix which
would look something like this.
And again what this matrix is doing is it's summarizing all the query to key relationships.
So we just talked about the word love.
And the question here is what does love attend to or what does it modify.
And these two numbers represent what we saw in the visualization on the last slide.
Love mostly attends to lemonade and is somewhat attends to cold as well.
Now if we look at the word cold, you can see it attends a lot to lemonade.
It really impacts the meaning of lemonade.
And then finally, if we look at one of these columns here for lemonade, you can see this is what it's
absorbing information about.
Mostly that it's cold lemonade and also somewhat that it's loved.
So you can think of the tokens and the rows of this matrix as ready to modify other words.
And then the tokens in the columns of this matrix are the ones that are absorbing those meanings.
And then the values inside tell you how much each token attends to or gives context to other tokens.
Now you may have a few observations about this attention scores matrix.
You'll see that all the values are between 0 and 1, and that all the rows also add up to one.
So your next question is probably how are these calculations being made?
And the answer is just like the last step, just like the embedding values in the last lesson, the
query and key values in this attention layer are randomly initialized.
And then as you train your LLM, they're slowly updated until they reach their final optimal values.
And in this attention layer, there were a few extra math calculations made.
First we talked about query key matches.
And the way those are calculated is by using a dot product which finds the angle between two vectors.
And it essentially calculates a similarity score.
And then after that, you saw in the attention scores matrix that everything was between 0 and 1, and
all the rows sum to one.
And the way that happened was by using a softmax normalization function.
That sounds very fancy, but it's essentially just a formula that you plug your inputs into that range
from negative infinity to positive infinity.
And as an output, everything ends up between 0 and 1.
And also the rows end up adding up to one.
Now this here is an example of what I meant when I originally talked about deep learning architectures,
how they're a combination of deep learning as well as additional math calculations.
Those query and key values are learned.
And that's the deep learning piece.
And the second part here, these are the additional calculations that help the math work for your input
data to be transformed to your output.
And after all this, amazingly, just like how the embeddings layer was able to capture word meaning,
this attention layer captures how much each token attends to or gives context to other tokens, and
as a result, the location of a token in space is slightly adjusted closer to its true meaning.
With this attention layer, I would say in theory, this lesson is the hardest one to conceptually understand.
It's a really big leap, even from neural networks.
So if you don't fully understand everything at this point, don't worry, that's absolutely normal.
This is another one of those concepts that you have to learn multiple times from different angles to
fully comprehend.
But I hope you end up with just one takeaway, which is that with this attention layer, we're able
to use surrounding words to give context to each of our tokens.
And that gives us a truer meaning for each token, which then in turn helps the overall LLM model make
better predictions.
And with that we've talked about two of the three layers.
So let's get to the last layer which is on feedforward neural networks.
```

### Transformer Architecture | Feedforward Neural Network {#transformer-architecture-feedforward-neural-networ}

```
The third layer of a transformer is a feedforward neural network.
Now, after we've spent so long talking about embeddings and attention, this piece should be the easy
one.
This is a concept that you're already familiar with, so we're just going to review it quickly here
in the context of Transformers.
Once again what an FN does is it learns patterns from your data.
If you remember back to when we first introduced neural networks, we talked about how it was a supervised
learning technique.
And it falls under the umbrella of machine learning, which is about computers learning from your data.
So that's what's happening with the FN, as we saw in the neural network section, within the hidden
layer of a neural network, each node was creating essentially a new feature.
So with our original inputs being temperature and weekend, it created two new features that were thirst
level and foot traffic.
And that's what's happening in this step as well.
It's going to learn and discover new patterns from the inputs, which at this point is what we're getting
from the attention layer.
So let's just recap all the layers so far and what's happening at each step.
We first started with the embedding layer, and that's where our words were placed in different locations
in space.
And each location had some meaning.
And then with the attention layer, those locations got adjusted based on information from surrounding
words.
And now at this step with the fan layer, we're going to lock in our learnings.
So what it's going to do is look at all the patterns that we found in the attention layer.
So all the ways that certain words attended to other words.
And then it's going to make note of that and learn it.
And then it's going to capture those learnings within the weights of this fan.
So this is typically when fan would look like within a transformer.
Remember these three layers are just one piece of the larger transformer.
And the input into this is what's coming out of the attention step.
And the output of this is what we're sending to our prediction.
And here are the typical sizes of each layer.
This will vary for each model, but I'll give you a rough idea of how many nodes there typically are.
You can see the input and output layers have the same amount of nodes.
And then the hidden layer actually has more nodes.
This is what we mean when we say we're adding complexity to the model.
So we're starting with that input layer.
And by blowing it up into these more nodes, each of those nodes is capturing different information
about that input.
And some examples of what it could be capturing is the idea of a non-addictive relationship.
The idea of love, and so on.
So maybe if in the attention layer a noun adjective relationship happened multiple times in this layer
here with the fn, that pattern is recognized and it gets remembered here in one of the nodes of this
hidden layer.
Now this idea here is pretty amazing, right?
But like I mentioned when we talked about neural networks, while you could interpret what's happening
at the nodes, it's typically not done first because of the complexity and then also because no one
truly knows what's happening at those nodes.
These ideas here that I mentioned about capturing the idea of a noun adjective relationship or the idea
of love.
These are just part of a widely accepted theory of what's going on within this hidden layer.
What's actually happening is more abstract and less interpretable to us as humans.
But at the end of the day, remember, the idea of a feedforward neural network is to take some input
information from the attention layer, learn what happened during that attention step, and then at
the very end, what you get is a transformed representation of your original tokens.
```

### Transformers Summary {#transformers-summary}

```
Let's summarize everything that we've learned in the last few lessons.
We talked about the three main layers of a transformer.
We started with raw text, and then we put it through the embeddings layer.
And that's where each token was represented by a vector.
And that vector's position in space had some meaning to it.
From there, we put that through the attention layer, and at this step, the location of those vectors
was adjusted based on information from the surrounding words.
And finally, all that was put through a feedforward neural network layer, where all the patterns were
learned from the previous step.
And with this layer, we added more nodes in our hidden layer to extract more information and add complexity
to our model.
And at the end of all that, our raw text is transformed to make a final prediction.
Now we spent about an equal amount of time on each of these layers here, but I want to emphasize that
the groundbreaking layer is the attention layer.
They are the reason that transformers work so well.
Remember, attention adds context to our embeddings, and by doing this, it enriches the meaning of
each word and brings it closer to its true meaning within different contexts.
Another thing that attention does that we haven't talked about is it allows for parallelization.
In the Deep Learning architecture section, we talked about RNNs and LSTMs and how they have that feedback
loop.
One of the downsides of that architecture was that it was slow to train, because each word would be
processed one at a time in a sequence.
However, with the way the attention layer is structured, you can process an entire sequence at once.
Remember, everything is stored in matrices, and matrix math is highly parallelizable, meaning you
can run different calculations at the same time.
And also these days we have GPUs which are graphics processing units and they're specifically used for
parallel processing.
While they were originally built for video games.
They're now used to do matrix math for Transformers and Llms.
And because of this parallelization, these models are able to be trained on huge text datasets, which
give us the amazing results we see today.
And finally, one really nice thing about attention is that it generalizes well.
This core architecture right here works well for a variety of NLP tasks out of the box.
And this is something that you'll see for yourself when we play around with Transformers in Python in
the next section.
So at the end of the day, the original Transformers paper was right.
Attention is all you need.
Now, the last thing I want to mention before we wrap up this discussion of the transformer architecture,
is that what we've been talking about?
This five step process you see on the top here is not truly what happens in practice.
It's a lot more complex than this.
But now that you understand all the general ideas, Dears, you'll understand the final visualization
here.
In reality, the layers actually follow this order.
We start with some raw text.
We put it through the embeddings layer.
You can see nothing's changed so far.
But then what follows is this transformer block.
And you can see what's happening here is we have a bunch of attention layers that are working in parallel
in this situation here.
There are eight attention layers in practice.
Sometimes you'll see 12 or more.
But this concept here is called multi-headed attention.
And this is what I was talking about earlier in the attention lesson.
Remember the query we asked in that case about the word love was what do I love?
But that question could just be captured in one of these attention layers.
Here.
All the variations of that, such as what is expressing the love or what kind of love is being expressed,
could be captured in these other attention layers.
Now I say could here because again, what's actually happening is more abstract.
And also we're not yet done with this transformer block.
What happens after this transformer block is yet another transformer block, and within large language
models there can be 12 plus or even 24 plus transformer blocks.
So what happens is you start with raw text that's made into embeddings.
And those embeddings are fed into all these transformer blocks.
We're looking at multiple queries and keys feeding that into a feedforward neural network.
And then we do that again and again until we end up with our final prediction.
So with this visualization here, I hope you have a better appreciation for just how complex everything
is.
We've come a long way from a basic neural network to this final transformer here.
That said, I also want you to appreciate the simple building blocks.
Remember, everything here is based on neural networks.
And within each node of a neural network, we're just doing that linear transformation followed by a
nonlinear transformation.
And within this transformer architecture.
We are doing that again and again millions of times.
And yes, there are other mathematical calculations alongside all this.
But as for the learning piece, these are the main pieces that are learning in a transformer.
So I find that pretty phenomenal as well, because what it tells us is that even though this architecture
is really complex, the smallest pieces of that use the same math of a simple neural network that was
invented half a century ago.
So sometimes I just sit back and think, how amazing is it that I'm talking with ChatGPT right now?
And it's able to do so by mainly using linear and non-linear transformations.
Pretty mind blowing.
Something to think about in your free time.
But at this point in this course, congratulations!
You've gone through the hardest pieces within the next few lessons.
We're just going to wrap everything up by transitioning from this Transformers architecture to popular
large language models.
```

### Breaking Down the Transformer Diagram {#breaking-down-the-transformer-diagram}

```
Now that you understand the main components of a transformer, we're going to be breaking down this
image here.
This is the transformer diagram from the original attention paper.
And if you've watched any videos or read any blog posts about large language models or Transformers,
this is the visualization they'll typically include.
Now, when I first saw this visualization, it was really hard to wrap my head around.
But now that we've gone through the three main layers of a transformer, it shouldn't be too bad.
Remember we talked about the embedding layer, attention layer, and feedforward layer.
And you can see all those boxes on the left here.
Take a look at the bottom and you'll see those two embedding boxes in pink in the middle.
You'll see those orange boxes with the Multi-head attention label.
And you know what that means now.
It's just many attention layers being processed in parallel.
And then the blue boxes are feedforward layers.
And those are the feedforward neural networks that we've been talking about.
So once again, these are the three main layers of a transformer that do the learning to capture patterns
in your text data.
Everything else around it are just math calculations.
They support the learning process, but they're not actually learning patterns themselves.
So I'm going to quickly go over these so you can get an idea of what's happening.
But the main takeaway I want you to get here is to not be intimidated by this visualization because
you already know the most important pieces.
So if we start at the bottom, you can see we have our inputs and those are our text inputs.
We'll be talking about those a little bit more in the next few slides.
But for the time being just think of those as our raw text inputs.
And then we're doing some addition here.
We're adding those inputs with the positional encoding.
So that's telling us the position of all the tokens in our text.
And then this symbol here is just the sine or cosine function.
And that's a transformation we're applying on our positional encodings.
And then all that gets fed into these grey transformer blocks.
And in those boxes you can see at every step there's this yellow add and norm calculation.
And that's just doing some addition and scaling our data.
And over here you see this n x that's saying repeat this transformer block here a number of times.
And that's what we just went over in our last lesson.
Remember our data doesn't just go through the embedding attention and feed forward neural network one
time.
Instead the attention and fan steps are repeated many times.
And finally, all that is put through a linear transformation which is just matrix multiplication.
Or taking a softmax here, which is a nonlinear transformation that helps normalize our data.
And at the very end we end up with an output.
So with this, I hope you feel like you have a better understanding of what this visual means at a high
level.
And now that we can roughly understand this visualization, we can move on to encoders and decoders.
```

### Encoders & Decoders {#encoders-decoders}

```
At this point, you understand the general idea of a transformer, but there are actually different
categories of transformers and we'll be breaking them down in this lesson.
The three main categories are encoder only, decoder only, and encoder decoder models.
And the best way to understand them is by looking at different parts of the transformer architecture.
Now I want to start this lesson by saying that everything we're going to be going through is very theoretical,
but it will pay off in the next section on Llms.
So keep that in mind.
Just understand things at a high level and I'll make more sense once you see the practical applications
throughout the rest of this course.
So once again, this is the transformer architecture from the original paper that we went through in
the last lesson.
And if we were just to look at the left side here, this is the encoding side of the architecture.
So what's happening here is we're inputting raw text.
And you can see it goes through the embedding step.
And it goes through several transformer blocks until we get to an output.
And what this process essentially does is it takes raw text and it encodes it as an embedding representation
of that text, just like we talked about within the embedding section and attention section, you can
represent tokens as a vector like we did in the embedding section.
And then in the attention section we talked about how you can represent queries as a vector.
And in this case here we're starting with raw text.
And at the very end we're representing that entire input text as some vector or as an embedding.
And the way to think about this is with an encoder it's understanding our text.
So we start with raw text.
And then it understands it by transforming it into a vector or into an embedding.
And that embedding captures the meaning of a raw text.
Now, one popular application of encoder only models is sentiment analysis.
So if we input into the model.
I love cold lemonade.
The encoder only model would represent I love cold lemonade as a vector or as an embedding.
And then using that embedding we would get a prediction which in this case is positive.
So how this works is that while encoders create embeddings for the outputs to be more interpretable,
they're often fine tuned for specific tasks like sentiment analysis.
So what happens is, at the very end of this architecture, a classification step is added.
And that's what takes it from that vector form to a binary output such as positive or negative.
That said, though, the general idea here is you're starting with raw text and it gets encoded into
a vector form, and that vector form has some meaning.
Okay.
So that's an encoder only model.
Now let's look at a decoder only model.
In this situation we would only be looking at the right side of this architecture.
And the idea here is that for decoder only models we start with a text sequence.
And it infers the next word we have an asterisk next to infers here because so far in the machine learning
space we've been using the term predict.
But with transformers and llms you typically use the word infers instead.
But the idea is the same, or trying to predict the next word.
So if you look at this diagram over here, it says those are outputs.
But if we're using a decoder only model these would be your inputs.
You can see we get those input embeddings.
They go through several of these transformer blocks.
And at the very end we get output probabilities.
So it gives us a probability distribution of what it thinks the next word might be.
So to say that succinctly it generates text by looking at the current words it gives you suggestions
for the next word.
So an example of this is text generation.
So if you inputted I love cold lemonade it would output likely next words such as it's the perfect drink.
And now finally let's take a look at encoder decoder models.
These models use this entire architecture, both the left and right sides.
And with these situations, your encoder decoder model would take two inputs.
First, an input text sequence as well as a shifted target sequence.
And the reason the target sequence is shifted is because we don't want to predict the current word,
but rather the next word.
And with those two inputs, both of those get encoded as embeddings, and then they're combined later
on in the architecture to finally infer the next word.
So in short, it does both of the things that we've already talked about.
If first understands the text and then it also generates text.
So an example of this would be translation.
For example, if your input was I love cold lemonade and you wanted to translate that into Spanish,
you could use an encoder decoder model to get this translation here.
Now you may be thinking, you just said that there were two inputs to this, but you're just inputting
one line here.
And the reason for that is because when the model is trained, it's trained on two inputs, both this
English phrase as well as a shifted Spanish phrase to predict the next word.
But as a user, you would just input in a single prompt and then it would generate this output for you.
Now at this point you may be thinking, okay, this is a lot of theory, but how do we use these in
practice?
And the answer to that is to download a pre-trained LLM, which is what we'll be talking about in the
next lesson.
```

### Large Language Models (LLMs) {#large-language-models-llms}

```
For the final lesson in this section, we're going to be talking about large language models or llms.
And specifically we're going to be talking about transformer based ones.
These are ones that use the transformer architecture that we've been talking about in this entire section.
And they're large language models because they've been pre-trained on large amounts of text data.
Now, there are so many llms that are out there, but I like to think about them in three categories.
And these are the three categories that we just covered in the last lesson encoder only, decoder only,
and encoder decoder llms.
As a reminder, with encoder only llms, what we do is we turn raw text into vectors or embeddings.
For decoder only Llms.
We infer the next token in our text, so we predict what the next word should be.
And then finally, for encoder decoder llms, that's when we turn one set of text into another set of
text.
Now for each category there are many llms, but here are some of the most popular ones for encoder only.
Burr is the most popular one, and it stands for bidirectional encoder representations from Transformers.
And at this point you can somewhat understand that acronym if we skip the bidirectional piece.
This is an encoder only model.
And you can see we're using the transformers architecture with the bidirectional piece is referencing
is that it reads all the words in a sentence at once.
So it's looking both to the left and the right to understand the context of each word.
That's different than a decoder only model where you would be reading from left to right.
But the main takeaway here is when you see this Bert model, just know that it's understanding your
text.
It's turning your text into embeddings.
As for decoder only llms, the most popular one is GPT, and at this point you understand all these
words.
Here generative means it's generating text.
Pre-trained means all the parameters are set.
And it's again using the transformer architecture.
And finally for encoder decoder llms LMS.
Here are two popular ones.
T5 is one of them.
Text to text transfer transformer.
You can see it's called T5 because the letter T occurs five times.
But this should make more sense to you now too, because with encoder decoder models or turning one
set of text into another set of text.
And also there's something called Bart and that combines Bert and GPT.
Now, in the news, you've probably seen many more LLM acronyms out there, but don't fret.
Many of them are just variations of these popular models.
For example, Bert is a popular LLM and it has many variants.
And you can see here they all have Bert in the name.
That means these were inspired by Bert architecture.
But maybe some of the math has been changed or the bottom ones there you can see they have been fine
tuned.
Remember what that means is we start with our initial Bert parameters, and then we train that Bert
model on a specialized dataset.
And those parameters get slightly adjusted to be better for our domain specific data set.
Sometimes you'll hear a model like Bert called a foundational model because it serves as a foundation
or a base for all these other variants.
So I hope that after this introduction here, when you encounter new llms, you have an idea of how
to mentally organize them as new models come out.
Some of them will be encoder only, and they'll be able to take your raw text and turn them into embeddings
that have some meaning.
Some of them will be decoder only and that will help you generate text.
And then the encoder decoder ones, they turn one set of text into another.
Now how do you use these popular models?
As a reminder, these are large language models, which means they're pre-trained.
And by pre-trained that means all the parameters are set.
So all those embedding values, the attention weights, the weights.
Those are all preset so that you can just download the LLM input in your new text and quickly calculate
an output.
Now, just in case you're curious, we've also included some information of how Llms are trained.
The way it works is that researchers will typically first design an architecture.
They'll confirm how to structure their inputs and outputs, and then finally select the datasets that
they want to use for training.
Like we mentioned earlier, Llms are trained on huge datasets.
This training that we're talking about is typically done on billions of words, and not just from one
data source, but multiple data sources.
And because this is such a huge effort, this process is mainly done by large tech companies.
And it's not just working with a single computer.
It's doing this with many computers, with many GPUs.
So there are a ton of calculations happening in parallel here.
And for the different categories of llms, the training looks slightly different.
You now have a decent grasp of the foundations to get a general understanding of how these work.
For encoder only models, this is often trained using something called masked language modeling.
And the idea here is that your inputs are raw text and you're trying to predict a missing word.
So this here during the training process would be done on billions of input output pairs for decoder
only models.
A popular technique is autoregressive language modeling.
And the idea here is you start with your input and your output is the next word.
Autoregressive means your data is in a sequence, and you're using past information to predict future
information.
For encoder decoder LMS, this is often trained by just converting everything to text.
So your input would say English to Spanish.
I love lemonade and then it would give you that translation.
So within the input we're including the action we want to happen.
So that's how llms are trained at a high level.
If this is interesting to you at this point, you know enough of the foundations to be able to understand
how these processes work on your own.
And finally, let's talk about popular pre-trained llms.
These are some of the most popular ones that are out there today.
You can see in the first two rows we're starting with Bert and Distilbert.
Again, these are both encoder only models that understand text.
They were created by these large companies Google and hugging face.
And they have millions of parameters.
And you can see they were also trained on multiple datasets that have millions and billions of words.
The next two llms are encoder decoder llms.
And again they were created by these large tech companies Google and Meta.
They have millions of parameters and were trained on massive datasets.
And finally we have GPT, which is a decoder only model that was created by OpenAI.
And what should stand out here is the number of parameters.
These other ones at the top are in the millions.
But down here GPT three has 175 billion parameters.
This is by far the most parameters out of all these other models here.
And you can see again, it was trained on a lot of text.
Now one interesting thing about these llms is that most of them are open source.
That means that they have been trained by these large companies, and they've given the general public
access to them so that other researchers can take those models and build on them.
The one exception here is GPT.
GPT three and later versions are proprietary for GPT four.
It's so secretive that OpenAI hasn't even shared the total number of parameters.
This is a really interesting tidbit, and I'm curious what's going to happen with Llms as they continue
to be released in the future.
So just something to keep an eye on.
And one last thing I want to mention about Llms is that while there's just a few of these acronyms that
you'll see again and again, each one of these Llms can have multiple applications.
And that's what makes them so versatile and useful specifically.
Bert is used for sentiment analysis, classification, and much more.
Remember it's an encoder only model.
So any situation where we're trying to understand our text by turning our raw text into a numeric form
can be done.
So using Bert.
And then you have T5 which is an encoder decoder model which is used to turn one set of text into another
set of text, and that can be used for summarization, translation, and more.
And then you have GPT, which is a decoder only model, and that's mainly used for generation.
But any of these here can be used for things like question answering.
They're all using that general transformer architecture that consists of embeddings, attention and
feed forward neural networks.
The main differences between these llms is one how many of each component they decide to include in
their architecture, as well as how it's arranged.
And then two how they structure their input and output text.
So remember, in certain cases you are predicting a word in the middle and other cases you are predicting
the next word and so on.
And finally what the text is trained on.
So you can see here we have a lot of general text.
But for more domain specific llms, it's often fine tuned on industry specific text.
And with that, let's move on to an exercise that covers everything we've learned in this Transformers
and LLM section.
```

### SOLUTION: Transformers & LLMs Concepts {#solution-transformers-llms-concepts}

```
The first part of this exercise is to match each layer with its definition.
At this point, you're very familiar with the order of these embeddings already.
They're also on the left here.
We start with the embeddings.
Then there's attention.
And finally a feed forward neural network.
And then in terms of what they do remember embeddings.
They provide a numeric representation of our text.
And that vector location has meaning.
And then for attention that adjusts the location of vectors or in other words, it adjusts the meanings
of our words based on surrounding words.
So the surrounding words provide context to each word.
And then finally with the feed forward neural network, that's the machine learning part.
This part learns patterns from the previous layers and adds complexity to our model by capturing all
those grammatical relationships.
Now down here in the next section, we're talking about encoder only, decoder only, and encoder decoder
models.
And the main thing to understand about these categories is what they do.
So for encoder only models they understand text or decoder only models they generate text.
And then for encoder decoder models they do both in terms of its applications with encoder only models,
it takes raw text and turns it into an embedding.
In other words, it takes a string of words and makes it a numeric vector.
And that vector has some meaning.
So, with the meaning of some text in a numeric form, you can use that for common machine learning
applications such as sentiment analysis or text classification.
And then for decoder only models, that's all about generating text.
So the obvious answer here is text generation.
And then finally encoder decoder models are sometimes called text to text models, and they take one
body of text, understand it, and generate another body of text.
So that is used for translation.
And now in terms of popular LMS, for each of these, BR is the most popular encoder model.
GPT is the most popular decoder model.
And then finally T5 is a popular encoder decoder model.
Okay, let's move on to the next slide where we have these open ended questions, starting with what
are Transformers?
Remember, an early lesson in this course was distinguishing Transformers versus LMS.
And for Transformers, the main thing to note about them is that they're a popular deep learning architecture.
In other words, they combine deep learning along with other mathematical calculations, into a series
of steps so that you can input data into this architecture.
And then at the end you get a prediction.
The next question is what are llms?
We know they stand for large language models.
And the key difference here is that transformers are an architecture.
They define the structure whereas llms are pre-trained models.
The large there means it's trained on a lot of data.
The language there means it's trained on specifically text data.
And the model piece there just tells us that this is a deep learning model.
Now the next question here is where do they overlap?
And as we mentioned earlier on in this section, while Transformers and llms are two different things,
the terms are often used interchangeably.
Because most transformers are used for NLP tasks.
And then most llms use transformers.
So the technical term here when we're referring to these concepts should be transformer based LLM.
But in practice people just use one term or the other.
Okay, the next question is what is the most important part of the transformer architecture?
Like we saw in the original paper, attention is all you need.
Attention allows us to add context to our tokens or our words.
It also allows the training process to be paralyzable and in general, with this attention piece, we're
able to apply transformers to a variety of NLP tasks.
The next question is what are the main companies that are creating these llms?
And the answer to this is large tech companies and research groups.
Specifically, these are the ones that we mentioned within this section.
Remember, these groups are spending weeks or months training these large language models, and they're
looking at billions of words and working with many GPUs to do so.
And finally, what does it mean to use a pre-trained LLM.
Now we're being a bit redundant here because large language models are already pre-trained, and we're
also stating that pre-trained term in this case.
But the goal here is to have you thinking about what that pre-training means.
And the answer to this question is after the large companies create a large language model, and that's
a pre-trained model, they've locked in all the parameters.
And so all you have to do as a data scientist is download the model along with its parameters, input
your text data and then make all those matrix calculations.
And then finally you get an output.
So as a general data scientist you don't need to be training these models on your own.
Instead you just use the pre-existing models to make your calculations and predictions.
And in addition to using a pre-trained model as is, you also have the option of further fine tuning
it for your specific data or task.
We'll be spending the entire next section working with pre-trained models within Python.
```

### Key Takeaways {#key-takeaways}

```
Let's review the key takeaways for this section.
Our focus was on Transformers, which consists of three main layers.
The embeddings layer captures word meanings in vector form, and these were invented a few years before
the transformer architecture so popular early models were word two, vec and glove, and they were created
using neural networks and matrix factorization, respectively.
And then the attention layer modifies those original embeddings, and it does this by incorporating
context into each word from surrounding words.
This was the new idea that came with Transformers.
In this section, we talked about the queries and key matrices that held the questions and answers in
vector form, and then those were summarized into an attention scores matrix, and that was then used
to modify the original word embeddings.
After that we had the feedforward neural network layer and that learned patterns from the previous layers.
Now fans are a much older concept that predates embeddings and attention.
And earlier we talked about how it's the most basic type of deep learning architecture.
But once again, because it's a machine learning technique, it captures or learns the patterns from
the previous layers, and together these three form the general architecture of a transformer.
But as we talked about, the actual architecture consists of many attention and ffnn layers one after
another.
In transformer block, after transformer block.
And in addition, there were many supporting calculations made to transform our raw starting text into
a final embedding that captured the meaning of the text.
That said, these three layers are the main ones that learn from our input data.
But the reason this is all called transformer is because we start with that raw text, and slowly it
gets transformed into a meaningful numeric representation.
We also emphasize that attention is the most important piece of the transformer architecture.
In the attention step, the attention score matrix tells us how much each word attends to other words,
or how much each token affects its surrounding words.
What's great about attention is not only how it modifies the words around it, which provides context,
but the matrix calculations can also be done in parallel.
So because of that, it makes it much more efficient to train on large data sets.
And the best part of all is that attention makes transformers work really well for a variety of tasks,
especially NLP tasks.
When we talk about Llms, we're not talking about the general architecture of a model, but rather a
pre-trained model that's been trained on millions or billions of text tokens.
In this section, we talked about three categories of Llms and their applications.
Encoder only models like Bert understand text by turning it from its raw text form into a meaningful
embedding, and that embedding can then be used for downstream tasks like classification, clustering,
or whatever other analysis you want to do.
And then decoder only models like ChatGPT generate text.
And finally encoder decoder models like T5 or Bart do both, which makes it really great for translation
and summarization.
And now with that, we are now done with the transformer theory and can move on to the exciting part,
applying what we've learned on actual data.
So get ready to code once again in the next section on hugging face Transformers.
```
